A huge flash of light lit up space. Every single alien on Earth suddenly stopped. Their scooters fell out of the sky. The giant whales stopped swimming. It was like someone had turned off all of them at once.

"Close it!" said Steve.

Natasha pushed the scepter hard against the machine. The blue beam flickered. The hole in the sky started to shrink.

But Tony hadn't come back yet.

"Come on, Tony," whispered Sydney, holding Tim's hand tight. "Come on."

The hole got smaller. And smaller.

And just before it closed, a tiny red and gold shape came falling out of it.

It was Tony. His suit was dark. His glowing eyes were off. He was falling. Falling fast. He wasn't flying anymore.

"He's not slowing down," said Thor, starting to spin his hammer.

But before Thor could even take off, a giant green shape leaped up off the side of a building. The Hulk jumped higher than he had ever jumped before. He caught Tony in his huge green arms. Then he slid all the way down the side of a building and landed on the street with a THUD.

He gently laid Tony on the ground.

Everyone ran over. Steve. Thor. The family. They all crowded around.

Tony wasn't moving.

Paisley grabbed Lindsey's hand. Lainey grabbed Tim's hand. Sydney's eyes filled up with tears.

Then the Hulk leaned down really close to Tony's face. And he ROARED.

"RAAAAAAAAAAAAR!"

Tony's eyes popped wide open. He gasped. He blinked.

"What just happened?" he said. "Please tell me nobody kissed me."

Everyone burst out laughing and cheering. Sydney threw her arms around him. "You're okay!"

"I'm okay," said Tony, hugging her back. "Did we win?"

"We won," said Steve.

Tony lay back on the street and looked up at the blue sky. The hole was gone. "Great. Hooray. Okay. Has anybody ever tried shawarma? There's a shawarma place two blocks from here. I've never had it. I want to try it."

"What's shawarma?" asked Lainey.

"I don't know," said Tony. "That's why I want to try it."

"We're not done yet," said Thor.

They all marched to the top of Stark Tower. And there, lying in a Loki-shaped dent in the floor, was Loki. The Hulk had found him first. He had picked Loki up by his ankle and bonked him on the floor, back and forth, like a floppy rag doll.

"Puny god," the Hulk had grumbled.

Loki groaned. He looked up at all the heroes standing around him. Then he looked at the family.

"I think," said Loki weakly, "I'll have a drink now."

"I think you won't," said Lindsey, putting her hands on her hips.

Thor took the Tesseract out of the machine. It glowed softly in his hands, blue and bright.

"What is it, really?" asked Sydney. "What's inside it?"

"Inside the cube is something very old and very powerful," said Thor. "It is called the Space Stone. It can make doorways to anywhere in the whole universe. It is one of six special stones. They are called the Infinity Stones. Each one has a different power."

"Six?" said Lindsey. She unfolded her map and turned it over to the empty back side. "Then we'd better keep track."

At the top, she wrote:

THE INFINITY STONES

And underneath it:

1. Space Stone (blue). Inside the Tesseract. Thor is taking it to Asgard.

Sydney looked over at the scepter, which Natasha was holding very carefully. The jewel at the top was still glowing.

"Is that one too?" asked Sydney.

Thor looked at the scepter for a long time. "I do not know," he said. "But it might be."

Lindsey wrote a little question mark at the bottom of her list.

Later that afternoon, Thor took Loki and the Tesseract home to Asgard across the rainbow bridge. Before he left, he bowed to each of the girls.

"Princess Paisley. Little Rocket Lainey. Hawk-Eyed Sydney," he said. "You were very brave today."

Then he shook Tim's hand. Very gently this time. Tim did not fall into anything.

And then everyone went to get shawarma.

The shawarma place was small, and it had gotten a little bit smashed during the battle. Some of the chairs were tipped over. One wall had a big crack in it. But the owner was so happy to see the Avengers that he cooked for all of them anyway.

They sat around a long table. Tony. Steve. Bruce. Natasha. Clint. Tim and Lindsey. Paisley, Lainey, and Sydney.

Nobody said very much. Everybody was very, very tired. They just ate their shawarma quietly, and it was warm, and it was good.

Paisley leaned her head on Bruce's arm. Lainey fell asleep with a piece of bread still in her hand. Sydney drew a picture in her notebook of everyone sitting at the table together.

Steve looked down the table at all of them. Then he looked at Tim.

"Nobody got left behind," said Steve. "Your family made sure of that. You were captains today too."

Tim looked at Lindsey. She was smiling at him with tired, happy eyes. Then he looked at his three girls, all sleepy and dusty and proud.

"We had a good team," said Tim.

Tony took one last bite of his shawarma. "Huh," he said. "This is actually really good."

And for the first time ever, the Avengers all agreed on something.

## Chapter 6: Sunday Spaghetti and the Winter Soldier

On Sunday morning, the whole house smelled like garlic.

Lindsey was stirring a big pot of tomato sauce. Tim was rolling meatballs with his hands. Paisley was putting sparkly napkins on the table, one for every chair. Lainey was running laps around the kitchen island, because she was too excited to stand still. And Sydney was drawing a picture of a robot that could roll meatballs, so Daddy would not have to.

"He's coming at five o'clock," said Paisley for the tenth time. "Steve is coming at five o'clock."

"We know, sweetheart," said Lindsey, smiling.

"It's four o'clock," said Sydney, looking at the clock. "That's sixty more minutes."

"SIXTY?" groaned Lainey, flopping onto the floor. "That's forever!"

"Then you can help me in the garden," said Lindsey. "Come on. I want to check on something."

They all went out the back door. Lindsey's garden looked very different than it did the night Thor crashed into it. The big crater was gone. The dirt was smooth and dark. And right in the middle, where Thor had planted his seeds from Asgard, there was a plant.

It was not a normal tomato plant.

Its leaves were a deep, shiny green. Its stem was as thick as Lainey's arm. It was already taller than Tim. And hanging from its branches were tiny round tomatoes, as small as marbles, and every single one of them was gold.

"They're still growing," said Lindsey. She touched one gently. "Not ready yet."

"When will they be ready?" asked Paisley.

"Thor said Asgard tomatoes take their own sweet time," said Lindsey. "So we'll have to be patient."

Lainey groaned again. "Everything takes FOREVER today."

At exactly five o'clock, the doorbell rang. DING-DONG.

All three girls raced to the door. Lainey got there first, of course. She flung it open.

There stood Steve Rogers. He wasn't wearing his blue suit or carrying his shield. He was wearing a plain shirt and a brown jacket. He was holding a bunch of yellow flowers in one hand and a big loaf of bread in the other.

And sticking out of his jacket pocket was one small, brown, floppy ear.

"BISCUIT!" shouted Paisley.

Steve laughed. "He wanted to come to dinner. I hope that's okay."

"Of course it's okay," said Paisley. "He lives here, you know. Well, he used to."

"These are for you," said Steve, handing Lindsey the flowers. "Thank you for having me."

"Come in, come in," said Lindsey. "And you didn't come alone, did you?"

Steve turned around. Standing behind him on the steps was another man. He had a big friendly smile and short dark hair, and he was wearing a sweatshirt and running shoes.

"This is my friend Sam," said Steve. "Sam Wilson. We met this morning in the park. We were both out running."

"Met?" said Sam, grinning. "He passed me. Then he passed me again. Then he passed me AGAIN. Every time, he said, 'On your left.' Thirteen times!"

"It was twelve," said Steve.

"It was thirteen," said Sam. "I counted."

Lainey's eyes went wide. "You passed him THIRTEEN times? How fast do you run?"

"Pretty fast," said Steve.

Lainey squinted at him. "After dinner, we're racing."

"Deal," said Steve.

Sam leaned down to the girls and whispered, "Good luck. He doesn't get tired. It's not fair."

Dinner was the noisiest, happiest dinner the house had ever seen.

Steve said grace. Tim served the spaghetti. Lindsey passed the bread. Sam told funny stories about being a pilot in the Air Force, and he flapped his arms like a bird to show them how he used to fly with a special set of metal wings.

"Wings?" said Sydney. "Real wings? That you wear?"

"Real wings," said Sam. "They fold up into a backpack. When you open them, WHOOSH, you can fly."

Sydney grabbed her notebook and started drawing right next to her plate. Lindsey gently moved the plate so the spaghetti would not land on the drawing.

Steve ate three plates of spaghetti. Then he ate a fourth.

"Is this how everyone cooks now?" he asked. "Because this is the best thing I've ever eaten."

"Better than pizza in the park?" asked Paisley.

Steve thought very seriously. "Tied," he said.

Then Steve looked around the table. At Tim and Lindsey. At the girls. At Sam. At the yellow flowers in a glass of water in the middle of the table.

"You know," he said quietly. "When I was a kid, we didn't have a lot of money. But on Sundays my mom always made something warm. It didn't matter what. And everybody she knew was welcome." He smiled. "This feels like that."

Lindsey put her hand on his arm. "Then you come every Sunday."

"Every single Sunday," said Tim.

"Even when it's broccoli," said Sydney.

"Especially when it's broccoli," said Steve. "Somebody has to eat it."

After dinner, everybody went into the backyard for the race. Tim stood at one end of the yard. Lindsey stood at the other.

"Once around the oak tree, past the golden tomato plant, and back to me," said Tim. "Ready. Set. GO!"

Lainey took off like a rocket. Steve took off too. Lainey zoomed past the swing set. Steve zoomed past the swing set. Lainey zipped around the oak tree. Steve zipped around the oak tree. They raced past the golden tomato plant side by side.

At the very end, Steve took one long step and touched Tim's hand first.

But only by a tiny bit.

"So close!" said Sam, clapping. "That was the closest anybody's ever come!"

Lainey flopped on the grass, panting. "Almost," she said. "Almost again."

Steve sat down next to her. "You know what? I don't think you're going to be 'almost' forever."

Just then, all five star pins went off. Plus a little red light blinked on a phone in Steve's pocket.

BEEP. BEEP. BEEP.

Tim pressed his pin. The small blue picture that popped up in the air was Natasha. She looked worried, and Natasha almost never looked worried.

"Steve. Tim. Everybody," she said. "We've got trouble. Nick Fury is hurt."

The yard went very quiet.

"Is he okay?" asked Paisley in a small voice.

"He's going to be okay," said Natasha. "The doctors say he'll get better. But someone attacked his car today. And before he went into the hospital, he told me something. He said S.H.I.E.L.D. isn't safe. He said some of the people working there are secretly bad guys."

"Bad guys?" said Tim. "Inside S.H.I.E.L.D.?"

"They have a name," said Natasha. "They call themselves Hydra."

Steve stood up. His face went hard and serious. "Hydra," he said. "No. That can't be. We beat Hydra. We beat them seventy years ago."

"Looks like they were hiding," said Natasha. "Hiding for a long, long time. Inside the very place that was supposed to keep everyone safe."

"Who attacked Fury?" asked Steve.

Natasha paused. "Someone very fast and very strong. He wore a black mask. And he had an arm made of shiny metal, with a red star on the shoulder." She lowered her voice. "Spies like me have heard stories about him for years. Nobody knows his real name. They just call him the Winter Soldier."

Paisley scooted closer to Lindsey. Lainey sat up. Sydney's pencil stopped moving.

"Where are you?" asked Steve.

"Somewhere safe," said Natasha. "I can't say on the pins. Hydra might be listening. Come tomorrow morning. Lindsey, I'm sending you a riddle. You're the only one I know who can solve it."

The picture blinked out.

Lindsey's pin buzzed one more time. Words appeared in the air, glowing blue:

WHERE STEVE LEARNED TO BE A SOLDIER.

Everyone looked at Steve.

"Camp Lehigh," said Steve slowly. "That's where the army trained me, back when I was small. It's in New Jersey. But it's been closed for years. There's nothing there anymore."

Lindsey was already unfolding her map. She ran her finger along the roads. She found New Jersey. She found a little gray square in the woods with a faded name.

"Camp Lehigh," she read. "It's still on the map. Just barely."

Sam looked at Steve. "So. Are we going to New Jersey?"

"You don't have to come," said Steve. "This isn't your fight."

Sam shrugged. "You passed me thirteen times today. I want to see what else you can do."

"Twelve," said Steve.

"Thirteen," said Sam.

Very early the next morning, the family met Steve, Sam, and Natasha at the edge of an old, empty army camp.

The buildings were gray and crumbly. The windows were broken. Tall weeds grew through cracks in the parking lot. A rusty sign hung sideways on a fence.

"It looks spooky," whispered Paisley.

"It looks closed," said Sam.

"It looks like a good place to hide a secret," said Natasha.

Steve walked slowly between the old buildings. He stopped in front of one. It was small and plain, with a dusty door. But the bricks on this building were a little bit different than the bricks on all the others. They were newer.

"This one wasn't here when I was here," said Steve.

"Then that's where we look," said Lindsey.

Inside the little building, there was a dusty room with an old desk and a big bookshelf. That was all.

"Nothing," said Sam.

Sydney walked slowly around the room, the way she did when something was bugging her. She looked at the desk. She looked at the floor. She looked at the bookshelf.

"There are scrape marks on the floor," she said. She pointed. "Little curved lines. Like the bookshelf has been moved. Lots of times."

Steve grabbed one side of the bookshelf and pulled. It swung open like a door.

Behind it was an elevator.

"Good eye, kid," said Natasha. "You'd make a good spy."

Sydney turned pink and wrote SPY in her notebook. Then she crossed it out and wrote SPY?? with two question marks.

The elevator went down. And down. And down. It went so far down that Paisley's ears popped. When the doors opened, they were in a big, dark room underground, full of old computers. The computers were not like Tony's computers. They were huge, as big as refrigerators, with big round tape wheels that spun around and blinking orange lights.

"These are really old," said Sydney. "Like from a museum."

Then one of the screens flickered on. A little green face made of dots appeared. It was an old man with round glasses.

"Hello, Captain," said the dotted face in a crackly voice.

Steve stepped in front of the girls. "Who are you?"

"Someone who has been waiting a very long time," said the face. "I am Hydra. And Hydra has been growing inside S.H.I.E.L.D. for seventy years, like a weed in a garden. Tomorrow, everything will be ready."

"What will be ready?" asked Tim.

"Three new Helicarriers," said the face. "Bigger than the one you rode on. They will fly over every city in the world. And they will make sure that everyone does exactly what Hydra says."

"That's a terrible plan," said Paisley.

The dotted face looked at her. If an old computer could look surprised, it looked surprised. "Who is the child in the crown?"

"I'm Paisley," said Paisley. "And making people do what you say isn't how you make friends."

Lainey giggled. Even Natasha smiled a little.

Then Sydney noticed something. On the side of one of the giant computers, a small red light had started blinking. And a number on the screen was counting down.

10... 9... 8...

"Um," said Sydney. "Is it supposed to be counting?"

Natasha looked. Her eyes went wide. "It's a trap. Everybody out. NOW."

"The elevator is too slow!" said Sam.

Lindsey had already found it on the wall. A little faded sign with an arrow. EMERGENCY STAIRS. "This way!"

Lainey zoomed up the stairs first, two at a time, yelling "Come on, come on!" Tim scooped up Paisley. Steve grabbed Sydney's hand. Natasha and Sam ran behind everyone, making sure nobody fell.

They burst out of a little door in the side of a hill and kept running across the grass.

Behind them, the ground went BOOM. Dust puffed up out of the hill like a sneeze.

Everyone stood on the grass, coughing and breathing hard. Everyone was okay.

"Nobody left behind," said Tim, counting heads. "Five, six, seven, eight. Everybody's here."

"That was close," said Sam.

"Too close," said Steve. He looked at Sydney and squeezed her hand. "If you hadn't seen that blinking light, we'd still be down there talking to a computer."

"It's always the little lights," said Sydney wisely.

That night, they hid at Sam's house. Sam lived in a small, cozy house with lots of pictures on the walls and a refrigerator full of snacks.

"Okay," said Sam, putting a big bowl of popcorn on the table. "Here's what I want to know. Who is this Winter Soldier? Why is he so good at fighting? And why does he work for Hydra?"

Natasha shook her head. "Nobody knows. Nobody has ever seen his face."

Steve was quiet. He had been quiet ever since the old computer. He kept looking at his hands.

Paisley climbed up on the couch next to him. "You're thinking really hard," she said. "You're tapping your knee. Like when I tap my crown."

Steve smiled a little. "I guess I am."

"What are you thinking about?"

"Old friends," said Steve.

The next morning, Steve asked the family to come somewhere with him before the big day. Somewhere quiet. They drove to a big, peaceful house with white walls and a garden full of roses. Inside, there were nurses in soft shoes, and people reading books in big chairs.

Steve walked down a hallway and knocked on a door. He was holding his hat in his hands. The girls had never seen him look nervous before.

"Come in," said a voice.

Inside the room, sitting up in a bed by a sunny window, was a very old lady. Her hair was white and soft. Her face had lots of wrinkles. But her eyes were bright and sharp and very, very kind.

"Steve," she said, and she smiled the biggest smile.

"Hi, Peggy," said Steve.

Paisley gasped. She remembered the black-and-white photo on Lindsey's phone. The lady with dark curly hair and bright red lipstick.

"You're Peggy!" said Paisley. "From the picture!"

Peggy laughed. It was a light, lovely laugh. "I was a good deal younger in that picture, darling."

Steve pulled a chair close to the bed and sat down. He held Peggy's hand very gently. The family stood near the door, quiet, so they wouldn't get in the way.

"You came back," said Peggy.

"I'm sorry I was late," said Steve.

"Seventy years late," said Peggy, with a twinkle in her eye. "That's quite a lot of late, Captain."

"I know," said Steve. "I still owe you a dance."

"Yes, you do," said Peggy. "And I don't plan on letting you forget it."

Then Peggy looked over at the family. "And who are all these lovely people?"

"These are my friends," said Steve. "Tim and Lindsey. Paisley, Lainey, and Sydney. They found me when I was lost. They've been teaching me how everything works now."

"Has he figured out the telephones yet?" Peggy asked the girls.

"No," said all three girls at once.

Peggy laughed so hard she had to wipe her eyes.

Then she looked at Steve, and her face grew serious. "Steve. You look worried. You always got that little line between your eyebrows when you were worried."

So Steve told her. About Hydra hiding inside S.H.I.E.L.D. About the three giant Helicarriers. About the man with the metal arm.

Peggy listened to every word. When he was done, she squeezed his hand.

"Steve," she said. "I helped build S.H.I.E.L.D. a very long time ago. I built it to protect people. If it's been poisoned, then it's not S.H.I.E.L.D. anymore. Not really." She looked into his eyes. "So you do what you always do. You stand up. Even when you're standing up alone."

"He won't be alone," said Tim.

Peggy looked at Tim. Then she looked at Lindsey, and at each of the girls.

"No," she said softly. "I can see that he won't."

Before they left, Peggy waved Paisley over to the bed.

"You're the one who gave him the little dog," she whispered. "Aren't you?"

Paisley nodded. "His name is Biscuit."

"Thank you for looking after him," Peggy whispered. "Somebody needed to."

The next day was the big day.

The three new Helicarriers sat on a long runway beside a river in Washington, D.C. They were enormous. Each one was as big as a whole town, gray and shiny, with giant fans on the sides. People in S.H.I.E.L.D. uniforms hurried around them, getting them ready.

Some of those people were good. And some of them were secretly Hydra. And nobody could tell which was which.

The family hid with Steve, Sam, and Natasha on top of a building, looking down through binoculars.

Steve was wearing his old uniform now. Not the shiny new one. The one from long ago, from the museum, with a dull star and brown leather straps. Sam had on his metal wings, folded up into a backpack.

"Here's the plan," said Natasha. "Each Helicarrier has a special computer chip near the top. If we swap each one for one of ours, the Helicarriers will stop listening to Hydra. They'll start listening to us."

"Three Helicarriers. Three chips," said Steve.

"And not much time," said Natasha. "When they go up, they'll start doing what Hydra wants."

Then Steve did something brave. He walked into the S.H.I.E.L.D. control room, right where everyone could see him, and he took the microphone that talked to the whole building.

The family watched him on a little screen in Natasha's hand.

"This is Captain Rogers," said Steve's voice, everywhere at once. "You've heard a lot about me. Most of it is probably true. Here's something else that's true. Hydra is hiding inside S.H.I.E.L.D. They want to use these new Helicarriers to control everyone. I'm going to stop them. And if you're one of the good ones, I'm asking you to help me."

All over the building, people looked at each other.

Then a woman at a computer stood up. "I'm with the Captain," she said.

Then a man stood up. Then another. Then lots of them.

Lindsey wiped her eyes. "That," she said, "is what a captain sounds like."

Tim nodded. He put his hand over the star pin on his shirt.

But just then, with a deep rumble, the three giant Helicarriers began to lift into the sky.

"They're launching!" said Sam. "Let's go!"

Sam opened his wings. WHOOSH! Silver feathers made of metal snapped out on either side of him, and he shot up into the air like a bird.

"WHOA!" yelled Lainey. "That's the coolest thing EVER!"

Steve ran and jumped onto the first Helicarrier as it lifted past the building. Natasha raced to a jet. And Tim and Lindsey and the girls ran down the stairs to a little S.H.I.E.L.D. control room by the river, where a friendly officer named Maria Hill was waiting with a headset.

"Natasha told me about you," said Maria. "She says you see things other people miss. I need eyes on those chips."

Sydney sat right down at the big computer screen. On it were three pictures of the Helicarriers, side by side. Each one had a little blinking dot near the top.

"Each dot is a chip slot," said Maria. "When one gets swapped, the dot turns green."

Sam swooped through the air like a falcon. He landed on the second Helicarrier, dodged two Hydra guards, and found the slot. He pulled out the old chip and pushed in the new one.

Sydney watched the screen. The second dot turned green.

"Two is green!" she said into the headset. "Good job, Sam!"

"Thanks, kid!" said Sam's voice.

Natasha's voice came on next. "I've got the third one." A minute later, Sydney saw the third dot turn green.

"Three is green!" said Sydney.

"That leaves the first one," said Maria. "Steve's."

But on the first Helicarrier, someone was waiting for Steve.

He stood in the middle of a metal walkway. He wore a black mask over the bottom of his face, and black goggles. His long dark hair blew in the wind. And his left arm was made of shiny silver metal, with a red star on the shoulder.

The Winter Soldier.

The family watched on the screen. Paisley grabbed Lindsey's hand.

The Winter Soldier was fast. He was strong. He moved like a machine. Steve blocked with his shield. CLANG. The Winter Soldier swung his metal arm. CLANG. Steve flipped. The Winter Soldier spun. They fought back and forth across the walkway, high above the river.

Then the Winter Soldier's mask fell off.

Steve stopped. He stood very still. He stared.

"Bucky?" he said.

Under the mask was a face the family had seen before. In the old black-and-white photograph. The man with the big friendly grin who had his arm around Steve's shoulders.

Except now he wasn't grinning. His eyes were empty and confused.

"Who is Bucky?" said the Winter Soldier, in a flat, cold voice.

In the control room, Paisley's mouth fell open. "That's his best friend," she whispered. "From when they were kids."

"But he fell," said Tim. "Steve said he fell off a train a long time ago."

"Hydra must have found him," said Natasha's voice through the headset. Her voice was very quiet. "They must have done something to his mind. Like Loki's scepter did to Clint. They made him forget who he was."

On the Helicarrier, Steve lowered his shield. "Bucky. It's me. It's Steve."

The Winter Soldier didn't seem to hear. He just kept fighting.

"We need that last chip in," said Maria. "Fast."

Steve dodged a punch. He ran for the chip slot. He pulled out the old chip. He pushed in the new one.

The first dot on Sydney's screen stayed red.

"It's not working!" said Sydney. "It's still red! Steve, it didn't go in all the way!"

Steve pushed it again. Still red.

Sydney leaned so close to the screen her nose almost touched it. She looked at the tiny picture of the slot. She thought about Tony's boot rocket. About the little blue wire. About how sometimes things don't fit because they're turned around.

"Steve!" she said. "Turn it over! The little gold lines go on the BOTTOM! It's upside down!"

On the screen, Steve pulled the chip out. He flipped it. He pushed it in.

CLICK.

The first dot turned green.

"ALL THREE ARE GREEN!" shouted Sydney.

Maria Hill pressed a big button. "Then let's turn them around."

High in the sky, the three giant Helicarriers stopped listening to Hydra. Instead of doing what Hydra wanted, they turned their giant cannons toward each other. Not toward the city. Not toward the people. Toward each other.

"Everyone off those ships!" said Maria into every radio at once. "Everyone get off!"

People poured out of the Helicarriers into rescue jets and little boats on the river. Sam swooped back and forth, helping people get away.

Then the three Helicarriers went BOOM, BOOM, BOOM, one after another, in big puffs of fire and smoke. Not one person was still on them. Everyone had gotten off in time.

But Steve was still on the first Helicarrier. And so was Bucky.

The ship was falling apart. A big metal beam fell and landed on Bucky, pinning him to the floor. He couldn't move.

Steve lifted the beam off him. Then he stood in front of him. He dropped his shield.

"I'm not going to fight you," said Steve. "You're my friend."

"You're my mission," said Bucky, but his voice was shaky now.

"Then finish it," said Steve softly. "Because I'm with you till the end of the line."

Bucky froze. Something flickered in his eyes. Something like a memory.

In the control room, Paisley pushed herself up next to Sydney and grabbed the headset.

"Bucky!" she said into it. "Bucky, it's okay. You're having really big feelings and you don't know what to do with them. I know. Try breathing. In, like smelling a flower."

On the screen, Bucky blinked. He looked up, confused, like he heard a tiny voice from far away.

"Out, like birthday candles," said Paisley. "Steve says you were his best friend since you were little. You used to stand up for him when he was small. When the bullies knocked him down. Do you remember?"

Bucky's eyes filled up. He looked at Steve. He looked at Steve's face very carefully, as if he was seeing it for the first time.

"You were small," said Bucky slowly. "You were so small. And you never stopped picking fights you couldn't win."

Steve smiled, even though his eyes were wet. "That's right. That's right, pal."

Then the floor under Steve broke apart. CRACK.

Steve fell. Down, down, down, through the smoke and into the river below. SPLASH.

"STEVE!" screamed all three girls at once.

Lindsey grabbed Natasha's little screen and pressed it against her chest. Tim put his arms around all three girls. Nobody breathed.

Then, on the riverbank, a shape climbed out of the water.

It was Bucky. And he was dragging Steve with him.

Bucky laid Steve gently on the grass. Steve coughed. He opened his eyes. He was okay.

Bucky stood over him for a moment. He looked like he wanted to say something. But he didn't know how yet. So he turned and walked away into the trees.

When the family got to the riverbank, Steve was sitting up, wet and tired, looking at the woods.

"He saved me," said Steve. "He remembered. Just a little. But he did."

"Where did he go?" asked Lainey.

"I don't know," said Steve. "I think he needs to figure out who he is again. Hydra took a lot of years from him."

Tim knelt down next to Steve in the wet grass. "Then we'll help you find him," he said. "When he's ready. Nobody gets left behind. Remember?"

Steve looked at Tim for a long moment. Then he smiled.

"I remember," said Steve. "I'm the one who told you."

"I know," said Tim. "I'm just reminding you."

Lindsey unfolded her map. On the front, under LOKI = TROUBLE, she wrote in neat letters:

FIND BUCKY. (When he's ready.)

Sam landed beside them with a soft WHOOSH, his metal wings folding up behind him.

"So," said Sam, crossing his arms. "That was a lot."

"That was a lot," agreed Natasha.

"Thanks for coming," said Steve. "You didn't have to."

"Are you kidding?" said Sam. "I got to fly. I got to help. And I met the smartest computer kid in the world." He held out his fist to Sydney. Sydney bumped it with hers. "Upside-down chip. Who knew?"

Then Sam looked at Lainey. Lainey was staring at his wings with very big eyes.

"Let me guess," said Sam. "You want to race."

"YES," said Lainey.

"Tomorrow," said Sam. "Your legs against my wings. On the ground. No flying."

"Deal," said Lainey, and she shook his hand very hard.

Natasha walked up last. She didn't say anything at first. She just knelt down and gave Paisley a quick hug.

"That breathing thing," said Natasha quietly. "That got through to him. You know that, right?"

Paisley straightened her crown. "Big feelings are easier with a friend," she said. "Even if the friend is on a headset."

The next Sunday, the doorbell rang at exactly five o'clock.

Paisley opened the door this time. Steve was there with yellow flowers and a loaf of bread. Sam was there with a big bowl of salad. And Natasha was there, too, holding a pie.

"I don't really cook," said Natasha. "So I bought it. Don't tell anyone."

"Your secret's safe," said Lindsey, winking.

They ate spaghetti. They told stories. They laughed until they cried. Sam told everyone that Lainey had beaten him in the race that morning, fair and square.

"Only because his wings kept getting caught on the bushes," said Lainey.

"That is also true," said Sam.

After dinner, Steve went out to the backyard alone. Paisley followed him. He was standing by the golden tomato plant, looking up at the first stars.

"You're thinking about Bucky," said Paisley.

"I am," said Steve.

"And Peggy," said Paisley.

Steve laughed softly. "How do you always know?"

"You get the little line between your eyebrows," said Paisley. "Peggy told me."

Steve laughed again. Then he reached into his pocket and pulled out Biscuit. The little brown puppy's floppy ear flopped in the breeze.

"He's been a lot of help," said Steve.

"You can keep him longer," said Paisley. "Until you find Bucky. And until you get your dance."

Steve smiled and put Biscuit back in his pocket, right over his heart.

"Deal," he said.

And in the garden, one of the tiny golden tomatoes had grown just a little bit bigger.

## Chapter 7: The Rainbow Bridge

It was a rainy Saturday, and the family was playing a board game in the living room.

Paisley was winning. Lainey was losing and pretending she wasn't. Sydney was counting everybody's points twice to make sure they were right. Tim had just landed on the worst square on the whole board, and Lindsey was trying very hard not to laugh.

Then the rain stopped. All at once. Like someone had turned off a faucet.

The room went bright. A rumble rolled across the sky.

Rrrrrrrrrumble.

"Oh no," said Lindsey. She jumped up and ran to the back window. "Not my garden. Please not my garden."

FLASH! BOOOOM!

The whole house shook. The board game pieces bounced. A picture fell off the wall.

But the lightning didn't hit the garden this time. It hit the front yard. Right next to Tim's car.

Everyone ran to the front door. In the middle of the lawn there was a small round dent in the grass, with smoke curling out of it. Standing in the dent, with his red cape flapping and his hammer in his hand, was Thor.

He looked at the front yard. He looked back at the house. He looked very proud of himself.

"Lady Lindsey!" he boomed. "Behold! I did not land in your garden!"

Lindsey put her hand on her heart. "Thank you, Thor."

"I aimed very carefully," said Thor.

Just then, Tim's car alarm started going WEE-OOO WEE-OOO. One of the car's side mirrors fell off and landed in the grass with a little plop.

Thor looked at the mirror. "Almost very carefully," he said.

"It's okay," said Tim, picking up the mirror. "It was getting old anyway."

The girls ran out onto the wet grass and threw their arms around Thor's legs. He laughed his big thunder laugh and lifted all three of them up at once.

"Princess Paisley! Little Rocket Lainey! Hawk-Eyed Sydney!"

"Did you come for pancakes?" asked Lainey.

"I always come for pancakes," said Thor. "But today I also came with a question." He set them down and turned to Paisley. "Do you remember, at your campout, what you asked about Asgard?"

Paisley's eyes went very big. "I asked if we could ever see the rainbow bridge."

"Well," said Thor, smiling. "Would you like to?"

Paisley could not speak. She just nodded, over and over, so hard her crown almost fell off.

"There is a second reason," said Thor, and his face grew a little worried. "I have a friend named Jane. She is a scientist. She studies the stars. She is very clever, and very brave, and she is in trouble. I am taking her to Asgard so our healers can help her. I thought she might feel braver with some friends from Earth beside her."

"Then of course we'll come," said Lindsey.

"Do we need to pack?" asked Sydney.

"Toothbrushes," said Tim. "Always toothbrushes."

Ten minutes later, everyone stood in a circle on the front lawn with their backpacks on. Next to Thor was a woman with brown hair and a rumpled jacket. She looked tired, and a little pale. But she smiled when she saw the girls.

"Hi," she said. "I'm Jane. Thor talks about you guys all the time. Especially the pancakes."

"Are you sick?" asked Paisley gently.

"Sort of," said Jane. She rubbed her arm. "A few days ago, I found something strange in an old building in London. It was red and swirly, like smoke and water mixed together. It got inside me. Now I feel sleepy all the time. And sometimes my skin glows a little."

She pushed up her sleeve. Under her skin, very faint, a red light swirled, like a lava lamp.

"Whoa," whispered Sydney. She wrote RED SWIRLY THING in her notebook.

"Everybody hold hands," said Thor. He looked up at the sky. "Heimdall! We are ready!"

For a second, nothing happened. Then a beam of light came down from the clouds.

It wasn't a regular beam of light. It was every color at once. Red and orange and yellow and green and blue and purple, all twisting together like a rope made of rainbows.

It wrapped around them. And then—

WHOOOOOOOOSH!

They shot up into the sky.

Paisley would remember the rainbow bridge for the rest of her life.

It was like being inside a kaleidoscope. Colors zoomed past on every side. Stars streaked by like they were racing. And there was a sound. A soft, high, humming sound, like a hundred voices singing one long note.

"It's SINGING!" Paisley shouted. "Thor, you were right! It's singing!"

Lainey tried to run inside the rainbow, but her legs just pedaled in the air. "I'm racing the light!" she yelled. "I'm winning!"

"You are not winning," said Sydney. "The light is winning."

Tim was holding Lindsey's hand very tightly. His face was a little green. "This is like the elevator at Stark Tower," he said. "But a thousand times faster."

Lindsey was laughing so hard she could barely hold her map.

Then—THUMP. Their feet landed on something solid.

They were standing in a round golden room at the very end of a long bridge. The bridge stretched out in front of them, and it was made of glass. Under the glass, colors rippled and flowed, like a river of rainbows.

At the end of the bridge was a city.

It was gold. All of it. Golden towers rose up so tall that clouds wrapped around their tops. Golden domes shone in the sunlight. Waterfalls poured off the edges of the city into a sparkling sea of stars below.

Nobody said anything for a long time.

"Welcome to Asgard," said Thor softly.

Standing at the edge of the round golden room was a tall man in golden armor. He held a huge sword. And his eyes were bright gold, too, like two shining coins.

"This is Heimdall," said Thor. "He guards the Bifrost. He can see everything in all the nine worlds."

"Everything?" asked Sydney.

Heimdall looked down at her. "Everything," he said in a deep, calm voice. "For example, I can see that you have a pencil behind your ear, a granola bar in your left pocket, and a drawing of me already started in your notebook."

Sydney looked down at her notebook. She had already started drawing him. She hadn't even noticed.

Heimdall smiled. It was a very small smile. But it was a real one.

"Could you see our house?" asked Lainey. "Right now?"

Heimdall looked out at the stars for a moment. "There is a tomato plant in your garden," he said. "It has grown three inches since you left this morning."

Lindsey gasped. "My golden tomatoes!"

"They are happy," said Heimdall. "Asgard seeds always grow faster when the soil is loved."

Thor walked them across the rainbow bridge into the golden city. Lindsey turned her map over and started drawing on a blank page. She drew the bridge. She drew the towers. She drew the waterfalls.

"Now Asgard is on a map," she said.

"I believe," said Thor, "that is the very first map of Asgard ever made by a person from Earth."

Lindsey looked very pleased.

The palace was the biggest building any of them had ever seen. Inside, there was a long hall with golden pillars and a ceiling painted with stars. At the very end of the hall, on a tall golden throne, sat an old man with a long white beard and a golden patch over one eye.

"That is my father," whispered Thor. "Odin. King of Asgard."

Odin looked down at them from his throne. He did not smile.

"Thor," he said in a rumbling voice. "You have brought humans to Asgard."

"I have, Father," said Thor. "They are my friends. They helped me on Earth. They helped stop Loki."

"Hmm," said Odin. He looked at each of them carefully, one at a time. When he got to Paisley, he stopped. "And what is a small human doing wearing a crown?"

Paisley curtsied, just like she did in the backyard. "I'm Princess Paisley," she said. "Well, not a real princess. But I'm working on it."

Odin looked at her for a long moment.

Then, very slowly, one corner of his beard twitched upward.

"Then you are welcome in my hall," said Odin. "Princess."

A tall woman with long golden hair walked down the steps from the throne. She wore a soft blue dress, and her face was gentle and wise.

"And I," she said, "am Thor's mother. Frigga. Welcome, all of you." She knelt down to look at Jane, and her face grew worried. "And you, my dear, must be Jane. Come. Our healers are waiting."

The healing room was full of soft golden light. Jane lay down on a bed made of shimmering stone. Glowing shapes floated in the air above her. The healers waved their hands, and the shapes turned and twisted.

The red swirly light inside Jane swirled faster.

The healers frowned. They whispered to each other. Then one of them shook her head.

"What is it?" asked Thor.

Odin had come into the room. He was looking at the red light too, and his face was very grim.

"It is the Aether," said Odin. "An old and terrible thing. Long ago, a race called the Dark Elves tried to use it to turn every star in every world dark. Forever. My father hid it away where no one could find it." He shook his head. "And now it has found its way into your friend."

"Can you take it out?" asked Tim.

"No," said Odin. "Not with any healing we know."

Jane's face went pale.

Lindsey sat down next to her and took her hand. "Then we'll find another way," she said firmly. "There's always another way. Sometimes you just have to look harder."

Sydney was standing by the healers, watching the glowing shapes. She tapped her pencil against her notebook.

"Mr. Odin," she said. "Sir. Your Majesty. Is the Aether one of those special stones? The Infinity Stones? Because Thor said there were six. And this one is red. And it's really, really powerful."

Odin turned his one eye toward her. He looked surprised.

"You know of the Infinity Stones?" he said.

"We're keeping a list," said Lindsey, and she turned her map over to show him.

Odin read the list. He was quiet for a long time.

"Yes," he said finally. "The Aether is an Infinity Stone. The Reality Stone. It can change what is real. It can make you see things that are not there. Or hide things that are."

Lindsey took out her pen. Under 1. Space Stone, she wrote:

2. Reality Stone (red). Inside Jane. Called the Aether.

"Well," said Thor, trying to sound cheerful. "We cannot solve it tonight. Tonight, we feast!"

The feast was in a great hall full of long wooden tables. There were roasted vegetables, golden bread, bowls of fruit the girls had never seen before, and a cake as tall as Lainey. Asgardians in armor and shining dresses laughed and sang and banged their cups on the tables.

When one big Asgardian finished his drink, he held up his cup and shouted, "ANOTHER!" And he threw his cup on the floor. CRASH.

Every single person in the family turned and looked at Thor.

Thor's cheeks went pink. He stood up on his bench.

"Friends!" he called out to the hall. "On Earth, I have learned a better way! When you want more, you do not smash things. You say PLEASE."

The hall went quiet. The Asgardians looked at each other.

Then the big Asgardian picked up a new cup. He held it up. And in a very loud voice, he shouted, "ANOTHER, PLEASE!"

Everyone cheered. Lindsey laughed so hard she had to hold onto the table.

"That one is because of you, Lady Lindsey," said Thor proudly.

The next morning, Thor took them to the royal stables.

The stables were tall and golden and smelled like hay. Inside, horses with shining white coats stood in big stalls. But at the very end, in the biggest stall of all, stood a horse that was gray, with a long silver mane.

And he had eight legs.

"SLEIPNIR!" shouted Paisley.

The horse stamped all eight of his feet at once. CLOP-CLOP-CLOP-CLOP-CLOP-CLOP-CLOP-CLOP. It sounded like a drum.

Paisley walked up slowly and held out her hand. Sleipnir sniffed it. Then he lowered his huge head and gently bumped her crown with his nose.

"He likes you," said Thor.

"He's the most beautiful horse in all the worlds," said Paisley.

Lainey walked up next. She looked Sleipnir right in the eye.

"I told Thor I could beat you," said Lainey. "So I came to find out."

Sleipnir snorted. It sounded like a laugh.

Thor grinned. "There is a racing field behind the palace. Once around the field and back to the golden post."

The whole family came to watch. So did Frigga, and Jane, wrapped in a warm blanket. Even some of the Asgardian guards came to see.

Lainey crouched at the starting line. Sleipnir pawed the ground with four of his eight feet.

"Ready," said Thor. "Set. GO!"

Lainey shot off like a rocket. Sleipnir thundered after her. CLOP-CLOP-CLOP-CLOP-CLOP-CLOP-CLOP-CLOP!

For the first half of the race, Lainey was in front. The Asgardians gasped. A tiny girl from Earth, beating the fastest horse in all the worlds!

Then Sleipnir's eight legs started to really go. He caught up. They were side by side. Lainey ran so fast her face turned bright red. Sleipnir ran so fast his silver mane streamed straight back like a flag.

They reached the golden post. Sleipnir's nose crossed first. By about the width of one finger.

The guards roared and cheered and banged their spears on their shields. Lainey flopped down in the grass, breathing hard.

"Almost," she panted. "ALMOST. Again!"

Sleipnir walked over and stood above her. Then he lowered his head and licked her whole face with one giant, slobbery lick.

"EWWWW!" yelled Lainey. But she was laughing.

"In all my years," said Thor, kneeling beside her, "I have never seen anyone run that close to Sleipnir. Not even me."

He reached into a pouch on his belt and pulled out a small silver horseshoe.

"An eight-legged horse has eight horseshoes," said Thor. "This is one of his old ones. He wants you to have it."

Lainey held the silver horseshoe in both hands. She didn't say anything. She just hugged it to her chest.

That afternoon, everything went wrong.

The family was in the palace garden with Jane and Frigga. Paisley was showing Frigga how she did the calming breath. Frigga was trying it, and laughing at how silly it felt. Then the sky turned dark.

Not cloudy dark. Dark-dark. Like someone had put a blanket over the sun.

A huge black ship appeared in the sky above Asgard. It was shaped like a long, sharp blade. Smaller black ships zoomed out of it and dove toward the palace.

BOOM! BOOM! BOOM!

"Dark Elves," said Frigga. She stood up straight and pulled a silver sword from beside the bench. All of a sudden she didn't look like a gentle queen anymore. She looked like a warrior. "They have come for the Aether. They have come for Jane."

Jane's arm was glowing bright red now, brighter than ever before.

"Everybody inside!" said Frigga. "Follow me!"

They ran into the palace. Behind them came the Dark Elves. They were tall and thin, with pale white faces like masks, and long black coats. They moved quickly and quietly. They did not talk. That was the scariest part.

Frigga led them to her room and shut the big golden doors. Tim and Lindsey stood in front of the girls. Jane sat down on the bed, shaking.

"Stay behind me," said Frigga.

The doors burst open. BANG!

Three Dark Elves rushed in. At the front was the tallest one, with a face that was half white and half dark gray. He had cold, hungry eyes.

"I am Malekith," he said, in a voice like ice. "Give me the Aether."

"No," said Frigga.

Then something amazing happened. Frigga waved her sword, and suddenly there were two of her. Then three. Then five. Five Friggas, all with swords, all standing in front of the family.

"It's a trick!" whispered Sydney. "She's making copies! Like magic mirrors!"

Malekith's elves didn't know which Frigga to fight. They swung at one, and it vanished in a puff of gold sparkles. They swung at another, and it vanished too. The real Frigga spun and struck, and her sword went CLANG against their swords.

One of the Dark Elves slipped around the side of the room. It crept toward Jane.

Tim saw it. He ran forward. He meant to grab the elf's arm.

But Tim's foot caught on the edge of a fancy rug.

He tripped. He flew forward. He crashed straight into the Dark Elf with his whole body, and they both went tumbling across the floor and into a big pile of soft pillows. WHUMP!

The Dark Elf's helmet bonked against a golden pillar. BONK. It blinked. It wobbled. And it fell over, too dizzy to stand up.

Tim sat up in the pillows. He looked at the elf. He looked at Lindsey.

"I meant to do that," said Tim.

"Sure you did, honey," said Lindsey.

Then the doors burst open again. Thor came charging in with Mjolnir spinning. He swung, and WHAM, the last Dark Elf went flying out the window.

Malekith stepped back. He looked at Thor. He looked at the five Friggas. He looked at Jane's glowing arm.

"This is not over," he hissed. "Soon, the worlds will line up. When they do, darkness will win." And he jumped out the window and vanished into his black ship.

The ship rose up into the sky and disappeared.

The five Friggas turned into one Frigga again. She lowered her sword. She was breathing hard, but she was okay. Everyone was okay.

Thor ran to her and hugged her. "Mother. Are you hurt?"

"I am perfectly well," said Frigga. Then she looked at Tim, still sitting in the pile of pillows. "Thanks to some help."

Tim brushed a feather out of his hair. "Anytime."

That night, everyone gathered in a room in the palace. Odin. Frigga. Thor. Jane. The whole family. Everyone was worried.

"Malekith said the worlds will line up," said Lindsey. "What does that mean?"

"It is called the Convergence," said Frigga. "Once every five thousand years, all nine worlds line up in a row, like beads on a string. When that happens, the walls between the worlds get very thin. Things can slip through from one world to another."

"And if Malekith has the Aether when the worlds line up," said Odin, "he can spread darkness through all of them at once."

"So we can't let him have it," said Sydney.

"But we need to get it out of Jane," said Thor. "It is making her weaker every day."

Everyone was quiet.

"I know someone who could help," said Thor slowly. "He knows secret paths out of Asgard that no one else knows. He knows tricks and spells and hidden things."

Lindsey looked at Thor. She knew exactly who he meant. She unfolded the front of her map and pointed at the big letters at the top.

LOKI = TROUBLE

"I know," said Thor. "But he is also my brother."

Loki was in a prison deep under the palace. It wasn't a prison with bars. It was a small, clean room with glowing golden walls that you could see through, like glass. He was sitting on a bench reading a book. He looked bored.

When he saw the family, he raised one eyebrow.

"Well," said Loki. "The goat-horn girl. And her whole family. How delightful."

"They're not goat horns," said Paisley. "They're your helmet. I just said they LOOKED like goat horns."

Loki looked at her. For a second he looked like he didn't know what to say. That almost never happened to Loki.

Thor explained everything. The Aether. Jane. Malekith. The Convergence.

"And you want my help," said Loki, smiling his sneaky smile. "How interesting. And what do I get?"

"A chance," said Thor. "To do something good. For once."

Loki laughed. But it wasn't a real laugh.

Paisley walked right up to the glowing wall. She looked at Loki through it.

"Are you lonely down here?" she asked.

Loki blinked. "What?"

"You're all by yourself," said Paisley. "With nobody to talk to. I'd be lonely."

Loki opened his mouth. Then he closed it. He looked down at his book. "Nobody has asked me that," he said quietly. "Not in a very long time."

"Well," said Paisley. "I'm asking."

Loki was quiet for a long moment.

"Fine," he said at last, standing up. "I'll help. But only because I'm bored."

Lindsey narrowed her eyes. She took out her pen. Under LOKI = TROUBLE, she wrote: WATCH HIM.

Loki's plan was sneaky, of course.

He knew a secret, hidden path between the worlds, through a crack in a cave. It led to Svartalfheim, the gray, dusty world where the Dark Elves came from.

"If Malekith wants the Aether so badly," said Loki, "we bring it to him. He'll pull it out of Jane himself. And when he does, we'll be ready."

"That is a very risky plan," said Frigga.

"All the best plans are," said Loki.

The family went with them, holding hands as they stepped through the crack in the cave. On the other side was a world of gray sand and black rocks and a dim, sad sun.

Malekith's black ship was waiting.

It happened just like Loki said. Loki pretended to betray Thor. He shouted mean things and pushed Thor down onto the sand. Malekith watched and smiled. He thought Loki was on his side now.

"Take her," said Loki, pointing at Jane. "Take the Aether. I don't care."

Malekith raised his hand toward Jane. The red swirly light came pouring out of her, like smoke out of a chimney. It twisted through the air toward Malekith.

Jane gasped. She sat up. Her face got pink again, right away. "I feel better," she whispered. "I feel so much better!"

"NOW!" shouted Loki.

Thor jumped up and swung Mjolnir at the red cloud of Aether.

But the Aether was too fast. It zoomed into Malekith's hand. His eyes turned red. He laughed. And then his ship lifted him up and carried him away, through a crack in the sky.

"Where's he going?" asked Lainey.

Lindsey was already looking at her map. She looked at the sky. She looked at the map. She looked at the sky again.

"If all the worlds line up like beads on a string," she said slowly, "then the middle bead is the most important. And the middle of the string is..." She tapped her map. "Earth. London. Where Jane found the Aether in the first place."

Thor stared at her. "Lady Lindsey. How did you know that?"

"Maps," said Lindsey. "Everything makes sense if you look at the map long enough."

They got back to Earth through another secret crack, which came out in a very surprised man's kitchen in London. The man was eating cereal. He dropped his spoon.

"Sorry!" said everyone, all together, as they ran out his front door.

In a big green park in London, the sky was going strange. Holes were opening and closing in the air. Through one hole, you could see a world of snow. Through another, a world of fire. A car fell through a hole and disappeared. A flock of pigeons flew through a hole and came out the other side as a flock of very confused purple birds.

And in the middle of the park, Malekith stood with the red Aether swirling around him like a storm.

Waiting for them was a young woman with big glasses and a woolly hat, holding an armful of strange metal poles.

"Darcy!" said Jane. "These are my friends! Everybody, this is Darcy. She helps me with my science."

"Hi," said Darcy. "Good timing. Also, the sky is broken. Also, I brought the spikes."

"What are the spikes?" asked Sydney.

Jane grabbed one of the metal poles. It had a pointy end and a little blinking light on top. "I built these to measure the holes between worlds. But I think, if we put them in the right places, we could use them to push Malekith through a hole. Right back to his own dark world."

"Where are the right places?" asked Tim.

"That's the problem," said Jane. "The holes keep moving. I can't figure out the pattern."

Sydney looked up at the sky. She watched the holes open and close. Open and close. She counted. She drew lines in her notebook. Her pencil moved faster and faster.

"They're not random," said Sydney. "Look. Every time one closes over there, a new one opens over here. It's like a clock. They go around in a circle."

Lindsey looked at Sydney's drawing. Then she looked at her own map of the park. She held them side by side.

"Then if we put the spikes in a circle," said Lindsey, "right here, here, here, and here..." She drew five little X marks on her map. "...Malekith will be in the very middle when the next big hole opens."

"That would work!" said Jane, staring at them. "That would actually work!"

"There's just one problem," said Darcy. "Those spots are all the way across the park. And the next big hole opens in about two minutes."

Everyone turned and looked at the same person.

Lainey was already holding out her arms. "Give me the spikes."

Darcy piled the metal spikes into Lainey's arms. Lindsey held up the map so Lainey could see the five X marks.

"Here. Then here. Then here, here, and here," said Lindsey. "Go."

Lainey went.

She ran faster than she ran from the Hulk's lab. Faster than she ran in the Battle of New York. Faster than she raced Sleipnir. She jumped over a park bench. She ducked under a hole full of snow. She zipped past a very surprised duck.

At the first X, she stabbed a spike into the grass. Beep!

At the second X, beep!

At the third, beep! At the fourth, beep!

Malekith saw her. He turned and threw a ball of red smoke at her. Lainey zigged. The smoke missed. She zagged. It missed again. She dove for the last X and jammed the last spike into the ground.

BEEP!

All five spikes lit up blue at once. A circle of blue light ran between them, around and around Malekith.

"Now, Thor!" shouted Jane.

Thor spun Mjolnir. Lightning crackled across the sky. He flew straight at Malekith and hit him with all his might. WHAAAAM!

Malekith stumbled backward into the middle of the blue circle. Right then, the biggest hole yet opened up behind him. Through it, you could see his gray, dusty, gloomy world.

Malekith fell through the hole.

But the red Aether didn't go with him. It swirled up, away from his hand, like it didn't want to go to a dark gloomy world either.

"Thor!" yelled Paisley. "Catch it!"

Thor held out a small silver box that Frigga had given him. The red swirl spun down into it, around and around, like water going down a drain.

Click. The lid shut.

The hole closed with a soft little pop. The other holes in the sky closed, too. Pop. Pop. Pop. The sun came out. A very confused purple bird landed on Tim's head.

Everybody cheered.

That evening, Thor and the family met a woman named Sif, a tall Asgardian warrior with long dark hair. She would help Thor carry the silver box somewhere safe.

"Two Infinity Stones in Asgard would be unwise," explained Thor. "We already keep the Space Stone in my father's vault. If anyone ever came looking, they would find both in one place."

"So where will the Reality Stone go?" asked Sydney.

"To a man called the Collector," said Sif. "He lives far out in space, in a strange place called Knowhere. He collects rare and powerful things. He will keep it locked away."

Lindsey took out her pen. She crossed out "Inside Jane" and wrote instead:

2. Reality Stone (red). The Collector has it. In a place called Knowhere.

"That's a very weird name for a place," said Lainey.

"It is a very weird place," said Thor.

The family went home the same way they came, across the singing rainbow bridge. But before they left, Loki came to say goodbye.

Thor had told Odin how Loki helped. Odin had grumbled. But in the end, he let Loki out of the glowing prison. He had to stay in the palace and help in the library. But at least he could see the sky.

Loki stood at the edge of the bridge with his hands behind his back. He didn't say anything. He just nodded at them, very stiffly.

Paisley walked up to him.

"Thank you for helping," she said.

Loki looked down at her for a long time.

"You're welcome," he said finally. It sounded like a very strange thing for him to say. Like he hadn't said it in a hundred years.

Then Paisley hugged him around the waist.

Loki froze like an ice cube. He held his arms out to the sides, like he didn't know where to put them. Then, very slowly, very awkwardly, he patted her on top of her crown. Pat. Pat.

When they got home, the front yard was dark and quiet. Tim's car still had only one side mirror.

Lindsey unfolded her map one more time. She looked at the words at the top. LOKI = TROUBLE. WATCH HIM.

Then she clicked her pen. And underneath, in very small letters, she wrote:

...but maybe not ALL trouble.

In the backyard, under the moon, the golden tomato plant had grown three more inches, just like Heimdall said. And far, far away, across the stars, someone with golden eyes was watching it grow, and smiling.

## Chapter 8: The Guardians of the Galaxy

It was the middle of the night when the star pins went off.

Not a beep. Not a siren. This time they made a sound like a spaceship. VWOOOOOM. VWOOOOOM.

Tim fell out of bed. Lindsey turned on the light. Down the hall, three doors flew open, and three girls ran in wearing pajamas. Paisley had her crown on. Lainey had one sock on. Sydney had her notebook and a flashlight, as usual.

Tim pressed his pin. Tony popped up in the air, glowing blue. He was wearing a bathrobe and holding a cup of coffee.

"Sorry to wake you," said Tony. "Again. Jarvis saw something fall out of the sky about an hour from your house. Something big. It wasn't a plane. It wasn't a satellite. It came from really, really far away."

"How far?" asked Sydney.

"Like, a different-galaxy far," said Tony.

"It might be dangerous," said Tim.

"It might be," said Tony. "But whatever it is, it landed in a cornfield. And there are about a million acres of corn out there. I could look for a week." He took a sip of coffee. "Or I could ask Lindsey."

Lindsey was already pulling on her shoes.

The cornfield was huge and dark. The corn stalks were taller than Tim. They rustled and swished in the wind. Tony landed next to the family's car in his Iron Man suit with a soft CLANK.

"Okay," said Tony, looking at all that corn. "Where do we even start?"

Lindsey looked around. She looked at the stars. She sniffed the air.

"Smoke," she said. "Something's burning. The wind is coming from that way." She pointed. Then she shined her flashlight on the ground. "And look. Some of the corn is pushed over. In a long line. Like something skidded."

They followed the line of flattened corn. It went on and on. Then, all at once, the corn opened up into a big circle of squished stalks. And in the middle of the circle sat a spaceship.

It was orange and blue and kind of beat up. One wing was bent. Smoke puffed out of the back. On the side, in faded letters, was a name: MILANO.

"Huh," said Tony. "I was expecting something scarier."

Then the door of the spaceship opened with a squeaky hiss.

Out stepped a man in a long red coat. He had shaggy brown hair and a big grin. He was wearing headphones around his neck, plugged into an old-looking music player clipped on his belt.

He looked at the corn. He looked at the stars. He took a deep breath of the night air.

"Earth," he said. "Smells like Earth."

Then he saw the family standing there. And a guy in a red-and-gold metal suit.

"Oh," he said. "Hi. I'm Peter Quill." He paused. "Also known as Star-Lord."

Nobody said anything.

"Star-Lord," he said again. "Legendary outlaw? No?"

"Never heard of you," said Tony.

"Ugh," said Peter. "Nobody ever has."

Behind him, more people came out of the spaceship. And they were not regular people at all.

First came a woman with bright green skin and dark hair with red ends. She moved quickly and quietly, like Natasha, and her eyes were sharp and serious.

Next came a man so big he had to turn sideways to fit through the door. He had gray-green skin covered in swirly red lines, and muscles on top of his muscles.

Next came a raccoon.

A raccoon wearing a little orange jumpsuit. And carrying a gun that was bigger than he was.

And last came a tree. A tall, thin, walking tree, with a face made of bark and kind, sleepy brown eyes.

The girls stared. Their mouths hung open.

"A RACCOON!" yelled Lainey.

The raccoon spun around and pointed his giant gun at her. "I am NOT a raccoon!"

"Rocket," said the green woman. "Put that down. She's a child."

Rocket grumbled and lowered the gun. "She started it."

"What ARE you, then?" asked Sydney, holding up her notebook, ready to draw.

Rocket puffed out his chest. "I'm the best pilot and the best engineer in the whole galaxy, that's what I am."

"But you look like a raccoon," said Paisley gently.

"I KNOW what I look like," said Rocket. Then he looked at Paisley's crown. "Nice crown, though."

Paisley smiled. "Thank you."

The green woman stepped forward. "I'm Gamora," she said. "The big one is Drax. And this is Groot."

The tall tree bent down very slowly until his bark face was right in front of Paisley.

"I am Groot," he said, in a deep, slow, rumbly voice.

"Hi, Groot," said Paisley. "I'm Paisley."

"I am Groot," said Groot again.

Paisley tilted her head. "Oh! You like my crown too? Thank you!"

Everyone stared at her.

"You understood that?" said Peter.

"He said he likes my crown," said Paisley, like it was obvious. "Didn't you hear?"

"He said 'I am Groot,'" said Tony.

"He always says 'I am Groot,'" said Rocket. "It's the only thing he can say. But it means different things. I'm the only one who understands him." He squinted at Paisley. "Usually."

Groot reached up with one of his twiggy hands. A tiny white flower bloomed right on the tip of his finger. He picked it and tucked it into Paisley's crown.

"I am Groot," he said softly.

"You're welcome," said Paisley.

The big gray-green man, Drax, stepped forward and looked at Tim.

"You," said Drax in a loud, flat voice. "Are you the leader of these small humans?"

"I'm their dad," said Tim. "So, yes. Kind of."

"Tony told us you might come," said Lindsey politely. "We're here to help."

"Then you must help us quickly," said Drax. "We are being chased by a very bad person. He wishes to destroy a whole planet. I do not like him. I would like to hit him very hard."

"Okay," said Tim. "That's... a lot of information all at once."

"I am always very honest," said Drax. "It is my best quality. For example, the small one with the pencil has a very large head for her body."

Sydney gasped. "I do NOT!"

"It is a compliment," said Drax. "A large head means a large brain."

Sydney thought about that. "Okay," she said. "That's fine, then."

They all sat on the edge of the spaceship ramp under the stars, and the Guardians explained.

Peter reached into his coat and pulled out a silver ball. It was about the size of a softball. It was covered in metal plates and little lines.

"We call it the Orb," said Peter. "I found it. Well, kind of stole it. Well, kind of borrowed it, from a really old temple. I was going to sell it for a lot of money."

"Peter," said Gamora.

"I'm getting to that part," said Peter. "Turns out, a lot of people want this thing. Like, a LOT of people. Scary people."

"What's inside?" asked Sydney.

Gamora took the Orb. She pressed two of the metal plates at the same time. Click. The Orb slid open like a flower.

Inside was a small stone, glowing bright purple.

The purple light was so strong it made the corn stalks around them shake. Everyone's hair stood up a little, like before a thunderstorm. Tony's suit started beeping.

"Whoa," said Tony. "Jarvis says that thing has more power than... well, than anything Jarvis has ever measured."

"It's called the Power Stone," said Gamora. "If anyone touches it with their bare hands, it would be too much power for them. Like grabbing a bolt of lightning."

Lindsey turned her map over to the back side. She looked at Gamora. "Is it an Infinity Stone?"

Gamora turned and stared at her. "How do you know about the Infinity Stones?"

Lindsey held up the map. Gamora read the list. 1. Space Stone (blue). 2. Reality Stone (red). The question mark at the bottom.

Gamora's face went very still.

"You have been close to two stones already," she said quietly. "Maybe three." She closed the Orb. Click. "Then you need to know who will come looking for them."

"Who?" asked Paisley.

Gamora took a long breath. "My father."

Everybody waited.

"His name is Thanos," said Gamora. "He is very big. And very strong. And very, very sure that he is always right. When I was a little girl, he took me to live with him. He taught me to fight. He told me I was his favorite. But he never once asked me what I wanted."

Paisley scooted closer to her.

"Thanos wants all six Infinity Stones," said Gamora. "He wants to put them in a giant golden glove. With all six stones in that glove, he could do anything he wanted. He could change the whole universe with one snap of his fingers. And he thinks he's the only one who knows what's best for everyone."

"That sounds like a terrible plan," said Paisley. "That sounds like the Hydra computer's plan."

"It is," said Gamora. "It's the worst plan in the universe."

Lindsey flipped her map over to the front. Under LOKI = TROUBLE, and FIND BUCKY, she wrote in big letters:

THANOS = WANTS ALL SIX STONES.

She underlined it three times.

"And the guy chasing you?" asked Tony.

"Ronan," said Drax, and he made a face like he'd eaten something sour. "He works for Thanos. For now. He wants the Power Stone so he can destroy a planet called Xandar. Xandar is a peaceful planet. Millions of people live there. Ronan hates them for no good reason."

"So what do we do with the stone?" asked Tim.

Peter shrugged. "Sell it to the highest bidder and get out of town?"

Everyone looked at him.

"Kidding," said Peter. "Mostly kidding."

Tim stood up. "Peter," he said. "My friend Steve taught me something. A good captain doesn't do what's easy. He does what's right. Even when it's hard."

"Who's Steve?" said Peter.

"Captain America," said Lainey.

Peter stared. "Captain AMERICA? Like the guy on the old lunch boxes? He's real?"

"He comes to our house for spaghetti," said Paisley.

Peter opened his mouth. Then he closed it. Then he opened it again. "Okay. Fine. What's the right thing?"

"Xandar has a police force," said Gamora. "They're called the Nova Corps. They have a vault. The strongest vault in the galaxy. If we give the stone to them, they can lock it away where Ronan can't reach it."

"Then that's what we do," said Tim.

Peter sighed a huge sigh. "Fine. FINE. We're going to Xandar." He looked at the family. "And you're coming with us, aren't you?"

"We'll help you find the way," said Lindsey, holding up her map.

"Lady, that map's for Earth," said Rocket. "Space is a little bigger."

Lindsey smiled. "Then I'll make a new one."

Tony stepped up. "I can't go. Somebody has to keep an eye on Earth while you're gone." He knelt down so he was eye to eye with Rocket. "But if my family's going into space, they need to be able to call home."

He held out his hand. In it was Tim's star pin.

Rocket picked it up and looked at it. He turned it over. He sniffed it. "Cute little gadget. Range is way too short, though. Out in space it won't reach past the moon."

"Can you fix it?" asked Sydney.

Rocket looked at her. "Can I fix it? Kid, I can fix anything." He paused. "But I could use an assistant. Somebody with small hands. And a big head. For brains."

Sydney jumped up so fast her notebook flew in the air.

For the next hour, Sydney and Rocket sat on the spaceship ramp with all five star pins laid out in a row. Rocket used tiny tools. Sydney held the flashlight and passed him tiny screws, the tiniest, shiniest ones, just like she had for Tony.

"Not that one," said Rocket. "The one with the little groove."

"This one?"

"Yeah. Good." Twist, twist, twist. "Now the blue wire."

"It's loose!" said Sydney. "Like Tony's boot!"

"Of course it's loose," grumbled Rocket. "Earth wires are always loose."

When they were done, each star pin had a tiny new silver ring around its edge.

"Now these will reach across the whole galaxy," said Rocket. He looked at Sydney. He looked away. "You did good, kid. For a human."

Sydney grinned so big her cheeks hurt.

Flying in the Milano was the most exciting thing the girls had ever done.

The spaceship zoomed up out of the cornfield, past the clouds, past the moon, and into the deep, dark, twinkling space. Earth got smaller and smaller behind them until it was just a little blue marble.

Peter put on his headphones. Then he took them off and plugged his old music player into the ship's speakers. Music filled the whole ship. Old songs, from a long time ago, with drums and guitars and happy singing.

"Are these old songs?" asked Sydney.

"They're Earth songs," said Peter, smiling. "My mom made me this tape when I was a kid. I've listened to it about a million times."

Then Peter started to dance. He wiggled. He spun. He snapped his fingers.

Lainey jumped up and started dancing with him. Then Paisley. Then Sydney. Even Groot swayed back and forth, his branches waving to the beat.

Drax watched them, standing very still with his arms crossed.

"Drax, come dance!" said Lainey.

"I do not dance," said Drax.

"Everybody dances," said Paisley.

"I am very still," said Drax. "So still that I am invisible."

"You're not invisible," said Lainey. "I can see you."

"Then you have very good eyes," said Drax.

Lindsey sat up in the front of the ship with Gamora, looking at the big glowing star map on the screen. It showed planets and moons and swirly clouds of colorful space dust.

Lindsey looked at it very carefully. She took out a fresh page. She started to draw.

"You're copying our star chart?" asked Gamora.

"I'm learning it," said Lindsey. "Every map has a pattern. You just have to find it."

Gamora watched her draw for a long time. "You're very good at that," she said finally.

"So are you," said Lindsey. "At finding things. You found the stone's secret, how to open the Orb. You found your way away from your father."

Gamora looked at her. Something softened in her eyes. "Nobody has ever said that to me before," she said.

Then the ship shook. BOOM!

Alarms went off. The lights turned red.

"We've got company!" yelled Rocket from his seat.

Out the window, a giant dark ship was coming after them. It was huge and black and shaped like a big pointy cross. Smaller ships buzzed out of it like angry bees.

"Ronan," said Gamora.

"He found us!" said Peter. "How did he find us?"

"Never mind HOW," said Rocket. "Hang on to something!"

The Milano zoomed and swerved. Little ships chased them. Rocket flew left, right, up, down. The girls held onto Tim and Lindsey and screamed and laughed at the same time.

"Rocket!" said Lindsey suddenly. "There's a field of space rocks ahead. Big ones. On the map."

"I see it!" said Rocket. "I'm going around!"

"Don't go around," said Lindsey. "Go THROUGH. Look at the map. There's a path. The rocks are spinning, but they always leave a gap on the left side, every few seconds. Like a revolving door."

Rocket looked at her. Then he looked at the map. "Lady, are you crazy?"

"Trust me," said Lindsey.

Rocket grumbled. Then he steered the Milano straight into the field of giant tumbling space rocks.

A huge rock spun right at them. Lindsey said "Left!" Rocket went left. The rock spun past.

"Now down!"

Down.

"Now wait... wait... NOW right!"

Right. The Milano slipped through a gap between two giant rocks just before they bumped together. CRUNCH. Behind them, the little ships chasing them didn't know the path. They had to stop and turn around.

The Milano zoomed out the other side of the rock field. Free.

Rocket slowly turned around in his seat. He looked at Lindsey for a long time.

"Lady," he said. "You can be my navigator anytime."

"Thank you," said Lindsey, folding up her new space map.

The planet Xandar was beautiful. It had sparkling blue oceans and tall white cities with curvy buildings. Little round ships zipped around in the sky. When they landed, people in shiny blue and gold uniforms came running out. These were the Nova Corps.

A tall woman with silver hair and a kind, serious face met them. She was the leader of the Nova Corps.

"You have the Orb?" she asked.

Peter pulled it out of his coat. He hesitated for just a second. He looked at Tim.

Then he held it out. "Here. Lock it up. Lock it up tight."

The leader smiled. "Thank you. You've done a good thing today."

Peter shrugged and looked at his shoes. "Yeah, well. Don't tell anybody."

But right then, the sky over Xandar went dark.

Ronan's giant black ship came roaring out of the clouds. It was even bigger up close. Its shadow covered the whole city.

"He followed us!" said Rocket. "He followed us all the way here!"

And from the bottom of Ronan's ship, a beam of light came down to the ground. And in the beam, floating down like an elevator, came Ronan himself.

He was tall, with dark blue skin and black paint on his face. He wore a long black cape. And he held a hammer even bigger than Thor's.

Before anyone could stop him, Ronan swung his hammer. A wave of power knocked everyone back. The Orb flew out of the Nova leader's hands. It rolled across the ground.

Ronan picked it up. He cracked it open. He pulled out the purple Power Stone.

And he pushed it right into the top of his hammer. CLICK.

His whole body glowed bright purple.

"Now," said Ronan, in a voice that made the windows rattle, "I will touch this stone to Xandar. And Xandar will be gone."

He rose back up into his ship.

"We have to stop him!" said Gamora.

The Nova Corps ships flew up into the sky. Hundreds of little round ships. They linked together, wing to wing, like a giant glowing net. They wrapped around Ronan's ship and held it back.

The Milano flew up after them. Rocket was steering. Everybody else was holding on.

"How do we get inside?" yelled Peter.

"Same way we always do," said Rocket. "We crash."

"WHAT?" yelled Tim.

CRASH!

The Milano smashed right through the side of Ronan's big black ship.

Inside was dark and smoky and full of hallways. Ronan's ship was falling. It tilted and groaned. Everything shook.

"This ship is going down," said Gamora. "We have to find Ronan before it does."

Lindsey looked at the hallways. Dark ones. Twisty ones. Ones that went up. Ones that went down.

"The bridge of a ship is always at the front," she said. "And Ronan would want to stand somewhere high, where he can see everything. Up. And forward." She pointed. "That way."

They ran. They climbed. Drax punched through a door. Rocket blasted through another. And they burst into a big room at the very front of the ship.

Ronan was standing there with his glowing purple hammer.

"You again," he said, and he raised the hammer.

The ship tilted. Everybody slid. Then, with a terrible CRUNCH, Ronan's giant ship crashed down onto the ground of Xandar.

Everything went dark.

When the dust settled, the family was wrapped up in a big ball of branches. Groot had grown his arms and branches all the way around everyone, like a cozy wooden nest. He had kept every single one of them safe.

But Groot himself was hurt. Lots of his branches had snapped. He was getting smaller and smaller, and his eyes were sleepy.

"Groot!" said Paisley. "Groot, are you okay?"

"I am Groot," whispered Groot.

Paisley's eyes filled with tears. "He says he's going to be okay," she told the others. "He says trees grow back. He says he just needs a little nap. And some sunshine."

Rocket wiped his nose with the back of his hand. "Yeah," he said in a rough voice. "That's what he said."

Groot's branches slowly let go. Everyone climbed out of the wreck. Groot had shrunk down until he was tiny, just a little twig with two leaves and a sleepy face. Paisley picked him up very gently and held him in her cupped hands.

Outside, Ronan was climbing out of the ship. His purple hammer was glowing brighter than ever.

"You are too late," he said. "Now Xandar ends."

He started to lower the hammer toward the ground.

And then Peter Quill did the strangest thing anyone had ever seen.

He started to sing.

Loudly. Badly. A silly old song from his tape. And he started to dance. He wiggled his shoulders. He kicked his feet. He spun in a circle.
