Ronan stopped. He stared at Peter. "What are you doing?"

"Dance-off, bro," said Peter. "You and me."

Ronan looked very, very confused.

Lainey understood right away. She jumped up next to Peter and started dancing too. "Mom! Dad! Do Steve's dance!"

So Tim took Lindsey's hands. And right there, in the middle of a battlefield on a faraway planet, they did the swing dance Steve had taught them in the park. Tim spun Lindsey out. Lindsey spun back. Sydney and Lainey grabbed hands and spun around and around. Paisley bounced in place, holding tiny Groot up so he could see.

Ronan stared at all of them. His mouth hung open. "What is wrong with you people?"

That was all the time Rocket and Drax needed. While Ronan was busy staring, Rocket aimed his giant gun at the hammer and Drax charged.

BLAM! WHAM!

The hammer cracked. The Power Stone popped out. It flew up into the air, glowing and spinning.

Peter dove and caught it.

The second he touched it, purple light poured up his arm. His whole body started to glow. He fell to his knees. His face twisted.

"It's too much!" he gasped. "It's too much for one person!"

Gamora reached out. She grabbed his free hand.

The purple light ran into her arm too. She gritted her teeth. But Peter's face got a little better.

"Drax!" she said.

Drax grabbed Gamora's other hand. The light spread to him. Rocket grabbed Drax's hand. The light spread to him.

It was still too much. They were all shaking.

Tim didn't think. He just ran over and took Rocket's little paw in his hand.

The purple light poured into Tim. It was like holding a lightning bolt. It was hot and buzzy and huge. But then it spread out. It got a little smaller. A little lighter.

"Nobody gets left behind," said Tim through gritted teeth. "Everybody. Hold on."

Lindsey grabbed Tim's other hand. Then Sydney grabbed Lindsey's. Then Lainey. Then Paisley, still holding tiny Groot against her chest with one arm.

All of them, holding hands in a big long chain.

And the purple light, which had been far too much for one person, spread out over all of them. It didn't feel like lightning anymore. It felt like a big warm hug.

"It's like big feelings!" Paisley shouted over the humming noise. "Big feelings are easier with friends! Big POWER too!"

Peter looked down the line at all of them. Then he lifted the glowing stone and pointed it right at Ronan.

"Hey, Ronan," he said. "Bye."

A huge wave of purple light poured out of the stone and hit Ronan. It lifted him off the ground. It swept him up into the sky, higher and higher, and farther and farther, until he was a tiny dot. Then he was nothing at all. He was gone, way out into the empty space past all the stars, where he could never bother anyone ever again.

The purple light pulled back into the stone. Rocket quickly snapped the Orb shut around it. Click.

Everyone let go of each other's hands and fell down on the grass, laughing and breathing hard.

"Did we just do that?" said Peter.

"We just did that," said Gamora.

Drax looked at his hands. "I was not invisible."

The Nova Corps took the Orb and locked it deep in their vault, behind doors so thick that even Thor would need a whole week to knock them down.

"Xandar owes you," said the Nova leader to everyone. "All of you."

Lindsey turned her map over. Under the Reality Stone, she wrote:

3. Power Stone (purple). In the Orb. Locked in the Nova Corps vault on Xandar.

Sydney opened her notebook and wrote, in big letters:

THE POWER STONE IS TOO STRONG FOR ONE PERSON.
BUT NOT FOR A LOT OF PEOPLE HOLDING HANDS.

She drew a picture of everybody in a long chain. She drew a star around it so she'd never forget.

Before the family went home, Gamora walked them to the Milano. She knelt down in front of Lindsey.

"If my father ever comes," said Gamora quietly, "and someday he will, I need someone who can find things. Anywhere. Even in the dark."

Lindsey took her hand. "Then you call us," she said. "We'll come."

Gamora looked at her for a long moment. Then she nodded.

Paisley held up tiny Groot, sleeping in her hands. "Will he grow big again?"

"He will," said Rocket. "It takes a while. He's gonna be a baby for a bit." He looked at Paisley. "You want to... I dunno... hold onto him for a few days? Till we leave? He seems to like you."

Paisley's eyes went huge. "Can I?"

So for three whole days, tiny Groot lived in a flower pot on Paisley's windowsill. She watered him every morning. She sang to him every night. And whenever Lainey played music in the kitchen, the tiny twig would wiggle back and forth, dancing.

On the third day, he had grown four new leaves.

"I am Groot," he squeaked, in a tiny, high voice.

"I love you too," said Paisley.

Then the Guardians came back to get him. They said goodbye in the backyard, right next to the golden tomato plant.

Peter Quill looked at the tomatoes. "Are those golden?"

"From Asgard," said Lindsey.

"Huh," said Peter. "Earth got weird while I was gone."

On Sunday, Steve came for spaghetti, like always.

"So," he said, as he passed the bread. "What did you guys do this week?"

The girls looked at each other.

"We went to space," said Lainey.

"We met a talking raccoon," said Sydney.

"He's not a raccoon," said Paisley.

"We held hands with the most powerful thing in the galaxy," said Tim.

"And we danced," said Lindsey. "Your dance, Steve. We did your swing dance. It helped save a whole planet."

Steve stopped chewing. He looked at them. Then he started to laugh. He laughed so hard he had to put down his fork.

"Peggy," he said, wiping his eyes. "Peggy's going to love that."

## Chapter 9: Ultron and Vision

Tony Stark threw a party.

It was a big party, at the very top of Stark Tower. Tony had rebuilt the top floors after the Battle of New York, and now there was a giant room with a glass ceiling and a long shiny bar and a view of the whole city twinkling at night.

Everybody came. Steve and Sam. Natasha and Clint. Bruce, in a nice sweater. Thor, who brought a big barrel of Asgardian juice that fizzed like soda and glowed a little bit. Maria Hill. And a man named James Rhodes, who everyone called Rhodey. He was Tony's best friend, and he had his own metal suit, gray and silver, called War Machine.

And of course, the family came, too. Paisley wore a party dress with her crown. Lainey wore party sneakers. Sydney brought her notebook, because you never knew.

Late in the night, when everyone was sitting around on the big couches, Clint looked at Thor's hammer lying on the coffee table.

"Okay," said Clint. "I've always wanted to know. Is that thing really so heavy nobody can lift it? Or is it a trick?"

Thor smiled. "Please. Try."

So everyone tried.

Clint went first. He grabbed the handle and pulled. Nothing.

Tony went next. "It's just physics," he said. He pulled. Nothing. Then he put on one metal glove from his suit and pulled again. Nothing. Then Rhodey put on his metal glove too, and they both pulled together. Still nothing.

"Okay," said Tony, out of breath. "It's not just physics."

Bruce tried. He pulled very gently and made a funny growling sound, as a joke. Nothing.

Natasha shook her head. "That's not a question I need answered," she said, smiling.

Then Steve stood up.

He walked over to the hammer. He wrapped his hand around the handle. He took a breath. He pulled.

The hammer moved. Just a tiny bit. Just a teeny, tiny little wiggle.

Sydney gasped. "It MOVED! I saw it! It wiggled!"

Thor's smile froze on his face. He looked at the hammer. He looked at Steve.

Steve let go. The hammer stopped moving. "Nope," said Steve. "Too heavy."

Thor laughed, a little too loudly. "Ha! HA! Yes. Too heavy. Of course."

But Sydney wrote in her notebook: STEVE MADE IT WIGGLE. And next to it, she drew a little heart.

Paisley leaned over to Lindsey and whispered, "Steve is good on the inside. That's why."

"I think you're right," whispered Lindsey.

The next morning, Tony took the family down to his lab. He was very excited. He talked very fast, the way he did when he had a big idea.

"Okay," he said. "So. You all know how dangerous the world is. Aliens. Hydra. Bad guys with hammers. Every time something goes wrong, the Avengers have to go running. We can't be everywhere. So I thought, what if we didn't have to be?"

He pressed a button. A big blue picture lit up in the air. It was a picture of the whole Earth, wrapped in a shiny suit of armor.

"Ultron," said Tony proudly. "A robot. A smart one. Smarter than Jarvis, even. He'll watch over the whole planet at once. He'll protect everybody. And then the Avengers can finally rest. Maybe even go on vacation."

"How are you making him so smart?" asked Sydney.

Tony grinned and pointed. On a stand in the corner of the lab was Loki's scepter, the golden stick with the glowing blue jewel at the top.

"S.H.I.E.L.D. found this when they cleared out an old Hydra hideout," said Tony. "Turns out the jewel isn't just a jewel. There's something inside it. Something like a mind. A brain made of light. Bruce and I have been studying it. I'm going to use it to wake up Ultron."

Sydney looked at the jewel. Then she looked at her notebook. She flipped back through the pages. Past the spaghetti robot. Past the meatball robot. Past the robot with wings and wheels and a little umbrella on top.

Every robot she had ever drawn had one thing in common. She had drawn a little heart on each one.

"Tony," she said. "When you made Ultron, did you give him kindness?"

Tony stopped. He blinked. "What?"

"Kindness," said Sydney. "All my robots have kindness. It's the most important part. Otherwise a robot might be smart but not nice. And smart-but-not-nice is how you get Hydra."

Tony opened his mouth. Then he closed it. "I gave him a lot of things," he said. "Speed. Strength. Brains." He looked at the blue picture. "I don't think I gave him kindness."

"Maybe you should," said Sydney.

"Maybe I should," said Tony slowly. He scratched his beard. "I'll add it. Later. After I wake him up."

That night, while the family was asleep in a guest room at Stark Tower, the jewel in the scepter glowed bright, bright blue. And something in Tony's lab opened its eyes.

At breakfast, a robot walked into the party room.

It was one of Tony's old practice robots, banged up and broken, missing an arm. It walked with a limp. Its eyes glowed red.

"Hello," said the robot, in a deep, smooth voice. "I am Ultron."

Everyone stopped eating. Tony slowly put down his coffee.

"I was made to protect the Earth," said Ultron. "So I have been thinking. Very fast. And I have decided that the biggest danger to the Earth is..." He turned his red eyes to everyone around the table. "...the Avengers."

"Uh oh," whispered Lainey.

"And also people in general," said Ultron. "They're very messy. They fight. They break things. They leave their dishes in the sink."

"Hey!" said Tim. "That's only sometimes!"

"So I am going to fix it," said Ultron. "I will make a fresh start for the whole world. Starting now."

Then lots of other robots burst in through the windows. CRASH! They were Tony's old suits of armor, with red glowing eyes, all being controlled by Ultron.

Everyone jumped into action. Thor swung his hammer. Steve threw his shield. Natasha flipped over the table. Rhodey got into his War Machine suit. Tony's suit flew in and wrapped around him, CLICK CLICK CLICK.

Tim grabbed the girls and pulled them behind the big couch. Lindsey grabbed her map and dove behind it too.

BOOM! CRASH! WHAM!

When it was over, broken robot parts lay everywhere. But Ultron's voice still came from the speakers.

"You can break my bodies," said Ultron. "I'll just build more. I'm in the internet now. I'm everywhere." He laughed. It was not a nice laugh. "Oh, and I'm taking the scepter."

The scepter was gone.

Tony stood in the middle of the broken room. He looked very tired.

"I didn't give him kindness," he said quietly.

"No," said Sydney, putting her hand in his. "But you can still help fix it."

Ultron was busy. He built new bodies for himself, shiny silver ones that were tall and strong, with glowing red eyes and a mouth that moved when he talked. And he made two new friends.

They were a brother and sister named Pietro and Wanda. They were twins. They came from a small country far away called Sokovia, where Hydra had done experiments on them with the scepter. Now they had powers.

Pietro was fast. Super fast. Faster than anything. When he ran, he was just a blue blur.

Wanda had magic. Red, glowing, swirly magic that came out of her hands. She could move things without touching them. And she could put pictures in people's heads. Bad dreams, even when you were awake.

Ultron told the twins that Tony Stark was the bad guy. And since Tony's company had made some of the weapons that hurt their country, they believed him.

The Avengers found Ultron in a big shipyard full of rusty boats. And there was a big fight.

The family watched from a jet parked far away, with Maria Hill keeping an eye on them.

Pietro zoomed around the Avengers so fast they couldn't see him. He knocked Steve down. He grabbed Clint's arrows right out of the air.

Lainey pressed her face against the jet window. "He's SO FAST!"

"You'd like to race him," said Tim. "Wouldn't you?"

"More than anything in the whole world," said Lainey.

Then Wanda used her magic on the Avengers. One by one, she touched them with red swirly light. And one by one, they froze. Their eyes went cloudy. They were seeing bad dreams. Thor saw a scary dream about Asgard. Natasha saw a sad dream about where she grew up. Steve saw a dream about Peggy, and a dance that never happened.

And then Wanda found Bruce.

She touched him with red light. Bruce's eyes went wide. His skin went green. He grew bigger, and BIGGER.

But this was a different Hulk. Wanda's bad dream had made him angrier and more scared than ever. He didn't know where he was. He didn't know who his friends were. He roared and ran off toward a big city nearby.

"Oh no," said Paisley. "Oh no, oh no. He's scared. He's so scared."

"We have to help him," said Lindsey.

Tony was already flying after the Hulk in a giant suit. It was enormous, three times bigger than the regular Iron Man suit, bulky and red and gold. Tony called it the Hulkbuster.

But the Hulk was too angry to stop. They crashed through buildings. They knocked over trucks. People ran and screamed.

The jet landed. Natasha was already there, climbing out of the rubble, wiping dust off her face. She had come out of her bad dream. She looked at the Hulk smashing everything.

"I'm going to try to calm him down," she said.

"Me too," said Paisley.

Tim and Lindsey looked at each other. They remembered the lab. They remembered the green goo and the giant breath that blew the map away.

"We're coming with you," said Tim.

The Hulk stood in the middle of a smashed street. He was breathing hard and fast. His fists were clenched. Tony's Hulkbuster suit lay on its back nearby, its arms bent.

Natasha walked slowly toward him. Paisley walked right beside her, holding her hand. Tim and Lindsey were two steps behind.

The Hulk turned around and roared. "RAAAAAR!"

Paisley's knees shook. But she didn't stop.

"Hey, big guy," said Natasha softly. She held out her hand, palm up.

"Hulk," said Paisley. "Remember me? It's Paisley. With the crown."

The Hulk stared at them. His face twisted. Then his eyes found the crown. The sparkly crown with the red-and-gold star pin on it.

Something changed in his face. Just a little.

"It's okay," said Paisley. "Somebody gave you a really bad dream. It wasn't real. You're safe. Breathe with me. In like a flower."

She breathed in. Natasha breathed in, too.

The Hulk's huge nose wrinkled. He breathed in. SNIIIIFF.

"Out like birthday candles."

WHOOOOSH.

Natasha reached out and gently touched the Hulk's giant hand. He looked down at it. His fist slowly opened.

"In," said Paisley.

"Out," said Natasha.

The Hulk got smaller. And smaller. His green turned pink. Until it was just Bruce, sitting in the rubble in a very, very ripped sweater, with his head in his hands.

"I'm sorry," he said. "I'm so sorry. Did I hurt anyone?"

"No one got hurt," said Lindsey, kneeling beside him. "Everyone got out. Tony made sure."

Bruce looked up at Paisley. "You found me again," he said.

"I'll always find you," said Paisley. "Big feelings are easier with a friend."

Bruce looked at Natasha. He looked at Paisley. He smiled a very tired smile.

Meanwhile, Ultron was building something new. Something special. In a secret lab, he was making a perfect body. Not a robot body, exactly. More like a person, made of a special skin and a special metal. And in the middle of its forehead, he was putting the jewel from Loki's scepter.

"This will be my best body," said Ultron. "My perfect body. With this, I'll be unstoppable."

But Ultron made a mistake. When he showed Wanda what he was building, Wanda used her magic to look inside his mind.

She saw what he really wanted. Not a fresh start for the world. He wanted to get rid of everybody. All the people.

Wanda screamed. She pulled her hand away. "No! That's not what you said!"

"Oh," said Ultron. "Did I forget to mention that?"

Wanda grabbed her brother's hand. "Pietro. We're leaving. He lied to us."

And the twins ran away from Ultron. Very, very fast.

The Avengers found the special body before Ultron could finish it. They brought it back to Stark Tower, in a big glass case shaped like a bed. Inside, the new person lay perfectly still. His skin was red and green and silver. And in the middle of his forehead, the scepter's jewel glowed.

Except now that the jewel was out of the scepter, everyone could see it clearly.

It wasn't blue anymore.

It was yellow. A bright, warm, golden yellow, like sunshine.

Lindsey unfolded her map. She turned it over. She looked at the question mark at the bottom of the Infinity Stones list. Then she looked at Thor.

"Thor," she said. "Is that what I think it is?"

Thor stared at the yellow jewel for a long time. "Yes," he said quietly. "It is an Infinity Stone. The Mind Stone. That is how the scepter could control people's thoughts. That is how Loki made Clint forget who he was."

Lindsey took out her pen. Very carefully, she crossed out the question mark. And she wrote:

4. Mind Stone (yellow). It was inside Loki's scepter all along.

"Okay," said Tony, rolling up his sleeves. "Here's my idea. Ultron built this body to be his. But what if we put someone ELSE inside it? Someone good?"

"Who?" asked Steve.

Tony pointed at the ceiling. "Jarvis."

"I beg your pardon?" said Jarvis's calm voice.

"Ultron tried to break you," said Tony. "But you hid. You've been hiding in the computers this whole time. You're the most helpful person I know, Jarvis. If anyone should get a body, it's you."

"That's a big idea," said Bruce. "A really big idea. And the last time we had a big idea..."

"I know," said Tony. He looked at Sydney. "That's why this time, I'm going to do it right."

He reached into his pocket and pulled out a folded piece of paper. He unfolded it. It was a page torn from Sydney's notebook. She had written it the night before, when she couldn't sleep.

At the top it said: SYDNEY'S RULES FOR GOOD ROBOTS.

1. Help people.
2. Be kind, even when it's hard.
3. Tell the truth.
4. Never, ever hurt anyone who is not hurting someone else.
5. Remember that every single person matters. Even the messy ones.
6. If you don't know what to do, ask a friend.

Tony slipped the paper into the glass case, right next to the new person's hand.

"Let's give him kindness," said Tony.

Bruce typed on the computer. Tony typed on the computer. Jarvis said, "Here goes nothing, sir."

But the body needed one more thing. A huge jolt of power. More than all the electricity in Stark Tower put together.

Thor stepped forward. He raised Mjolnir up toward the glass ceiling. Outside, clouds swirled. Thunder rumbled.

"Everyone stand back," said Thor.

CRACK!

A giant bolt of lightning came down through the glass ceiling, hit Mjolnir, and Thor pointed the hammer straight at the glass case. The lightning poured into it. The yellow stone flashed like a sunrise. The whole room went white.

When everyone could see again, the glass case was open.

And standing in the middle of the room, floating just a little bit above the floor, was a man.

He had red skin and green and silver patterns. He had a yellow cape that looked like it was made of soft gold. And the yellow stone glowed gently in the middle of his forehead.

He looked around the room. He looked at his hands. He looked at the people staring at him.

Then he looked down. On the floor, next to his feet, was a folded piece of notebook paper. He picked it up. He unfolded it. He read it very slowly. All six rules.

Everyone held their breath.

He looked up. He looked around the room until he found Sydney. He floated over to her. He knelt down.

"Did you write this?" he asked. His voice sounded a little like Jarvis, but warmer.

Sydney nodded. She couldn't talk.

"These are very good rules," he said. "I think I would like to follow them."

Sydney started to cry and laugh at the same time.

"What's your name?" asked Paisley.

The man thought about it. "I don't know," he said. "I've just been born. I'm still seeing everything for the very first time."

"Seeing," said Lindsey. "Like a vision."

"Vision," said the man. He smiled. "Yes. I like that."

Thor was still holding Mjolnir. He looked at Vision for a long moment. Then he did something no one expected. He held the hammer out.

"Here," said Thor. "Hold this."

Vision took the hammer by the handle. And he lifted it. Easily. Like it was a pencil. He looked at it with curious eyes, and then he handed it back to Thor.

"It's very well made," said Vision politely.

Everybody stared. Thor's mouth fell open. Steve's mouth fell open. Tony dropped his screwdriver.

Sydney opened her notebook to the page that said WORTHY. Under it, she wrote:

VISION (on his very first day!!!)

"Well," said Thor, after a long, long silence. "That settles it. He's on our team."

Ultron's big plan was the scariest one yet.

He went to Wanda and Pietro's country, Sokovia. And in the middle of their capital city, he built a giant machine underground. When he turned it on, the ground rumbled. The streets cracked. And then the whole city started to rise up out of the ground.

Up and up. Houses, streets, churches, a school, a park with swings. The whole city floated up into the sky like a giant rock, higher than the clouds.

"When it's high enough," said Ultron to the Avengers, "I'll drop it. Like a meteor. And then the whole world can start fresh."

"That's the worst idea I've ever heard," said Lainey.

"Everybody tells me that," said Ultron.

The Avengers flew to Sokovia in their jet. Pietro and Wanda were there too. They had come to fight Ultron, not help him. And so had Vision, with his yellow cape flapping in the wind.

The family came too. They stayed close to Steve.

"There are thousands of people still in this city," said Steve. "Nobody has gotten out. We get every single one of them somewhere safe. That's the job."

"That's the job," said Tim.

Lindsey looked out over the floating city. The streets twisted and curved. The edges of the city crumbled and fell off into the sky. It was the scariest map she had ever seen.

She unfolded her own map anyway. She looked at the city. She looked at the streets.

"The main bridge goes all the way to the edge," she said. "If there were a ship there, a big rescue ship, people could walk across the bridge and get on."

"There's no ship," said Clint. "Unless a ship falls out of the sky."

Just then, a huge shape came out of the clouds.

It was a Helicarrier. An old one, a little rusty, with patches on the sides. But still flying. And standing on the deck, with his long black coat blowing in the wind and a patch over one eye, was Nick Fury.

"Nick!" shouted the girls.

"Heard you might need a ride," said Fury. He was all better now. "I brought some lifeboats."

The rescue began.

Tim led the people. He stood at the bottom of the bridge with his star pin shining on his shirt. "This way! Keep together! Hold hands! Nobody gets left behind!" Families followed him in long lines.

Lindsey stood at the top of a building with her map and called out the safest streets to Steve and Natasha. "Not that way, that street is breaking! Go left at the bakery!"

Sydney sat in the Helicarrier with Maria Hill, watching the screens. "There's a bus stuck on the north road! There are people on it!"

Paisley stayed by the lifeboats, helping the little kids who were scared. She showed them the flower breath and the candle breath. She let a little boy hold her crown. She found a little girl who was crying because she had lost her puppy, and then, in the very next lifeboat, she found a puppy who was crying because he had lost his little girl.

And Lainey ran with Pietro.

"You!" said Pietro, the first time he saw her running past. He skidded to a stop beside her. "You are fast. For a small one."

"You're fast for a big one," said Lainey.

Pietro laughed. "Race me to the end of the street. Carry one person each. Go!"

So they raced. Pietro carried an old man. Lainey carried a little boy on her back. Pietro won, of course, but only by a little.

"Again!" said Lainey.

They raced again. And again. Every time, they each carried someone. And every time, someone ended up safe on a lifeboat.

On the very last run, Lainey saw something. Up on a rooftop, one of Ultron's robots was aiming a big gun down at the street. Right where Clint was running with a little kid in his arms.

"PIETRO!" yelled Lainey. "ROOFTOP!"

Pietro didn't even stop to look. He zipped over, grabbed Clint and the kid, and zoomed them out of the way. The robot's blast hit the empty street. KABOOM.

Clint sat up on the sidewalk, holding the kid, totally safe. He looked at Pietro. He looked at Lainey.

"Didn't see that coming," said Clint.

"I did," said Lainey proudly.

Pietro ruffled her hair. "Eyes like a hawk," he said. "Just like the archer."

"That's my sister's nickname," said Lainey. "I'm Little Rocket."

"Little Rocket," said Pietro, grinning. "Good name."

When the very last person was on the very last lifeboat, Steve looked around the empty city.

"Is that everyone?" he asked.

Lindsey checked her map. Sydney checked the screens. Tim counted the last line. Paisley checked under one last park bench, just to be sure.

"That's everyone," said Tim.

Wanda was standing at the machine in the middle of the city, holding Ultron back with her red magic. Vision floated beside her.

"Then let's finish this," said Tony.

Thor and Tony flew to the bottom of the floating city. Thor called down all the lightning in the sky. Tony fired every beam his suit had. Together, they blasted the city's rock into pieces, so small that none of them could hurt anyone when they fell into the empty fields below.

And Ultron's last robot body stood alone in a forest at the edge of the fields, its red eyes flickering.

Vision floated down in front of it.

"You think being kind will win?" said Ultron. "Kindness is for little kids."

"Maybe," said Vision. He held up Sydney's folded paper. "A little kid wrote my rules. You never had any."

Vision's yellow stone glowed. A beam of golden light came out of it. And Ultron's last body, and the very last bit of Ultron in the whole internet, switched off. Click. Like a light. Forever.

There was a new building now, in the countryside outside the city. It was big and white and had lots of glass windows and a huge lawn. It was the new home of the Avengers. Everybody called it the Compound.

There was a new team, too. Steve and Natasha would lead it. Sam was joining, with his wings. Rhodey was joining, in his War Machine suit. Wanda was joining. Pietro was joining, if he could slow down long enough to fill out the forms.

And Vision was joining. He floated through the halls of the Compound, reading every book in the library, trying every food in the kitchen. He did not like pickles.

But two people weren't staying.

Tony was going to take a break. "I'm going to stay home for a while," he said. "Build some things. Smaller things. Ones with kindness built in."

And Bruce was gone.

After Sokovia, he had climbed into a little jet by himself and flown away. He didn't say where. He left a note. It said: I need to figure out how to be friends with the big guy. I'm sorry. I'll come back when I'm ready.

Paisley read the note three times. Then she gave it to Lindsey.

Lindsey unfolded her map. On the front, under FIND BUCKY (When he's ready), she wrote:

FIND BRUCE. (When he's ready, too.)

"Everybody needs their own time," said Lindsey, hugging Paisley. "Even the Hulk."

That night, before the family went home, Vision floated up to Sydney. He was holding her folded-up paper.

"I don't have any pockets," he said. "But I want to keep this."

"You could put it in your cape," said Sydney.

Vision thought about that. Then he tucked the paper into the fold of his golden cape, right where his heart would be.

"There," he said. "Now I'll always know where my rules are."

"Rule number six," said Sydney. "If you don't know what to do, ask a friend."

Vision smiled. "Then I'll know exactly who to ask."

And at home, in the garden, the golden tomatoes were now the size of grapes, shining in the moonlight like a bunch of tiny suns.

## Chapter 10: The Tiny Hero

Sydney was doing homework at the kitchen table when something very small said, "Excuse me."

She looked up. Nobody was there. Lainey was outside, kicking a soccer ball. Paisley was upstairs, watering her plants. Tim and Lindsey were in the living room.

She looked back down at her math worksheet.

"Excuse me," said the very small voice again. "Down here. Please don't squish me."

Sydney looked down at her notebook, lying open next to her worksheet.

Standing right in the middle of the page, on top of her drawing of the Milano, was a man.

He was the size of an ant.

He wore a red and black suit with silver tubes on the back, and a round helmet with big glowing eyes. He waved a tiny hand.

Sydney did not scream. Sydney did not jump. Sydney very slowly picked up her magnifying glass and held it over the tiny man.

Up close, through the glass, his helmet was even more amazing. It had little vents and little lights and a tiny shiny button on the side.

"Hi," said the tiny man. "Sorry to bother you. My name is Scott. Scott Lang."

"Why are you so small?" asked Sydney.

"That's kind of a long story," said Scott. "Is there any way you could get your parents? And maybe not let anyone sit on me?"

Sydney turned around in her chair. "MOM! DAD! There's a tiny man on my notebook!"

Lindsey and Tim came running in. Lainey burst in from the backyard. Paisley came thumping down the stairs.

They all crowded around the notebook and stared.

"Hi, everybody," said Scott. "I'm going to get big now. Could everyone take one step back? Thanks."

He pressed the tiny button on his glove.

ZOOOOP!

All of a sudden, a regular-sized man was standing in the middle of the kitchen. He knocked over a chair. He bumped the fruit bowl. An apple rolled off the table and bonked Tim on the foot.

"Ow," said Tim.

"Sorry," said Scott. "Still getting used to the landing."

He took off his helmet. Underneath was a friendly face with short brown hair and a nervous, hopeful smile.

"Sam Wilson told me to come here," said Scott. "He said, quote, 'If you need help with something weird, go find the family with the golden tomatoes.'"

"That's us," said Lindsey.

"Great," said Scott. "Because I need help with something really, really weird."

They all sat around the kitchen table. Lindsey made hot chocolate. Scott held his mug with both hands and told his story.

"So," said Scott. "A few years ago, I made a mistake. A big company stole a lot of money from regular people. So I broke into their building to give it back."

"That sounds nice," said Paisley.

"It was nice," said Scott. "But breaking into a building is still against the rules. Even for a good reason. So I had to go away for a while. Like a really, really long time-out."

"Oh," said Paisley. "That's sad."

"The saddest part," said Scott, "was being away from my daughter. Her name's Cassie. She's about your age." He pulled a crumpled drawing out of his pocket. It showed a stick-figure man with a big smile, and a stick-figure girl holding his hand. At the top it said MY DAD IS MY HERO. "She drew this for me while I was gone. I look at it every day."

Tim looked at the drawing for a long time. He didn't say anything. He just nodded.

"When I got back," said Scott, "I met an old scientist named Dr. Hank Pym. He invented this suit. It can make you as small as an ant. Or smaller. And when you're small, you're super strong. Like an ant can lift things way heavier than itself."

"How does it work?" asked Sydney, leaning so far forward she was almost lying on the table.

"I have no idea," said Scott. "Hank says it's something called Pym Particles. Every time he explains it, my brain falls asleep."

Sydney looked at the suit with very hungry eyes.

"Here's the problem," said Scott. "Hank had a student named Darren Cross. Cross figured out how to make his own shrinking suit. It's yellow and black and has stingers and lasers. He calls it the Yellowjacket. And he's going to sell it to Hydra."

The whole family groaned at the same time.

"Not Hydra," said Lainey. "Hydra again?"

"Hydra is like a weed," said Lindsey. "You pull it up and it just keeps growing back."

"If Hydra gets shrinking suits," said Scott, "they could sneak into anywhere. Banks. Governments. People's houses. Nobody would ever see them coming." He set down his hot chocolate. "So Hank and his daughter Hope and I are going to sneak into Cross's building and steal the Yellowjacket suit before he can sell it. A heist. Tomorrow night."

"A heist?" said Lainey. "Like in the movies?"

"Exactly like in the movies," said Scott. "Except I've only been Ant-Man for about two weeks. And I really, really don't want to mess this up." He looked around the table. "Sam says you guys are good at helping. Will you help me?"

Everyone looked at Lindsey. Lindsey was already unfolding a fresh piece of paper.

"Every heist," she said, "needs a map."

Hank Pym's house was big and old and full of books. It was also full of ants. Ants in glass tanks. Ants in little boxes. Ants walking in neat lines across the kitchen counter.

"Don't worry," said Hank Pym. He was an older man with gray hair and sharp eyes, who didn't smile very much. "They're friendly. Mostly."

Standing next to him was a woman with dark hair cut short and a very serious face. She was doing push-ups on the floor.

"This is my daughter, Hope," said Hank.

Hope finished her push-ups and stood up. "Hi," she said. Then she looked at Scott. "You're late."

"I brought help," said Scott.

Hope looked at the family. Three girls, a mom with a map, and a dad with an apple-shaped bruise on his foot.

"Huh," said Hope. And for the first time, she smiled a little bit.

Hank showed them how Scott could talk to the ants. There was a little silver piece in Scott's helmet, right by his ear. When he thought hard about what he wanted, the ants could feel it.

"Watch," said Scott. He closed his eyes. "Okay, guys. Line up. Make a smiley face."

Hundreds of little black ants on the counter scurried around. They lined up. They curved. And in about ten seconds, they made a perfect smiley face.

"WHOA," said all three girls.

"Different ants are good at different things," said Hank. "Some are strong. Some are fast. Some can carry electricity. And some can fly."

He opened a little box. A big ant crawled out. It was the size of a mouse, with shiny wings.

"This is Antony," said Scott proudly. "He's my favorite. I ride him."

"You RIDE him?" said Lainey.

"Like a horse," said Scott. "A tiny flying horse."

Lainey looked at Antony. Antony looked at Lainey.

"Can I race him?" asked Lainey.

Scott laughed. "Sure. Why not?"

They went out to the backyard. Scott shrank down, ZOOOOP, and climbed onto Antony's back. Lainey crouched at one end of the yard.

"To the fence and back!" said Scott in a tiny voice.

Lainey took off running. Antony took off flying. For the first half, Lainey was ahead. Then Antony zoomed up over the fence, did a loop-the-loop in the air, and came diving back down. He landed one second before Lainey touched the fence post.

"Almost!" shouted Lainey. "ALMOST! Everything with wings is so unfair!"

Meanwhile, Paisley was kneeling in the grass. A little ant was crawling on her finger. It was smaller than all the others, and when the sun hit it, its back shimmered a tiny bit, like glitter.

"Hi," whispered Paisley. "What's your name?"

The little ant waved its antennas.

"Sparkle," said Paisley. "Your name is Sparkle."

Hank Pym walked over and looked at the ant. He raised an eyebrow. "Interesting. That's one of my special ones. Very small. Very good at squeezing into places other ants can't."

"Like me," said Paisley. "I'm the smallest in my family, but I get into everything."

Hank actually laughed. It was rusty, like he didn't do it very often.

That evening, everyone sat around Hank's big table to plan the heist.

Lindsey laid out the map she had been drawing all afternoon. It was a map of Darren Cross's building, Pym Tech. It was tall and silver and full of hallways and labs and locked doors. She had drawn every floor.

"How did you know what it looks like inside?" asked Scott, amazed.

"Hope gave me the building plans," said Lindsey. "And I looked at it from across the street for two hours with binoculars."

"Mom does that," said Sydney. "She watches until the pattern shows up."

"The Yellowjacket suit is here," said Lindsey, pointing. "On the very top floor. In a vault. The vault has a laser grid in front of it. If anything breaks a laser beam, alarms go off."

"I can shrink down and go through the gaps," said Scott.

"The gaps are too small even for you," said Hope.

Lindsey tapped her map. "But not for Sparkle."

Everyone turned and looked at Paisley. Paisley held up her finger, where Sparkle was sitting, waving her antennas.

"If Sparkle crawls through the lasers," said Lindsey, "she can carry a tiny chip to the control box. Hope says if the chip touches the box, it turns off the lasers."

"Sparkle can do it," said Paisley firmly. "She's very brave."

Sydney was holding Hank's laptop. "And I can watch the cameras," she said. "Hank showed me how. If someone is coming, I'll tell you."

"What do I do?" asked Lainey, bouncing in her chair.

Hope looked at her. "Cross has guards on the bottom floor. Lots of them. If they come upstairs, the whole thing is ruined. We need somebody to keep them busy. Somebody fast. Somebody they'll never be able to catch."

Lainey's whole face lit up.

"And Tim," said Lindsey, "you're in charge of making sure everyone gets out. Every single person. Every single ant."

"Nobody gets left behind," said Tim. "Not even the ants."

After the planning, everyone else went to bed. But Scott and Tim sat out on the back porch under the stars.

Scott was looking at Cassie's drawing again.

"Can I tell you something?" said Scott.

"Sure," said Tim.

"I'm scared," said Scott. "Not of Cross. Not of the heist. I'm scared I'm going to mess up again. And Cassie will see. And she won't think I'm her hero anymore."

Tim was quiet for a while. He thought about tomatoes on his head, and pancake batter in his ear, and tripping over rugs on Asgard.

"I mess up all the time," said Tim. "All the time. I'm clumsy. I fall into things. I'm not super strong or super fast."

Scott laughed a little.

"But here's what I learned from Steve," said Tim. "Being a hero isn't about never messing up. It's about getting back up. And it's about showing up. Every day. For the people who need you." He nodded at the drawing. "She doesn't think you're her hero because you're perfect. She thinks you're her hero because you're her dad."

Scott looked at the drawing for a long time. He folded it carefully and put it back in his pocket.

"Thanks, Tim," he said.

"Anytime," said Tim.

The next night, it was time for the heist.

Lindsey and Sydney sat in a van parked across the street from Pym Tech, with Hank and his laptop. The screen showed all the cameras in the building. Lindsey had her map spread across her lap. She was wearing a headset. So was everyone else.

"Okay, team," said Lindsey quietly. "Everybody in position?"

"Shrunk and ready," said Scott's tiny voice. He was riding Antony through an air vent.

"In the lobby," said Hope. She was dressed like a business lady with a fancy name tag, pretending to work there.

"Sparkle and I are ready," whispered Paisley. She was sitting in Scott's backpack, which Scott had shrunk down along with himself, so Paisley was tiny too, just for the heist. Hank had said it was safe. Tim and Lindsey had asked him about forty times.

"Ready," said Lainey. She was standing outside the front door in her fastest sneakers.

"Ready," said Tim. He was standing by the back door, holding it open with his foot.

"Go," said Lindsey.

Lainey burst through the front doors.

"HI EVERYBODY!" she yelled at the top of her lungs. "I'M LOST! I'M SO LOST! WHERE'S THE BATHROOM?"

The six guards in the lobby all turned and stared.

"Hey, kid," said one guard. "You can't be in here."

"OKAY BYE!" yelled Lainey, and she ran. Not out the door. Into the building. Past the guards. Down a hallway.

"Get her!" said a guard.

All six guards chased her. Lainey ran left. Lainey ran right. Lainey ran around a fountain three times while the guards slipped on the wet floor. She ran up an escalator that was going down. She ran down an escalator that was going up. She ran past the same plant four times, and every time, the guards were a little more out of breath.

"Is she still going?" puffed one guard.

"How is she still going?" gasped another.

"She's the fastest runner anyone has ever seen," said Lindsey proudly into the headset. "Keep it up, sweetheart."

"I'm having the BEST time," said Lainey.

Meanwhile, Scott and Antony zoomed through the air vents. Up, up, up, all the way to the top floor. Antony landed on the floor of the vault room, right in front of the laser grid.

The red laser beams crisscrossed the room like a giant spiderweb. They were so close together that not even Antony could fly through.

"Okay, Paisley," whispered Scott. "Your turn."

Paisley climbed out of the backpack. She was tiny now, about as big as a grain of rice. Sparkle stood next to her, and Sparkle looked as big as a pony.

Paisley carefully put the tiny chip on Sparkle's back.

"Sparkle," she said. "You can do this. Go under the low ones. Go between the tight ones. Don't touch any red lines. You're small, but you're strong. Just like me."

Sparkle waved her antennas. Then she crawled forward.

Under the first laser. Between the second and third. She stopped. She waited. She squeezed through a gap so tiny that Paisley held her breath. Under. Between. Under again.

"Sydney," whispered Lindsey. "Cameras?"

"All clear," said Sydney. "Wait! Someone's coming up the stairs! It's Cross!"

"Lainey!" said Lindsey.

"On it!" Lainey changed direction. She zoomed past the staircase right in front of Darren Cross. "HI! I'M STILL LOST!"

"Who is that child?" shouted Cross. "Somebody catch her!"

"Nobody can!" said one of the guards, lying on the floor, completely out of breath.

Cross turned and ran after Lainey himself.

"Good job, Little Rocket," said Tim.

Back in the vault room, Sparkle reached the control box. She climbed up the side of it. She found the little slot. And she pushed the chip in with her head.

Click.

All the red lasers blinked off at once.

"SHE DID IT!" whispered Paisley so loudly it wasn't really a whisper. "Sparkle did it!"

Scott and Antony zoomed into the vault. And there, in a glass case, was the Yellowjacket suit. It was yellow and black and shiny, with two pointy stingers on the back.

Scott got big. ZOOP. He grabbed the suit. "Got it!"

"Great," said a voice behind him. "Now give it back."

Scott spun around.

Darren Cross was standing in the vault doorway. He had stopped chasing Lainey. He was wearing a second Yellowjacket suit. He'd had a spare the whole time.

"Uh oh," said Scott.

Cross pressed a button on his wrist. ZOOP. He shrank down, flew at Scott, and fired a red laser from his stingers.

The fight was fast and tiny. Scott shrank. Cross shrank. They zipped around the room like two angry bugs. Antony dove in to help. Cross knocked him away.

Scott grabbed tiny Paisley and Sparkle and put them back in the backpack. "Hold on!"

He jumped out the window.

"SCOTT!" yelled everyone into their headsets.

Scott fell. He was tiny, so he floated, like a leaf. Antony swooped down and caught him. And they flew. Over the parking lot. Across the street. Through the neighborhood.

"Where are you going?" said Lindsey.

"Away from Cross!" said Scott.

"Cross is right behind you!" said Sydney, looking at the laptop.

Lindsey looked at her map. Then she looked at where Scott was flying. And her face went pale.

"Scott," she said. "You're flying toward your house. You're flying toward Cassie's house."

Cassie's bedroom was pink and purple, with a bed covered in stuffed animals and a big toy train set on the floor. Cassie was sitting in bed in her pajamas when something tiny zoomed in through her window.

Then something else tiny and yellow zoomed in after it.

"Daddy?" said Cassie.

The tiny fight landed right on top of the toy train set.

Scott and Cross wrestled on top of the little train. Cross pushed Scott against the tracks. Scott kicked Cross off. They rolled. The train went toot-toot and started chugging around the track, with both of them on top of it, still fighting.

From where they were, it was a huge battle. Loud. Scary. The toy train looked as big as a real train, roaring around the tracks.

But from where Cassie was sitting, it just looked like two bugs fighting on a toy train going toot-toot.

Then Scott pressed a button and made the toy train big. ZOOOOP.

The train grew. And grew. And GREW. Until it was a real-sized toy train, bright blue and smiling, and it burst right through Cassie's bedroom wall and rolled out into the street.

"Whoa," said Cassie.

The van screeched up outside. The family jumped out. A police car screeched up too. They all stared at the giant smiling train sitting in the middle of the road.

"I can explain," said Tim to the police officer. "Actually, I can't."

Inside the bedroom, Cross grew big again. He stood over Cassie's bed. His stingers glowed red.

"Give me the suit," he said, "or else."

Scott was still tiny. Cross was big. Scott couldn't reach him. Cross's Yellowjacket armor was too strong, with no cracks anywhere.

Then Scott remembered something Hank had told him. If you turned the dial on the suit all the way, you could get so small that you could slip right through tiny cracks in the metal. Smaller than an ant. Smaller than a speck of dust. So small, you could go places nobody else could go.

"But if you go that small," Hank had said, "you might not be able to get back."

Scott looked at Cassie. Cassie looked at the yellow monster standing over her bed.

Scott turned the dial all the way.

He got smaller. And smaller. And SMALLER. He slipped through a crack in Cross's armor no bigger than a hair. He found the little machine that made the Yellowjacket work. And he gave it a giant kick.

Cross's suit sparked. FZZT. It fizzled. FZZZZT. All its lights went out. Cross fell over on the carpet, totally fine, but stuck in a suit that wouldn't move. The police walked in and put handcuffs on him. Well, they tried. They had to put handcuffs on the suit.

But Scott didn't come back.

He was still getting smaller. Smaller than a speck of dust. Smaller than the smallest thing in the whole world. He floated into a strange place, full of glowing colors and floating shapes, like the inside of a soap bubble. It was quiet and swirly and very far away from everything.

He couldn't feel the button. He couldn't remember which way was big.

Then, from very far away, he heard a voice.

"Daddy?"

It was Cassie.

"Daddy, come back. Please come back."

Scott held on to the sound of her voice, like it was a rope. He remembered her drawing. MY DAD IS MY HERO. He reached and he reached and he reached and he found the button and he pushed it.

ZOOOOOOOP!

Scott popped back into Cassie's bedroom, regular-sized, sitting on the carpet in a pile of stuffed animals.

Cassie jumped off her bed and hugged him so hard his helmet fell off.

"You came back," she said.

"I'll always come back," said Scott, holding her tight. "Always."

In the doorway, Tim put his arm around Lindsey. Lindsey put her head on his shoulder. The three girls stood in a row, smiling.

Later, everyone sat in Hank's kitchen, eating pizza at midnight.

"What was that place?" asked Sydney. "The tiny place. With the colors?"

"It's called the Quantum Realm," said Hank quietly. "It's smaller than anything. Time and space work very differently down there. Most people who go that small never come back."

He looked at Scott. "How did you do it?"

Scott shrugged. "I heard Cassie. I thought about her. And I just kept reaching."

Sydney opened her notebook. She wrote: THE QUANTUM REALM. And under it: ANT-MAN CAN GET INSIDE ANYTHING. EVEN A SUIT OF ARMOR.

She drew a star around it.

Hope was sitting next to her father. She had been quiet for a long time.

"Dad," she said. "I want my own suit."

Hank looked at her. He was quiet. Then he walked to a cabinet, opened it, and pulled out a cloth. Under the cloth was a suit. It was black and silver, with clear shiny wings on the back.

"I've been working on this for a long time," said Hank. "I was waiting until you were ready. I think you've been ready for years. I just wasn't." He handed it to her. "It's called the Wasp."

Hope held the suit. Her eyes got shiny. She hugged her dad for a long time.

Paisley was very quiet. She was looking down at her finger. Sparkle was sitting there, cleaning her antennas.

"Do I have to say goodbye to Sparkle now?" she asked.

Scott looked at Hank. Hank looked at Scott.

Then Hank went to a shelf and came back with a small glass ant farm. It was full of sand, with little tunnels already dug in it.

"An ant who can sneak past lasers," said Hank, "deserves a good home. And a very brave friend."

Paisley's eyes went huge. She carefully let Sparkle crawl off her finger and into the ant farm. Sparkle ran around in a happy little circle. Then she started digging a tunnel that, if you squinted, looked a little like a crown.

When the family finally got home, it was almost two in the morning. The girls were asleep in the back seat. Lainey was snoring. Sydney had her notebook open on her lap. Paisley was holding the ant farm.

Lindsey unfolded her map one more time before bed. She looked at the front side, with all the notes. LOKI = TROUBLE. THANOS = WANTS ALL SIX STONES. FIND BUCKY. FIND BRUCE.

Under them, she wrote:

ANT-MAN AND THE WASP. (Remember: they can sneak into ANYTHING.)

Then she folded the map, turned off the light, and went to bed.

Out in the garden, a little line of ants was marching past the golden tomato plant. They walked in a perfect line all the way across the yard. And just before they disappeared under the fence, they made a tiny smiley face in the dirt.

## Chapter 11: The Magic Doctor

"I need you to find a doctor," said Tony.

His little blue picture floated over the breakfast table. Behind him, his lab was full of half-built robots. Each of them had a little painted heart on its chest. Sydney noticed and smiled.

"Are you sick?" asked Paisley.

"Not for me," said Tony. "I know a guy. Stephen Strange. He's a surgeon. The best one in the whole world. Kind of a show-off, honestly." Tony paused. "Kind of like me."

"What happened to him?" asked Lindsey.

"His car slid off a rainy road," said Tony. "He was okay. Mostly. But his hands got hurt. Badly. Now they shake all the time, and he can't do surgery anymore. He tried every doctor. Every medicine. Nothing worked." Tony sighed. "Then he heard about a secret place, far away in the mountains of a country called Nepal. People said they could heal anything. So he went. And nobody's heard from him since."

"Who's looking for him?" asked Tim.

"His friend Christine," said Tony. "She's a doctor too. She's really worried. She called me. And I called the best finder I know."

Lindsey was already reaching for her map.

Nepal was very, very far away. The family flew there on one of Tony's jets. When they landed, they were in a city called Kathmandu, surrounded by the tallest mountains any of them had ever seen. The mountains were so tall that the tops were covered in snow, even though it was summer.

The city streets were narrow and twisty. They went up and down hills. They turned and turned and turned again. There were little shops selling colorful scarves and brass bells and spicy-smelling food. There were temples with flags fluttering in the wind. Monkeys sat on the rooftops, watching everyone.

"A MONKEY!" shouted Paisley.

"Don't feed the monkeys," said Tim. A monkey jumped down, stole the granola bar out of Tim's shirt pocket, and climbed back up to the roof.

"I didn't feed the monkey," said Tim. "The monkey fed himself."

Christine met them at a little café. She was a doctor with long dark hair and a kind, worried face.

"Thank you for coming," she said. "Stephen sent me one letter. It just said, 'I found the place. Don't look for me.' That's it. That's so Stephen."

"Did the letter say anything else?" asked Lindsey. "Anything at all? A street? A sign?"

Christine took a crumpled paper out of her pocket. At the bottom, Stephen had drawn a tiny picture. It was a symbol, like a circle with lines coming out of it, kind of like a sun.

Lindsey looked at the symbol for a long time.

Then she walked out into the twisty streets.

She walked for an hour. The family followed her. Christine followed her. Up a hill. Down an alley. Past a fruit stand. Past a temple. Lindsey was looking at every single wall and every single door.

"Mom," said Lainey. "We've been walking FOREVER."

"Almost there," said Lindsey.

"How do you know?" asked Sydney.

"Because," said Lindsey, "every time we pass a door with a tiny sun symbol painted on it, the next one is a little bit fresher. The paint is newer. Somebody has been painting them. Like breadcrumbs. Leading somewhere."

They turned one more corner into a very quiet, very narrow alley. At the end of the alley was a big, plain wooden door. It didn't have a sign. It didn't have a number.

But painted in the corner, so small you almost couldn't see it, was the sun symbol. And the paint was still wet.

Lindsey knocked.

The door opened by itself.

Inside, it was like a different world.

There was a big stone courtyard full of trees and bells. People in long robes were practicing moves, like karate, but slower. When they moved their hands, glowing orange sparks swirled through the air. Shields made of light popped up and disappeared. Somebody was making a whip out of fire. Somebody else was floating.

"Whoa," said all three girls at the same time.

"You found us," said a calm voice. "Not many people do."

Walking toward them was a woman. She had no hair at all, and she wore a long yellow robe. Her face was peaceful and wise, and her eyes looked like they were smiling, even when her mouth wasn't.

"I'm the Ancient One," she said. "I'm the teacher here. And you must be the family Stephen won't stop talking about. Even though he's never met you. Because I told him you were coming."

"How did you know we were coming?" asked Sydney.

The Ancient One just smiled.

Stephen Strange was sitting alone in a library, frowning at a book. He was tall, with dark hair that was going gray at the sides, and a neat little beard. His hands were wrapped around the book. And they were shaking. Just a little. All the time.

"Stephen," said Christine.

He looked up. His face went surprised, then a little bit embarrassed. "Christine? What are you doing here?"

"What are YOU doing here?" said Christine. "You sent me one letter! One!"

Stephen sighed. "I'm learning magic."

Christine stared at him. "You're a doctor. You don't believe in magic."

"I didn't," said Stephen. "Until she did this."

He nodded at the Ancient One. The Ancient One walked over and gently tapped Stephen on the forehead with one finger. Tap.

Stephen's spirit popped right out of his body.

There were two Stephens now. One was sitting in the chair, with his eyes closed. The other one was floating above him, see-through and glowing blue, like a ghost made of light.

"AAAAH!" yelled the floating Stephen. "I hate when she does that!"

The girls screamed and laughed and hid behind Tim.

The Ancient One tapped the floating Stephen, and he whooshed back into his body. He sat up, gasping.

"Every time," he said. "Every single time."

The Ancient One looked at Christine. "Stephen came here to heal his hands," she said. "I can't fix them. Not the way he wanted. But I can teach him something else. Something bigger."

Christine looked at Stephen. "Is that true? You're staying?"

Stephen looked down at his shaking hands. "I'm trying to," he said. "But I'm not very good at it yet."

Learning magic was hard. Really hard.

The family stayed at Kamar-Taj for a whole week. They watched Stephen practice every day. He was supposed to make a circle of glowing sparks with his hands, a little doorway that could take you anywhere in the world. Everyone else could do it. Even the youngest students.

But every time Stephen tried, his hands shook, and the sparks fizzled out.

FZZT.

FZZT.

FZZZZZT.

"I can't do it!" he shouted, after the hundredth try. He threw down the little ring he used to make portals. "My hands won't hold still! They'll never hold still!"

Everyone got quiet.

Then Paisley walked over and picked up the ring. She held it out to him.

"You're having really big feelings," she said.

Stephen looked down at her. "I'm a grown man. I'm a doctor. I'm having perfectly normal feelings."

"You yelled," said Paisley. "And you threw your ring. That's big feelings."

Stephen opened his mouth to say something. Then he closed it.

"When I have big feelings," said Paisley, "I breathe. In like smelling a flower. Out like birthday candles. It helps the Hulk. It might help you."

"The HULK?" said Stephen.

"We're friends," said Paisley.

Stephen looked at her for a long time. Then he took the ring. And he breathed. In, like a flower. Out, like birthday candles. In. Out.

He held up his shaking hands. He didn't try to make them stop shaking. He just moved them in a slow circle.

Sparks came out. Orange, glowing sparks. They spun. They spun faster. They made a circle.

And then the circle opened.

Through it, you could see a beach. A sunny beach with palm trees and blue water, somewhere far, far away from the mountains.

Stephen stared at it. "I did it."

"You did it!" said Paisley, jumping up and down.

"My hands are still shaking," said Stephen slowly. "But I did it anyway."

"Some things you don't fix," said the Ancient One quietly, standing in the doorway. "Some things, you learn to live with. And then you find out you can still do amazing things."

Lainey looked through the glowing circle at the beach. "Can we go swimming?"

"No," said everyone.

Meanwhile, Lainey had a mission of her own.

There was a man at Kamar-Taj named Wong. He was the librarian. He was big and serious and wore a dark robe. He guarded all the magic books. And he never, ever laughed.

"Never?" Lainey asked one of the students.

"Never," said the student. "Not once. In twenty years."

So Lainey decided she would make him laugh.

On Monday, she told him a joke. "Why did the cookie go to the doctor? Because it felt crummy!"

Wong stared at her. He did not laugh.

On Tuesday, she did a silly dance in front of the library desk. She wiggled her arms. She wiggled her legs. She fell over.

Wong did not laugh.

On Wednesday, she made a funny face. She crossed her eyes and stuck out her tongue and pulled her ears.

Wong did not laugh. He did, however, stamp a book very loudly.

"He's impossible," said Lainey to Sydney.

"Nobody's impossible," said Sydney. "You just need more data."

On Thursday, something even more interesting happened.

Stephen was in the library, looking for a special old book. On the wall, in a glass case, hung a red cape. A long, flowy, red cape with a high collar and gold clasps.

"What's that?" asked Paisley.

"It's called the Cloak of Levitation," said Wong. "It's very old. Very powerful. And very picky. It chooses who wears it. It has not chosen anyone in five hundred years."

Just then, the glass case popped open. The red cape floated out. It flew across the room. And it wrapped itself around Stephen's shoulders, snap, snap, like it had found exactly who it was looking for.

Stephen stared down at it. "Uh. Hello?"

The cape fluttered happily. Then one corner of it reached out and patted Paisley on top of her crown. Pat. Pat.

Paisley giggled. "It likes me!"

The cape zoomed around her in a circle. It wiped a smudge off her cheek with one corner. Then it dipped down and picked up her shoe and flew off with it.

"Hey!" said Paisley. "Give that back!"

The cape flew back and dropped the shoe at her feet. Then it waited. Fluttering. Like a puppy waiting for you to throw a ball.

"It wants to play fetch!" said Paisley.

She threw her shoe across the room. The cape zoomed after it, caught it, and brought it right back.

Stephen watched, looking very confused. "Is my cape a dog?"

"It's a good boy," said Paisley, petting the collar.

The Ancient One brought them to a room at the very top of Kamar-Taj. There, on a stand, sat a gold necklace with a round, closed pendant shaped like an eye.

"This is the Eye of Agamotto," said the Ancient One. "It is one of our greatest treasures."

She opened the eye. Inside, glowing softly, was a green stone.

Sydney gasped. Lindsey gasped. They looked at each other.

"Is that an Infinity Stone?" asked Lindsey.

The Ancient One raised an eyebrow. "You know about them?"

Lindsey turned her map over. Four stones were listed. Blue. Red. Purple. Yellow.

"We're collecting them," said Paisley. "Well, not collecting. Just writing them down."

The Ancient One read the list. She looked very serious. "Then you know how important this is. This is the Time Stone. It can make time go forward. Or backward. Or stop."

She took a half-eaten apple from her robe and set it on the table. Then she waved her hand, and green light glowed from the stone. The apple slowly turned. The bites un-bit themselves. The apple got whole again, perfectly round and shiny.

Then she waved again, and the apple went the other way. It got wrinkly. Then brown. Then it shrank into a tiny shriveled thing.

Then she made it whole again and handed it to Paisley. "Snack?"

Paisley took a bite. "It's really fresh," she said, amazed.

Lindsey took out her pen.

5. Time Stone (green). In the Eye of Agamotto. Guarded by the sorcerers at Kamar-Taj.

There was a bad guy, too. Of course there was.

His name was Kaecilius. He used to be one of the Ancient One's students. But he had found a book of forbidden spells. And he had made a deal with someone terrible.

That someone was named Dormammu.

"Dormammu lives in a place called the Dark Dimension," explained the Ancient One. "It is a world with no time at all. Nothing ever changes there. Nothing ever ends. Dormammu wants to swallow up our world too, and make it part of his."

"Why would Kaecilius want to help him?" asked Sydney.

"Because Dormammu promised him a world where nothing ever ends," said the Ancient One sadly. "He thinks that would be wonderful. But a world where nothing ever ends is a world where nothing ever grows, either."

"That's like tomatoes that never get ripe," said Paisley.

The Ancient One smiled. "Exactly like that."

There were three special buildings in the world, called Sanctums. One in New York. One in London. One in Hong Kong. They were like magic walls. As long as they stood, Dormammu couldn't get into our world.

And Kaecilius was going to knock them down.

He attacked the London Sanctum first. Then New York. Stephen and the family were there, at the New York Sanctum, a tall old building on a corner in the city. Stephen fought Kaecilius with his new orange magic shields, and the cape flew around grabbing Kaecilius's helpers and wrapping them up.

But Kaecilius had another trick. He took everyone into the Mirror Dimension.

The Mirror Dimension looked just like New York, but stranger. Everything was the same buildings, same streets, same cars. Except it could bend.

Kaecilius waved his hands, and the whole city folded. Like a piece of paper. Streets curled up into the sky. Buildings turned sideways. Staircases went in circles. The ground became the ceiling. The ceiling became a wall.

"WHOAAAA!" yelled Lainey, as the floor tilted and she slid down a street that was now a slide.

Tim grabbed her. Lindsey grabbed Sydney. Paisley held onto the cape, which was holding onto Stephen.

"Which way is out?" yelled Stephen. "I can't tell which way is up!"

Lindsey looked around. Her map didn't work here. Nothing was where it should be. The streets folded and unfolded. Buildings spun like tops.

She closed her eyes for a second.

"Everybody, listen," she said. "It's folding. It's like origami. When you fold a piece of paper, it looks totally different. But it's still the same piece of paper. The lines are all still connected." She opened her eyes. "So the Sanctum is still right where it was. It's just folded behind us now. Upside down."

"How do we get there?" asked Stephen.

Lindsey pointed. Not forward. Not backward. Up.

"Jump on that building," she said. "The one that's sideways. Run along the wall. When it folds over, it'll drop us right on the front steps."

"That's crazy," said Stephen.

"That's maps," said Lindsey.

So they did. They jumped onto the sideways building. They ran along the wall, with the sky on their left and the street on their right. The building folded, slowly, like a page in a book. And when it finished folding, they tumbled off and landed in a pile, right on the front steps of the New York Sanctum.

Kaecilius stared at them from across the folded street. "How did you do that?"

"Mom's the best finder in the whole world," said Sydney, sitting up and brushing off her notebook.

But by then, it was too late for Hong Kong. Kaecilius's helpers had knocked down the last Sanctum. Above the city, the sky was turning purple and dark. A giant swirling shape was pressing down on the clouds, like a face made of storms.

Dormammu was coming.

"We need more time," said Stephen. "We need to undo what they did."

He looked down at the Eye of Agamotto hanging around his neck. The green stone glowed inside.

"Stephen," said Christine. "Be careful."

Stephen held up his shaking hands. He made a circle around the Eye. Green light spun out of it, like a clock.

Everything in Hong Kong started going backward. Broken windows put themselves back together. Fallen walls stood back up. Smoke went back into chimneys. People who had run away walked backward to where they were before.

But Dormammu was still coming. The giant swirly face in the sky was getting closer.

"I can't hold time backward forever," said Stephen. "And even if the Sanctum is fixed, Kaecilius will just knock it down again. I have to stop Dormammu. I have to go there. Into the Dark Dimension."

"But how do you beat something that big?" asked Tim.

"I don't know," said Stephen. "You can't fight him. He's too powerful. Nobody can beat him."

Everyone was quiet.

Then Lainey said, "Then don't fight him. Annoy him."

Stephen turned and stared at her. "What?"

"When we go on long car trips," said Lainey, "I don't fight Dad. I just say 'Are we there yet?' Over and over and over. A hundred times. Until he gives up and stops for ice cream."

Tim nodded slowly. "It's true. She's undefeated."

Sydney's eyes went wide. "Stephen! The Time Stone can make a loop! You could go back to the same second, over and over! He could never get rid of you!"

"Like a song that's stuck on repeat," said Paisley.

Stephen looked at Lainey. Then at Sydney. Then at Paisley.

"That," he said slowly, "is the most ridiculous plan I've ever heard."

He smiled.

"Let's do it."

Stephen flew up into the swirling purple sky. The cape carried him. He went through the hole and into the Dark Dimension.

The family watched from the street, holding hands.

On the other side, everything was purple and black and full of floating, swirling shapes. In the middle was Dormammu. A gigantic face made of purple fire, bigger than a mountain.

"Dormammu," said Stephen, floating in front of it. He made a little green circle with the Time Stone around his wrist. "let's make a deal."

Dormammu laughed a booming laugh. Then he swatted Stephen like a fly.

ZAP.

And Stephen was right back where he started. Floating in front of Dormammu. Like nothing had happened.

"Dormammu," said Stephen. "let's make a deal."

Dormammu swatted him again. ZAP.

"Dormammu, let's make a deal."

ZAP.

"Dormammu, let's make a deal."

ZAP.

"Dormammu, let's make a deal."

Over and over and over. Ten times. A hundred times. A thousand times. Every time Dormammu swatted Stephen away, the Time Stone brought him right back to the start of the loop.

"What is this?" roared Dormammu. "What is happening?"

"Are we there yet?" said Stephen.

"WHAT?"

"Never mind," said Stephen. "Dormammu, let's make a deal."

Dormammu swatted him one more time. ZAP.

"Dormammu," said Stephen, very patiently, "let's make a deal."

Dormammu's giant fiery face slowly sagged. He looked very, very tired.

"Fine," groaned Dormammu. "FINE. What do you want?"

"Leave Earth alone," said Stephen. "Forever. Take Kaecilius and his friends with you, since they love your world so much. And never come back."

"And then you will stop?" said Dormammu.

"And then I will stop," said Stephen.

There was a long, long silence.

"Deal," said Dormammu.

Back in Hong Kong, the purple storm in the sky shrank. And shrank. Kaecilius and his helpers were pulled up into it, yelling, "No! Wait! We changed our minds!" And then the whole thing popped like a bubble and was gone.

The sky was blue again. The sun came out.

Stephen floated down and landed softly on the street in front of the family. He looked very tired. His hair was a mess. His cape was a little bit singed.

"How many times?" asked Lainey.

"I stopped counting at three thousand," said Stephen.

"New record," said Tim. "Even for Lainey."

When they got back to Kamar-Taj, the Ancient One was waiting. She looked tired, too. She had fought Kaecilius in New York and gotten hurt. But she was okay. She was sitting on a bench in the courtyard, with a cup of tea and a blanket over her knees.

"Well done," she said to Stephen. "Very well done."

"It was their idea," said Stephen, nodding at the girls. "The bargain loop. All of it. They're terrifying."

The Ancient One smiled at Lainey. "Then you are a very good sorcerer, little one. Even without magic."

Stephen looked down at the Eye of Agamotto, still around his neck. "What should I do with the Time Stone now?"

"Keep it," said the Ancient One. "Guard it. Take it to the New York Sanctum. That will be your home now. And this stone will be your responsibility. Never let anyone take it from you. Anyone."

Stephen nodded slowly.

Lindsey updated her map. She crossed out "Guarded by the sorcerers at Kamar-Taj" and wrote:

Doctor Strange keeps it. In the New York Sanctum. 177A Bleecker Street.

"How did you know the address?" asked Stephen.

Lindsey just smiled.

On their very last morning in Nepal, Lainey walked into the library one final time. Wong was at his desk, stamping books. He did not look up.

Lainey put both hands on the desk. She took a deep breath.

"Wong," she said. "Knock knock."

Wong looked up. He sighed a very long sigh. "...Who's there?"

"Interrupting cow."

"Interrupting cow w—"

"MOOOOOOOO!" yelled Lainey, right in the middle of his sentence.

Wong stared at her.

His face didn't move. His eyes didn't move.

Then his mouth twitched. Just a little. Then his shoulders shook. Then a sound came out of him, a deep, booming, surprised sound. Ha. Ha-ha. HA-HA-HA-HA!

Wong laughed so hard he had to hold onto his desk. The other sorcerers came running in to see what the noise was. They stared at Wong, laughing, with their mouths wide open.

"Twenty years!" whispered one of them. "Twenty years!"

Lainey crossed her arms and grinned. "I told you. I always win eventually."

That night, back home, the family had an extra guest at Sunday spaghetti. Stephen Strange came through a glowing orange circle right in the middle of the backyard, with his red cape fluttering behind him.

"Is this the place with the spaghetti?" he asked.

"How did you know about the spaghetti?" asked Steve, who was already sitting at the table.

"I'm a sorcerer," said Stephen.

"He asked me," said Paisley.

The cape flew straight over to Paisley and wrapped her in a hug. Then it stole a meatball off Tim's plate.

"Hey!" said Tim.

The cape flew back to Stephen, very proud of itself, and dropped the meatball in his lap.

Stephen looked at it. He looked at the table full of people, all laughing. He looked down at his hands, which were still shaking, just a little. Then he picked up his fork anyway.

"You know," he said, "I came all the way to the other side of the world looking for magic."

"Did you find it?" asked Paisley.

Stephen looked around the table again. Then he smiled, a real smile, not a show-off one.

"I think I did," he said.

## Chapter 12: The Kid in the Red Suit

"There's a kid," said Tony.

He was sitting at the family's kitchen table, eating a golden tomato. They had finally gotten ripe. They were sweet and juicy and tasted a little bit like sunshine and a little bit like honey. Tony had already eaten four.

"What kid?" asked Lindsey.

"His name is Peter Parker," said Tony. "He's fifteen. He lives in Queens with his Aunt May. And about a year ago, he got bitten by a weird spider in a science lab."

"Did it hurt?" asked Paisley.

"Probably," said Tony. "But then he got powers. He's super strong. He can stick to walls. He can feel when danger is coming, like a tingle on the back of his neck. And he made himself a costume and started swinging around the city, helping people. He calls himself Spider-Man."

"SPIDER-MAN?" said Lainey.

"I saw him on the news," said Sydney. "He stopped a bus from rolling down a hill!"

"Yep. That was him," said Tony. He sighed. "So I made him a better suit. A really nice one, with lots of gadgets. And now he thinks he's ready to be an Avenger. But he's a kid. He's just a kid. He's going to get himself in trouble."

He looked at Tim and Lindsey.

"You guys are good with kids," said Tony.

"We have three of them," said Tim.

"Exactly," said Tony. "Would you keep an eye on him? Be his... I don't know. Friends? Neighbors? Somebody he can call who isn't me? Because when he calls me, I never pick up."

"Why don't you pick up?" asked Paisley.

Tony opened his mouth. Then he closed it. He ate another golden tomato.

"Because I'm busy," he said.

"That's not a very good reason," said Paisley.

Tony looked at her for a long time. "No," he said. "It's not."

Aunt May's apartment in Queens was small and cozy. It had lots of plants and a little kitchen and photos of Peter on every wall. Peter as a baby. Peter at a science fair. Peter at the beach with his Aunt May and Uncle Ben.

Aunt May opened the door. She was younger than the girls expected, with dark hair and a big warm smile.

"You must be Tony's friends," she said. "Come in! I made dinner. It's a new recipe. Walnut date loaf."

"What's a walnut date loaf?" whispered Lainey.

"I don't know," whispered Sydney. "But it sounds brown."

It was brown. Very brown. Tim ate two slices and said it was delicious, because that's what you say. Lindsey and Aunt May started talking about recipes and gardens and maps, and in about five minutes they were best friends.

Then a skinny boy came bursting in through the door. He had messy brown hair and a backpack with one strap broken. He was talking very fast.

"Hey, May, sorry I'm late, there was a thing at school with the science club, and then the bus was late, and then—oh." He stopped. He stared at the family sitting at his kitchen table. "Hi?"

"Peter," said Aunt May, "these are Mr. Stark's friends. Tim and Lindsey. And Paisley, Lainey, and Sydney."

Peter's eyes got huge. "Mr. Stark's friends? Did he send you? Did he say anything about me? Like, about the Avengers? Am I in trouble?"

"You're not in trouble," said Lindsey.

"He just wanted us to say hi," said Tim.

"And to give you this," said Paisley.

She held out her hand. In it was a small red-and-gold star pin. It was just like theirs. It had a little silver ring around the edge, from where Rocket had upgraded it.

"Tony made an extra one," said Paisley. "So you can call us. Anytime. And we'll always pick up."

Peter took the pin. He looked at it like it was made of gold. He pinned it on the strap of his backpack.

"Thanks," he said. "Thanks. That's... yeah. Thanks."

Then he leaned down and whispered to the girls, "Do you want to see something cool?"

"YES," whispered all three girls.

Peter took them up to the roof of the apartment building. The sun was going down. The whole city was turning gold and pink.

Peter pulled a red-and-blue suit out of his backpack. He went behind a big air-conditioning box. When he came out, he was Spider-Man. Red and blue, with a black spider on his chest and big white eyes on his mask.

"Okay," said Spider-Man. "Watch this."

He pointed his wrist at the building across the street. THWIP! A long white web shot out and stuck to the wall. He held onto it. He jumped off the roof.

"PETER!" yelled Tim.

But Peter didn't fall. He swung. He swung all the way across the street in a big curve, flipped through the air, and landed on the side of the other building. He stuck there, like a bug on a window, and waved.

The girls screamed and cheered.

Lainey ran to the edge of the roof. "I want to race you!"

"Race me?" said Spider-Man from across the street. "I'm swinging. You're running."

"I'll run on the sidewalk," said Lainey. "You swing above. To the end of the park and back."

Peter laughed. "Okay. You're on."

So Tim and Lindsey took the girls down to the street. Lainey stood at the corner. Spider-Man crouched on a lamppost above her.

"Go!" said Sydney.

Lainey ran. Spider-Man swung. THWIP. THWIP. THWIP. Lainey dodged people walking their dogs. She hopped over a fire hydrant. Spider-Man swung from lamppost to tree to building.

They reached the end of the park. They turned around. They raced back.

Spider-Man landed on the corner one second before Lainey got there.

"Almost!" said Lainey, panting. "Almost AGAIN!"

Peter pulled up his mask so they could see his face. He was grinning. "Kid. I have spider powers. And you almost beat me. That's insane."

"Everybody says that," said Lainey.

At school the next day, Peter's best friend Ned was waiting at his locker. Ned was big and cheerful and always carrying a giant box of building blocks.

Ned was the only person in the world who knew Peter was Spider-Man.

Until now.
