"So there's a family," Peter told him. "And they know everybody. Mr. Stark. Captain America. Thor. A raccoon. And there's a girl who's like, a genius with machines."

"Cool," said Ned. "Can I meet them?"

So after school, Sydney came to Peter's science club. Peter showed her how he made his own web fluid. He mixed stuff in little test tubes. He shook them. He poured them into tiny cartridges that fit on his wrists.

"Why does it break sometimes?" asked Sydney, holding a web up to the light. It was strong, but at the very end it was a little bit thin.

"I don't know," said Peter. "It's the best I can do with the stuff from the science room."

Sydney looked at the web for a long time. She opened her notebook. She looked at the chemistry chart on the wall. She tapped her pencil against her teeth.

"What if you add a little more of this one," she said, pointing at a bottle, "and shake it longer? Like, a lot longer. Because the long strings need time to stick together. Like when Mom makes bread dough."

Peter stared at her. Then he tried it. He mixed. He shook. And shook. And shook. Ned timed it.

When Peter fired the new web at the wall, it was thicker. Stronger. It didn't get thin at the end at all.

"Whoa," said Peter. He looked at Sydney. "Are you sure you're not secretly a grown-up scientist?"

"I'm sure," said Sydney. "I still have a bedtime."

"Can I hire you?" said Ned.

Tim had come to pick Sydney up. He walked into the science room just as Peter was cleaning up.

"Careful, Mr. Tim," said Peter. "There's some extra web fluid on the—"

Tim stepped right in the middle of a puddle of it.

The web fluid went FWUMP. It puffed up into a big sticky white blob around his shoe. He tried to lift his foot. It didn't move. He tried to lift his other foot. He stepped in the edge of the blob. Now it didn't move either.

He wobbled. He waved his arms. And then he tipped over sideways, very slowly, like a tree falling down, and landed in the blob with a SPLUT.

Now his shoes were stuck. And his elbow was stuck. And the side of his face was stuck.

Everyone stared at him.

"...on the floor," finished Peter.

"Thanks," said Tim, from the floor. His voice was a little squished. "Good tip."

Sydney looked at the blob very closely. "Wow," she said. "The new formula is REALLY strong."

"Can somebody get me out?" said Tim.

"It dissolves in about two hours," said Peter.

"TWO HOURS?"

"Or," said Peter quickly, "I have a spray that dissolves it right away. Somewhere. In my backpack. Hang on."

It took Peter ten minutes to find the spray. By then Ned had taken forty pictures. Sydney had drawn a diagram of the blob in her notebook. And Tim had decided that being stuck to a floor wasn't so bad, as long as you had a good view of the ceiling.

On the drive home, Lainey asked Peter a question through the star pin.

"What's spider-sense feel like?"

Peter thought about it. "It's like a tingle," he said. "On the back of your neck. Right before something happens. Like when you just know someone's about to throw a ball at you."

"I get that!" said Lainey. "When Sydney's about to steal my fries!"

"That's not spider-sense," said Sydney. "That's just because I always steal your fries."

"Then my spider-sense is very good," said Lainey.

Peter laughed so hard he almost fell off the building he was sitting on.

That's when things started getting dangerous.

Peter was out swinging one night when he saw some men robbing an ATM. They were wearing masks of old Avengers. One of them was dressed like Captain America. One was dressed like Iron Man.

But their weapons were not normal weapons. They glowed purple. They hummed. When one of them fired, a beam of purple light went right through a car door like it was butter.

Peter stopped them, mostly. But the bad guys got away in a van.

The next day, Peter brought a piece of one of their weapons to the family's house. He laid it on the kitchen table.

Sydney took one look and sat straight up.

"I know this," she said. "I know this metal."

She ran upstairs. She came back down with her notebook, the one from the Battle of New York. She flipped back, back, back, through pages and pages, until she found it. A drawing of an alien flying scooter. The one the gray aliens rode out of the hole in the sky.

She held the drawing next to the purple weapon. They had the same bumpy gray metal. The same curly purple lines.

"It's alien," said Sydney. "It's from the battle. Somebody's been picking up the alien junk that fell after the battle, and turning it into weapons."

Tim and Lindsey looked at each other.

"After the battle, there were a lot of crews cleaning up," said Lindsey slowly. "Picking up all the broken pieces. Someone must have kept some."

"Someone's selling alien weapons," said Peter. "To bad guys. In my neighborhood."

He stood up. "I have to stop them."

"You should call Tony," said Tim.

Peter's face fell. "I have. Like, a hundred times. He never calls back. He just has his assistant say, 'Leave it to the grown-ups.'"

Tim looked at Peter. A fifteen-year-old boy who wanted to help so badly he could barely sit still.

"Then let's find them first," said Tim. "And then we'll call the grown-ups. Together."

Lindsey unfolded a map of Queens. Peter told her every place he had seen the bad guys. The ATM. A gas station. An alleyway behind a sandwich shop. Lindsey marked each one with a red X.

Then she looked at the X's. She looked at the streets. She drew lines between them.

"They're all along the same truck route," she said. "The big trucks that carry things to the warehouses by the water. If I were hiding a bunch of heavy alien junk..." She tapped a spot on the map. "I'd put it here. In a warehouse by the river. One that doesn't have a name on the front."

That night, Peter swung down to the river. The family waited in the car a few blocks away. Peter's new star pin was on, so they could hear everything.

The warehouse was dark and quiet. But inside, there were lights. And workers. And tables piled high with alien junk: broken scooters, bent armor, glowing purple pieces.

And there was a man.

He was older, with short gray hair and a hard, tired face. He wore a big green jacket with a fur collar. And on his back were wings. Huge metal wings, made out of alien scrap, with sharp blades on the ends and two big jet engines.

"That's the boss," whispered Peter into his pin. "His crew calls him the Vulture."

The Vulture's real name was Adrian Toomes. He used to clean up after the Battle of New York. Then the government told him to stop, and he got very angry. So he kept the alien junk. And he started making weapons.

"I'm just taking care of my family," the Vulture told his crew. "The rich people don't care about guys like us. So we take what we need."

In the car, Tim frowned. "That's not how you take care of your family," he said quietly.

Peter tried to sneak closer. But one of the workers saw him. "SPIDER!"

The Vulture spread his wings. His jets roared. WHOOOOOM!

He grabbed Peter and flew straight up into the sky, higher than the buildings. Then he dropped him into the river.

SPLASH!

"PETER!" yelled everybody in the car.

Peter came up coughing. He was tangled in a parachute that popped out of his suit. He was okay. But the Vulture was gone.

Then, out of the dark sky, a red-and-gold shape came zooming down.

Iron Man pulled Peter out of the water and set him on the riverbank.

"I told you to stay out of this," said Tony.

"I was trying to help!" said Peter.

"I know you were," said Tony. "And you almost got hurt. That's what I'm worried about."

A few days later, it happened again. Peter followed the Vulture's crew onto a big ferry boat in the middle of the river. Hundreds of people were on board. There was a fight. Peter tried to stop the bad guys. A purple weapon went off.

And the whole ferry split right down the middle.

CRRRRAAAACK!

One half started floating one way. The other half started floating the other way. People screamed.

Peter shot webs across the gap. THWIP. THWIP. THWIP. He tried to pull the two halves together. His webs stretched. His arms shook. He was holding the whole ferry together with just his webs and his own strength.

"I can do it!" he grunted. "I can do it!"

But he couldn't. Not alone. The webs started to slip.

Then Iron Man flew in. Tony welded the two halves of the ferry together with his lasers, zzzzt, zzzzt. Everyone was safe.

But Tony was not happy.

He landed on the deck. He took off his helmet. He looked at Peter.

"Give me the suit," said Tony quietly.

"What?" said Peter.

"You could have gotten someone hurt," said Tony. "You could have gotten yourself hurt. I'm taking the suit back."

Peter looked at Tony. His eyes filled up. "But I'm nothing without it."

Tony looked at him. He didn't say anything. He just held out his hand.

Peter took off the suit. He handed it over.

Tony flew away.

That night, Peter sat on the family's back porch in a T-shirt and sweatpants, staring at the golden tomato plant. He didn't say anything for a long time.

Tim came out and sat down next to him.

"I really messed up," said Peter.

"Yeah," said Tim. "You did."

Peter looked surprised.

"I mess up all the time," said Tim. "Everyone does. Tony messed up when he made Ultron. Steve messed up when he trusted the wrong people at S.H.I.E.L.D. Thor messed up so badly his dad took his hammer away."

"Really?" said Peter.

"Really," said Tim. "You know what I've learned? The suit doesn't make you a hero. Steve was a hero when he was skinny and small and getting knocked down by bullies in an alley. The suit just helps."

Peter thought about that.

"You stopped a bus before you had a fancy suit," said Tim. "You were Spider-Man in a hoodie and a pair of goggles. The hero part was you. It was always you."

Peter wiped his eyes with the back of his hand. "Yeah," he said. "Yeah. Okay."

Paisley came out onto the porch. She sat on Peter's other side. She was holding something behind her back.

"I made you something," she said.

She held it out. It was a red sweatshirt with a hood. On the front, someone had drawn a black spider with a marker. It was a little wobbly. And around the spider, someone had glued sparkly sequins.

"Lainey drew the spider," said Paisley. "Sydney picked the color. I did the sparkles."

Peter stared at it. Then he started to laugh. Then he hugged her.

"It's perfect," he said. "It's the best suit I've ever had."

Lindsey found the Vulture's plan.

She had been looking at the map of Queens every night, trying to figure out what the Vulture would do next. And then Aunt May mentioned something at dinner.

"Did you hear?" said Aunt May. "Tony Stark is moving all of the Avengers' things out of Stark Tower tonight. To their new Compound. On a big airplane. It was on the news."

Lindsey's fork stopped halfway to her mouth.

"An airplane," she said. "Full of Avengers' things. Weapons. Armor. Inventions." She looked at Tim. "If I were the Vulture, that's what I'd steal."

Peter was already pulling on his red hoodie.

The airplane was huge and gray and flying over the city in the dark. The family watched from the roof of Aunt May's building with binoculars. Peter had his star pin on his hoodie.

The Vulture flew up out of the night. He landed on top of the airplane. He started cutting into it with his wing blades.

Peter swung up after him, in his red hoodie and goggles. He climbed onto the plane. He jumped on the Vulture's back.

They fought on top of the plane. The wind was so strong it almost blew Peter off. The Vulture's blades went WHOOSH. Peter's webs went THWIP. Then the Vulture flipped the plane. It turned sideways. It wobbled. And it started to fall.

"Peter!" said Lindsey into the pin. "The plane's going down! Steer it toward the beach! There's nobody on the beach at night! Coney Island! Straight ahead!"

Peter grabbed the plane's tail. He pulled with all his strength. He webbed it. He pulled again. Slowly, slowly, the plane turned.

It crashed onto the sand at Coney Island, right next to the old roller coaster. BOOOOM! Sand flew everywhere. The plane skidded and stopped.

Nobody was on the beach. Nobody got hurt.

But Peter was trapped.

When the plane crashed, a giant chunk of metal had fallen right on top of him. He was pinned under it, flat on the sand. He couldn't move his arms. He couldn't move his legs. The metal was so heavy. So, so heavy.

"I can't," he whispered into the pin. "I can't. It's too heavy. I can't lift it."

In the car, racing toward the beach, the whole family heard him.

Paisley grabbed Tim's pin and held it right up to her mouth.

"Peter," she said. "It's Paisley."

"Paisley," gasped Peter. "I can't get up."

"Steve told us a story," said Paisley. "When he was little and skinny, bullies used to knock him down. Every single time. They were way bigger than him. But you know what he did?"

"What?"

"He got back up," said Paisley. "Every time. He said he could keep getting up all day long if he had to." She took a breath. "Peter, you're not nothing without the suit. You're Spider-Man in a hoodie with sparkles on it. That's the best kind."

Under the giant heavy metal, Peter was quiet.

Then he took a deep breath. In, like a flower.

Out, like birthday candles.

"Come on, Spider-Man," he whispered to himself. "Come on."

He pushed. His arms shook. The metal creaked. He pushed harder. He yelled. He pushed with everything he had, everything Tim had told him, everything Paisley had said, everything Steve had ever done in an alley in Brooklyn.

And the metal lifted. Slowly. Up, and up, and up. Until he threw it off and stood up on the sand, breathing hard.

"I DID IT!" he yelled.

In the car, everyone cheered so loudly that Tim almost drove off the road.

The Vulture was climbing out of the crashed plane. His wings were broken. Sparks flew out of them. The jet engines were sputtering and smoking.

"Hey!" yelled Peter. "Your wings! They're going to blow up! Get away from them!"

The Vulture didn't listen. He grabbed a box of Tony's inventions and tried to fly. The wings sparked. They smoked. They started to glow red.

Peter didn't think. He jumped. He grabbed the Vulture and pulled him off the wings, just as—

BOOM!

The wings exploded in a big ball of fire on the sand. But the Vulture wasn't in them anymore. He was lying on the beach next to Peter, coughing, totally safe.

He looked at Peter. "Why'd you do that? Why'd you save me?"

Peter shrugged. "Because that's what you do."

By the time the police got there, the Vulture was wrapped up in webs from head to toe, like a big gray burrito, sitting next to the crashed plane. Stuck to the webs was a note, written on a page from Sydney's notebook:

FROM YOUR FRIENDLY NEIGHBORHOOD SPIDER-MAN. (AND FAMILY.)

The next day, Tony asked Peter to come to the Avengers Compound.

The family came too. Peter wore his red hoodie with the sparkly spider. Tony was waiting in a big room with lots of reporters and cameras.

"Peter," said Tony. "You stopped the Vulture. You saved the plane. You saved the guy who was trying to hurt you." He took a breath. "I was wrong. You're ready. I want you to join the Avengers. Officially. Right now. In front of all these cameras."

Peter's mouth fell open. He looked at the cameras. He looked at Tony.

Then he looked at the family.

"Thanks, Mr. Stark," he said slowly. "Really. Thanks. But... I think I want to stay a friendly neighborhood Spider-Man for a while. You know? Help people in Queens. Help old ladies cross the street. Get cats out of trees. Finish high school." He smiled. "I'm only fifteen."

Tony looked at him for a long time. Then he smiled. A proud smile.

"Yeah," said Tony. "Yeah, that's the right answer, kid."

He handed Peter a bag. Inside was the fancy red-and-blue suit.

"You earned it back," said Tony. "And Peter? Next time you call, I'll pick up. I promise."

Paisley looked at Tony. Tony looked at Paisley.

"Every time," Tony said to her. "I promise. Okay?"

"Okay," said Paisley.

That night, Peter came home to Aunt May's apartment. He walked into his bedroom. He closed the door. He took the fancy suit out of the bag and put it on, just to see it again.

Then the door opened.

Aunt May stood there, holding a laundry basket. She stared at Peter. Peter stared at her. He was standing in the middle of his bedroom, in the full Spider-Man suit, with the mask in his hand.

"WHAT THE—" said Aunt May.

Peter dropped the mask. "May! I can explain!"

At the family's house, Lindsey's star pin buzzed. A tiny message popped up from Peter's pin:

MAY FOUND OUT. HELP.

Lindsey laughed so hard she had to sit down.

The next Sunday, Aunt May came to spaghetti dinner. She brought a walnut date loaf. She sat next to Steve and asked him a thousand questions about the 1940s. Steve answered every single one.

Peter sat at the end of the table with the girls. Lainey told him about racing Sleipnir. Sydney showed him her drawing of Vision. Paisley told him all about Sparkle the ant.

"You know," said Peter, "you guys are kind of like little sisters. Like, the best little sisters."

"We know," said Lainey.

"Obviously," said Sydney.

"Want some walnut date loaf?" said Paisley.

Peter looked at the brown loaf. He looked at Aunt May, who was watching him with a big, hopeful smile.

He took a slice.

"Delicious," said Peter.

"Delicious," said Tim.

And they both ate the whole thing.

## Chapter 13: The Hidden Kingdom

Steve came to Sunday spaghetti with a letter.

It wasn't an email. It wasn't a text. It was a real letter, on thick paper the color of cream, with a little purple seal on the back shaped like a panther's head.

"It came this morning," said Steve. "By special messenger. She dropped it off and disappeared before I could even say thank you."

"What does it say?" asked Paisley.

Steve unfolded it and read it out loud.

"Captain Rogers. My people found a man in the mountains near our border. He was cold and hungry and confused. He has an arm made of metal. He says his name is James. But sometimes he says it is Bucky. He says he knows you. Please come. I believe we can help him. Bring your friends. T'Challa, King of Wakanda."

Steve put the letter down. His hands were shaking a little.

"Bucky," he said. "They found Bucky."

Lindsey was already unfolding her map. She ran her finger across Africa. She looked at the big country in the middle where Wakanda was supposed to be.

On the map, it was just a little gray shape. There were no roads. No cities. Just one word, and a tiny picture of some farms.

"That's strange," said Lindsey. "My map says Wakanda is a small, poor country. Just farms and mountains. No cities at all."

Steve smiled a little. He reached over his shoulder and tapped his round shield, which was leaning against the wall.

"This shield is made of vibranium," he said. "The strongest metal in the world. Do you know where vibranium comes from?"

Sydney's eyes went wide. "Wakanda?"

"Wakanda," said Steve. "I think your map might be missing a few things."

The plane that came to pick them up was sleek and black, shaped like a giant bird. Inside, it was quiet and smooth, with purple lights glowing along the walls.

They flew over a big green forest. Mountains rose up on every side. A river wound through the trees like a silver ribbon.

"There's nothing down there," said Lainey, pressing her face against the window. "Just trees."

"Look closer," said the pilot.

The pilot was a tall woman with a shaved head, painted with beautiful gold patterns. She wore red armor and held a long silver spear across her lap, even while she was flying. Her name was Okoye, and she was the general of Wakanda's army.

The plane flew straight toward a wall of trees on a mountainside.

"We're going to crash!" yelled Tim.

"We are not going to crash," said Okoye calmly.

The plane flew right into the trees.

And went straight through them. Like they weren't even there.

On the other side, everything was different.

There was a city. A big, beautiful, sparkling city. Tall towers with round tops. Gardens growing on the roofs. Trains that floated above the ground, zooming silently from building to building. A huge waterfall poured down off a cliff into a pool so blue it almost glowed. People in bright, colorful clothes walked through busy markets full of fruit and music.

Everybody in the plane went silent.

"The trees aren't real," whispered Sydney. "It's a hologram. A giant picture. To hide the whole city."

"For a very long time," said Okoye, "Wakanda has kept its secrets. The world thinks we are small and poor. We let them think so. It keeps our vibranium safe."

Lindsey looked down at her map. At the little gray shape with one word and some farms. Then she looked out the window at the glittering city.

She turned her map over to a blank page. She started to draw.

"You are making a map of Wakanda?" said Okoye, raising an eyebrow.

"If that's okay," said Lindsey. "I'll keep it secret. I promise."

Okoye watched her draw for a moment. "Make the waterfall bigger," she said. "It is our favorite thing."

The King of Wakanda was waiting for them on the landing pad. He was tall and calm, with a short beard and kind, serious eyes. He wore a black shirt with a silver necklace made of shiny little teeth.

"Welcome to Wakanda," said King T'Challa. "Captain Rogers. I am glad you came."

"Thank you for your letter," said Steve. "Where is he?"

"With my sister," said T'Challa. "She is the smartest person in Wakanda. Possibly in the world. She insisted on helping him herself."

Then a girl came running across the landing pad. She was a teenager, with braids and a big grin, wearing a white top and very cool sneakers.

"Brother! You took forever!" she said. "Hello, hello, welcome, welcome! I'm Shuri! Which one of you is the girl who likes robots?"

Sydney raised her hand very slowly.

Shuri grabbed her hand and started pulling her toward a big glass building. "Come on. I have to show you my lab. You're going to lose your mind."

"Shuri," said T'Challa. "Manners."

"Fine," said Shuri. She stopped, turned around, bowed to the family very politely, then turned back to Sydney. "Okay, now come ON."

Shuri's lab was the most amazing place Sydney had ever been.

It was huge and white and full of glowing purple lights. There were robot arms. There were floating screens. There were sneakers on a shelf that lit up when you looked at them. There was a whole wall of black panther suits, each one a little bit different.

"Everything here is made with vibranium," said Shuri proudly. "It's a metal from space. A big meteor full of it crashed here a long, long time ago. Vibranium is so strong it can't break. And it soaks up energy. If you hit it, it doesn't hurt it. It just stores the energy and gets stronger."

"Like Steve's shield," said Sydney.

"Exactly like Captain America's shield," said Shuri. "Which, by the way, my great-great-great-grandparents made. He's welcome."

Shuri pulled a bracelet off a shelf. It was made of shiny black beads.

"Kimoyo beads," she said. "Each bead does something different. This one is a phone. This one is a map. This one heals scratches. This one plays music. This one..." She pressed it. A tiny hologram of a dancing cat popped up. "Okay, that one is just for fun. I made it when I was bored."

She slipped the bracelet onto Sydney's wrist.

"Keep it," said Shuri.

Sydney looked at the bracelet. She looked at Shuri. She did not know what to say. So she said the only thing she could think of.

"You're my hero."

Shuri laughed. "You're my hero too, little scientist. Now come on. I need your help with something."

In a quiet room at the back of the lab, Bucky was sleeping.

He was lying on a soft white bed. His long dark hair was spread out on the pillow. His metal arm was gone. In its place was just a bandage. His face looked peaceful.

Steve stood in the doorway and didn't move for a long time.

"Is he okay?" Steve whispered.

"He will be," said Shuri, her voice soft now. "When Hydra had him, they did something terrible. They put words in his head. Special words. Whenever someone said them, he couldn't control himself anymore. He had to do whatever they said."

"Like the scepter did to Clint," said Paisley quietly.

"Like that," said Shuri. "But worse. Because it lasted for years. I have been working on a way to take the words out. Gently. So they can never hurt him again."

"Can you do it?" asked Steve.

Shuri looked at her floating screens. She looked at Sydney.

"I think so," she said. "With a little help."

Sydney sat next to Shuri for the whole afternoon. Shuri explained how Bucky's brain worked, like a map with lots of roads. Hydra had built a few bad roads. Shuri wanted to close them, without closing any of the good ones.

"It's like untangling a necklace," said Sydney. "You have to find the right knot."

"Exactly!" said Shuri. "You have very good hands for knots, I can tell."

They worked and worked. Sydney found a knot. Shuri untied it. Sydney found another. Shuri untied that one too.

While they worked, Paisley sat on a stool next to Bucky's bed. She held his hand. Not the metal one. The real one.

She didn't say anything. She just held it.

When the last knot was untied, Bucky's eyes fluttered. He opened them. He looked around.

He looked at Paisley first.

"Hey," he said in a scratchy voice. "I know you. You're the kid with the headset. You told me to breathe."

"I'm Paisley," said Paisley.

"Thank you, Paisley," said Bucky.

Then he looked up and saw Steve, standing in the doorway.

"Hey, pal," said Bucky.

Steve walked over. He sat down on the edge of the bed. He didn't say anything. He just hugged Bucky, for a long, long time.

Lindsey wiped her eyes. Tim put his arm around her. Lainey and Sydney held hands.

Paisley reached into Steve's jacket pocket. She pulled out Biscuit, the little brown puppy with one floppy ear. She held it up.

"Look, Biscuit," she whispered. "We found Bucky."

That night, Lindsey unfolded her map. On the front, under FIND BUCKY (When he's ready), she drew a big, happy check mark.

The next few days in Wakanda were wonderful.

Paisley met the war rhinos. They were giant rhinoceroses wearing armor, as big as a truck, with huge horns. They lived in a big field near the mountains. Everyone told Paisley not to get too close. Paisley got too close.

The biggest rhino lowered its huge head, snorted, and stomped one foot. STOMP. It looked very scary.

Paisley held out her hand. "Hi," she said. "You're very big. Are you having big feelings? Because I'm having big feelings too. Breathe with me."

She breathed in. She breathed out.

The giant rhino looked at her. It breathed out a huge, warm, grassy-smelling breath. WHOOOOSH. Paisley's crown blew sideways.

Then the rhino licked her. One giant, slobbery lick from her chin to the top of her crown.

"EWWW," said Paisley, laughing. "You're just like Sleipnir!"

Okoye, watching from the fence, shook her head. "That rhino has never licked anyone but me," she said. "And it took him three years."

Lainey met Shuri's maglev train, the floating train that zoomed through tunnels under the city. She raced it along the platform until the end of the station.

"Almost!" she yelled, as the train whooshed away into a tunnel.

"She's very fast," said Okoye to Tim.

"She's almost always almost," said Tim.

And Tim met M'Baku.

M'Baku was the leader of a tribe that lived high in the snowy mountains. He was very big and very loud, with a beard and a wooden club and a cape made of white fur. He came down from the mountain to meet the king.

When he walked into the room, he made a sound like a gorilla. "HOO! HOO! HOO! HOO!"

Tim jumped so high that he knocked over a tray of drinks. They splashed all over the floor. Tim slipped on them. He slid across the shiny floor on his back, all the way to M'Baku's feet.

M'Baku looked down at him.

"Hello," said Tim, from the floor.

M'Baku stared at him. Then he threw back his head and laughed and laughed. "I like this one! He is very funny! Come to my mountain. I will feed you."

"What do you eat?" asked Lainey.

"Vegetables," said M'Baku seriously. "We are vegetarians."

So that night, the family went up the mountain for dinner.

M'Baku's great hall was made of dark wood, with a huge fire in the middle and furs on the benches. His people sat at long tables, laughing and singing. The food was amazing. Roasted squash. Sweet potatoes. Big bowls of beans and greens. Warm flat bread you tore with your hands.

Tim ate three bowls. He wiped his mouth. He looked at M'Baku.

"This is the best food I've ever had," said Tim. "Besides my wife's spaghetti."

M'Baku looked at Lindsey. "Your spaghetti is that good?"

"It's pretty good," said Lindsey modestly.

"It's the best in the universe," said Paisley. "Captain America says so. And Thor. And a raccoon."

M'Baku stroked his beard. "Then someday," he said, "I will come down from my mountain and eat this spaghetti. And we will see." He leaned down to Tim. "If it is better than my squash, I will give you my cape."

"Deal," said Tim, and they shook on it.

Later, while the grown-ups talked by the fire, Lainey found Okoye standing in the doorway, looking out at the snow.

"Can you teach me to fight?" asked Lainey. "With a spear? Like you?"

Okoye looked down at her. She didn't laugh. She thought about it very seriously.

"No," said Okoye.

Lainey's face fell.

"I will teach you something better," said Okoye. "The first thing every Dora Milaje learns. Before spears. Before armor. Before anything." She crouched down. "When to run. And where to."

Lainey's eyes lit up.

"Anyone can run away," said Okoye. "But a warrior runs toward something. Toward help. Toward the people who need her. Toward her family. That is why you are fast, little one. Not to get away. To get there."

Lainey thought about that for a long time.

"To get there," she repeated.

"To get there," said Okoye. And she tapped Lainey once on the forehead, very lightly, with two fingers. "Remember."

"I'll remember," said Lainey.

But then something bad happened.

A man came to Wakanda. A man nobody expected.

He was young and strong, with a hard face and an angry look in his eyes. His name was Erik. Some people called him Killmonger.

"I'm the king's cousin," Erik announced to the whole throne room. "My father was Wakandan. But I grew up far away, in America, all by myself. Wakanda forgot about me. And I've come to take what should have been mine."

"What do you want?" asked T'Challa.

"I want to be king," said Erik.

The whole room went quiet.

"In Wakanda," explained Okoye to the family in a low voice, "anyone of royal blood can challenge the king. They fight by the waterfall. Whoever wins is king."

"That's a scary rule," whispered Paisley.

"It is an old rule," said Okoye. "Not all old rules are good ones."

The challenge happened at the great waterfall. Thousands of Wakandans stood on the cliffs, all around a pool of blue water. T'Challa and Erik faced each other on a ledge over the water.

The family stood with the Queen Mother, T'Challa's mom. She was tall and elegant with white hair in a crown of curls. She held Paisley's hand tightly.

The fight was long and hard. Erik was fast and fierce. T'Challa was strong and steady. They pushed each other back and forth across the ledge.

Then Erik pushed T'Challa too hard.

T'Challa fell. Over the edge of the waterfall. Down, down, down, into the rushing water. And he was gone.

The whole crowd gasped. The Queen Mother cried out. Shuri screamed, "BROTHER!"

Paisley grabbed Lindsey. Tim put his arms around all of them.

Erik climbed up onto the ledge and lifted his arms. "I am your king now!"

That night, the Queen Mother, Shuri, and Nakia met in secret with the family. Nakia was a spy, and T'Challa's best friend. She was strong and brave and kind.

"We must leave the city," said Nakia. "Erik is going to do terrible things. He wants to use Wakanda's weapons to start wars all over the world."

"And my brother..." Shuri's voice cracked. "My brother..."

Lindsey sat up very straight.

"Wait," she said. "Wait. Everyone. We don't know that anything happened to him. We just saw him fall into the water."

Everyone turned to look at her.

She unfolded the map of Wakanda that she had been drawing all week. She traced the river with her finger. From the waterfall. Down through the canyon. Around a bend. Into the cold, snowy mountains.

"Rivers go somewhere," said Lindsey. "They always go somewhere. If he fell in here, the water would carry him down here, around this bend, and right up to the edge of the mountains." She tapped the spot. "Where M'Baku's people live."

Nakia's eyes went wide. "M'Baku's people fish in that river."

"Then that's where we look," said Lindsey.

They went up the mountain in the dark. Through the snow. Up a long, winding path. M'Baku was waiting for them at the top, in a big wooden hall with a roaring fire.

"I wondered when someone would come," said M'Baku.

He led them to a back room. There, lying on a bed of snow under thick furs, was T'Challa. His eyes were closed. He was very still. But when Paisley touched his hand, it was warm.

"My fishermen found him in the river," said M'Baku. "He is very hurt. But he is still here. He needs the heart-shaped herb to heal."

"The heart-shaped herb?" said Sydney.

"It is a special plant," said Shuri. "It only grows in Wakanda. It glows purple. It's what gives the Black Panther his powers. It could heal him." Her face fell. "But Erik is burning the whole garden. He wants to make sure nobody else can ever become Black Panther."

Then Paisley stood up.

"No, he's not," she said. "Not all of it."

She unzipped her backpack. She reached in, very carefully. And she pulled out a small glass jar with a lid. Inside, sitting on a little bit of dirt, was one small plant with glowing purple petals, shaped like a heart.

Everyone stared.

"I saw the garden on our first day," said Paisley. "It was so pretty. I asked the garden lady if I could have one to take home and grow next to our golden tomatoes. She said yes, as long as I took really good care of it." She held the jar out to Shuri. "I've been watering it every day."

Shuri took the jar. Her hands were shaking. Tears ran down her face.

"Princess Paisley," she whispered. "You are the best thing that has ever happened to this kingdom."

They crushed the glowing flower into a drink and gave it to T'Challa. They covered him in snow, the old Wakandan way. And they waited.

For a long time, nothing happened.

Then T'Challa took a deep breath. In, like a flower.

And he opened his eyes.

"Mother?" he said. "Shuri?"

Shuri threw herself on top of him and hugged him so hard he said, "Ow."

T'Challa sat up slowly. He looked around at all the faces. His mother. His sister. Nakia. M'Baku. The family.

"What happened?" he asked.

"You fell," said Lainey. "Off a waterfall. It was really scary."

"And my mom found you," said Sydney. "With a map."

"And my sister saved you," said Lainey. "With a flower."

T'Challa looked at Lindsey. He looked at Paisley. He bowed his head to both of them.

"Then I owe you my life," he said.

"Now let's go get your kingdom back," said Okoye, gripping her spear.

The final battle happened on the big green plains outside the city, and deep down in the vibranium mine under the mountain.

Erik had his own panther suit now. It was gold, with spiky lines. T'Challa had his black one, with silver lines that glowed purple when he got hit.

The war rhinos charged. The Wakandan warriors fought. And M'Baku and his mountain people came running down the hill, shouting "HOO! HOO! HOO!" to help their king.

Sydney stayed in Shuri's lab, watching everything on the big screens. Shuri had given her a headset, just like Maria Hill had.

T'Challa and Erik crashed down into the vibranium mine. They fought on top of a train that zoomed through the dark tunnels. Their suits glowed. Their claws scraped. Neither one could win. The suits were too strong.

"The suits are the same!" said Shuri, biting her nails. "Neither of them can break the other one!"

Sydney leaned toward the screen. She watched the train zoom down the tracks. She watched the suits flicker as they went past.

She looked closer.

Every time the fighters got near the train tracks, the glowing lines on their suits flickered. Just for a second. Like a light about to turn off.

"Shuri," said Sydney. "Look at the lines on the suits. When they go near the tracks, they get dimmer."

Shuri stared. "The train tracks are magnetic. They float the trains. The magnets must be messing with the suits. They turn them off!" She grabbed Sydney's shoulders. "If the suits turn off near the tracks, it's not suits fighting anymore. It's just T'Challa and Erik. And my brother has been training his whole life!"

Shuri turned on her microphone. "Brother! Fight next to the tracks! The magnets turn your suits off!"

Down in the mine, T'Challa heard. He grabbed Erik and pulled him right next to the glowing train tracks. Both suits flickered. Fizzled. And went dark.

Now it was just two cousins, fighting in the dark. And T'Challa had been training his whole life.

It was over very quickly.

Erik fell. His gold suit cracked. He lay on the ground, breathing hard. He didn't get up.

T'Challa knelt down beside him.

"I'm not going to hurt you, Erik," he said.

Erik looked at him, surprised. "Why not? I tried to take everything from you."

T'Challa looked at him for a long time. "Because you are my family. And I think you have been alone for too long."

Erik didn't say anything.

"There is a place in Wakanda," said T'Challa, "where you can see the sunset over the mountains. My father used to tell me about it. I think your father told you too."

Erik's eyes got shiny. "He did," he whispered. "He said it was the most beautiful thing in the world."

T'Challa held out his hand. "Then come and see it."

Erik looked at the hand for a long, long time.

Then he took it.

The two cousins sat together at the top of the mountain and watched the sun go down over Wakanda. It was the most beautiful thing in the world. Orange and pink and gold, spreading over the city and the river and the waterfall.

Erik didn't say anything. But he didn't leave, either.

"He's going to stay," said T'Challa to the family afterward. "He's going to learn. It will take a long time. But we have plenty of time."

"Nobody gets left behind," said Tim softly.

T'Challa looked at him. "Yes. That is exactly right."

On the last day, T'Challa made a big announcement. He stood in front of all of Wakanda and said that Wakanda would not hide anymore. It would share its science and medicine with the rest of the world. It would help people everywhere.

Everyone cheered.

Bucky was getting better every day. He had a little hut by a lake now, where he lived with a herd of goats. He played with the kids from the village. They called him White Wolf.

Paisley visited him on the last morning. She had named every single goat.

"That's Buttercup," she said, pointing. "That's Sir Fluffington. That one's Pancake. And that grumpy one is Loki."

Bucky laughed. A real laugh. Steve, standing beside him, laughed too.

"Shuri's making me a new arm," Bucky told them. "Out of vibranium. She says it's going to be better than the old one."

"Of course it is," said Shuri, who had come up behind them. "I'm making it."

Then she turned to Sydney.

"I've been thinking about something," said Shuri. "Your friend Vision. The one with the yellow stone in his head."

"What about him?" asked Sydney.

"That stone is very powerful," said Shuri. "And someday, someone might try to take it from him. Right now, without the stone, he couldn't live." She tapped her chin. "But vibranium can do anything. If I studied him, maybe I could build him something. A new heart. Made of vibranium. So he'd be safe, even without the stone."

Sydney grabbed her notebook. She wrote it down in big letters.

VIBRANIUM HEART FOR VISION???

She underlined it twice. Then she drew a star next to it.

"Promise you'll call me if you need help with it?" said Sydney.

Shuri tapped the kimoyo bracelet on Sydney's wrist. "Push the third bead," she said. "Anytime. Day or night."

When the family climbed back into the black bird-shaped plane, all of Wakanda seemed to come out to say goodbye. Okoye. Nakia. The Queen Mother. M'Baku, who gave Tim a big hug and nearly squished him. The biggest war rhino, who licked Paisley one last time through the plane window.

T'Challa stood at the front. He crossed his arms over his chest, in the Wakandan salute.

"Wakanda forever," he said.

The family crossed their arms, too.

"Wakanda forever," they said.

And as the plane rose up into the sky and flew back through the hologram trees, Paisley pressed her face against the window. She was holding a new little glass jar. Shuri had given it to her right before they left.

Inside was another heart-shaped flower. Small and purple and glowing.

"To grow next to your golden tomatoes," Shuri had said. "Since you take such good care of things."

## Chapter 14: Ragnarok

Thor came back on a Tuesday. He didn't land in the garden. He didn't land in the front yard. He knocked on the front door like a regular person.

Knock, knock, knock.

Lindsey opened it. Thor was standing on the doormat with his hammer in one hand. Behind him was Loki, in a long black coat, looking at the house like he was trying very hard not to look interested in it.

"Lady Lindsey," said Thor. "I knocked."

"You did," said Lindsey, smiling. "I'm very proud of you."

"I would like some help," said Thor. "Again. I'm afraid I'm always asking."

"That's what friends are for," said Lindsey. "Come in. And Loki, you too."

Loki raised an eyebrow. "You're letting me into your house?"

"Paisley would never forgive me if I didn't," said Lindsey.

At the kitchen table, Paisley was showing Sydney her new heart-shaped flower from Wakanda, glowing purple in its little jar. When she saw Loki, she jumped up and hugged him around the waist.

Loki froze like an ice cube again. Then, very slowly, he patted her crown. Pat. Pat. He was getting a little better at it.

"We are looking for our father," said Thor, sitting down. "Odin has left Asgard. He is somewhere on Earth. But he is old, and he is very tired, and he has hidden himself well. Even Heimdall cannot see him."

"Then we'll find him," said Lindsey.

It took two days. Lindsey looked at every map she had. She called Doctor Strange, who opened a glowing orange circle right in the kitchen and stepped through with his red cape. The cape flew straight to Paisley, picked her up, and spun her around in a circle.

"Hello to you too," said Stephen. "My cape missed you."

Stephen helped them search with his magic. Lindsey helped with her maps. And finally, Lindsey put her finger down on a spot in the far north, on the edge of a cold blue sea.

"Norway," she said. "There's an old story about a king who came from the sky and lived by the sea. People there still tell it. If I were a very old king looking for somewhere quiet, I'd go somewhere that already knew my story."

Thor looked at her with wide eyes. "That is exactly the kind of place my father would choose."

Stephen opened a portal. On the other side, there was a green cliff over a sparkling blue sea, with waves crashing on rocks far below and the wind blowing through the grass.

And sitting on a rock at the edge of the cliff, wrapped in a gray cloak, looking out at the water, was Odin.

He looked older than they remembered. Much older. His white beard blew in the wind. His one eye was tired.

"My sons," he said, without turning around. "I've been waiting for you."

"Father," said Thor. "Come home. Asgard needs you."

Odin shook his head slowly. "Not anymore. I am very old, Thor. I am very tired. I must go into the Odinsleep. A long, long sleep. Many years, maybe. To get my strength back."

"Then sleep in Asgard," said Loki. "Where we can watch over you."

"I cannot," said Odin. "And there is something I must tell you before I go." He turned around and looked at both of his sons. "You have a sister."

Thor and Loki stared at him.

"We have a WHAT?" said Thor.

"Her name is Hela," said Odin. "She is my firstborn. Long ago, she was my warrior. She helped me conquer the nine worlds. But she wanted more and more and more. She would never stop. So I locked her away, far from Asgard. My power has kept her locked up all these years." He looked at the sea. "When I go to sleep, my power will sleep too. And she will be free."

"Then don't go to sleep!" said Thor.

"I must," said Odin gently. "I have no choice. My body needs it. But you are strong, Thor. Stronger than you know. Remember that."

He looked at the family. He looked at Paisley, holding Lindsey's hand, with the wind blowing her hair across her crown.

"Princess," he said. "Look after my sons."

"I will," said Paisley.

Odin smiled. Then he closed his eye. His body glowed a soft golden light, like a sunset. The light grew brighter and brighter, until they couldn't look at it. Then it floated up into the sky and drifted away over the sea, toward a quiet place where Odin could sleep, safe, for as long as he needed.

Everyone stood on the cliff for a long time.

Then, behind them, the air ripped open.

A black hole appeared in the sky. A swirling, shadowy doorway. And out of it walked a woman.

She was tall and pale, with long black hair. She wore a black and green suit that fit like armor. And when she lifted her chin, a giant crown of black antlers grew out of her head, like a deer, but made of sharp, shiny spikes.

"So," she said, in a voice like cold silk. "Father is finally asleep."

"You must be Hela," said Thor. He stepped in front of the family. He raised Mjolnir.

Hela looked at the hammer. She smiled a terrible smile.

"Oh, little brother," she said. "Is that what you're using now?"

Thor threw Mjolnir as hard as he could.

Hela caught it. With one hand.

Then she squeezed.

The hammer cracked. CRACK. Lines shot across it like a broken window. And then it burst into pieces. Big chunks and little chunks of gray metal fell onto the grass at Thor's feet.

Thor stared at them. He couldn't speak.

Loki, behind him, whispered, "Bring us home, Heimdall! NOW!"

A rainbow beam came crashing down from the sky. It wrapped around them. Thor, Loki, and the family shot up into the air.

But Hela jumped in after them.

Inside the rainbow bridge, everything was spinning. Hela flew after them through the colors. She reached out and grabbed Loki's coat. She pulled. She pushed. She knocked Thor sideways.

And everyone went tumbling out of the side of the rainbow bridge.

Out into the darkness of space.

Falling. Spinning. Down and down and down.

The family landed in a pile of garbage.

Not a little pile. A giant mountain of it. Old spaceship parts. Broken chairs. Squashed cans. A sofa with no cushions. A toaster with eyes.

Tim sat up first. He had a banana peel on his head. "Is everyone okay?"

"I'm okay," said Lainey, climbing out of a broken refrigerator.

"I'm okay," said Sydney, checking that her notebook was still in her pocket.

"I'm okay," said Paisley. "But my crown is crooked." She straightened it.

Lindsey was already standing, looking around with her hands on her hips. The sky above was orange and purple. And it was full of holes. Big swirling holes, like whirlpools, with junk falling out of them and landing in big mountains.

"Well," said Lindsey. "This isn't on any of my maps."

Thor was sitting nearby, holding one small piece of his broken hammer. He wasn't saying anything.

"Thor?" said Paisley softly.

"She broke it," said Thor. "She just... broke it."

Just then, a little ship zoomed down and landed in front of them. A woman jumped out. She had dark hair braided back, a stripe of white paint across her face, and a leather jacket. She looked tough. She looked tired. She looked at them all like they were a bunch of junk that had fallen out of the sky, which they kind of were.

"Well, well," she said. "Fresh ones."

She pointed a little gadget at Thor and zapped him. ZAP! Thor jolted and fell over.

"HEY!" said Lainey.

"Sorry," said the woman. "Business. I'm taking him to the Grandmaster. He's going to love this one." She looked at the family. "You lot can come too, I suppose. You look harmless."

"We are NOT harmless," said Lainey.

The woman looked at her. "No," she said, after a moment. "Maybe you're not."

The planet was called Sakaar. And the Grandmaster ruled it.

He lived in a huge tower in the middle of a big, sparkly city made of junk. He was tall, with silver-gray hair and a gold robe and a blue stripe on his chin. He loved music and parties and games. He talked very, very fast.

"Oh, look at you!" said the Grandmaster, clapping his hands when he saw Thor, who was wearing a little sparky collar now. "You're big! You're blond! You've got muscles! You're going to be PERFECT for the Contest of Champions!"

"What's the Contest of Champions?" asked Sydney.

"It's a game!" said the Grandmaster. "Everybody's favorite! Fighters from all over the universe, fighting my champion! My champion always wins. Always, always, always. He's the best! The crowd loves him!"

"I'm not fighting anyone," said Thor. "I need to get back to Asgard."

"Sure, sure, sure," said the Grandmaster, not listening at all. "After the game."

He waved his hand at the family. "And these little ones? Cute. They can be my guests. Snacks? Juice? Anybody want a snack?"

Lainey grabbed a juice box. Tim grabbed Lainey.

Loki, of course, was already sitting next to the Grandmaster on a fancy sofa, eating grapes. He had landed on Sakaar weeks before everyone else, because falling through space is strange that way. And he had already made friends with the most important person on the planet.

"Brother!" said Loki, waving a grape. "You made it."

"You!" said Thor.

"Me," said Loki, smiling.

That afternoon, they took Thor to the giant arena. Thousands of strange aliens sat in the seats, cheering and yelling. The family sat in the Grandmaster's special box, way up high.

Thor stood in the middle of the arena. He had a sword. He had a shield. He had no hammer.

"And now," shouted the Grandmaster into a microphone, "your champion!"

A giant door at the end of the arena slowly opened.

And out stomped someone very big. And very green. Wearing a gold helmet with spikes and a cape made of red cloth.

"HULK!" yelled the crowd. "HULK! HULK! HULK!"

Thor's face lit up. "YES! I KNOW HIM! HE'S MY FRIEND! WE WORK TOGETHER!"

"BRUCE!" screamed Paisley, standing up on her chair.

The Hulk didn't hear her. He roared at Thor. And charged.

The fight was loud. Hulk was strong. Thor was strong. They smashed. They bashed. They crashed into walls. The crowd went wild.

"He doesn't remember us," said Sydney, gripping the edge of the box.

"He's been the Hulk for a long time," said Lindsey quietly. "Maybe a really long time. Maybe Bruce got lost inside."

Then Thor did something surprising. He stood still in the middle of the arena. He closed his eyes. And for the first time since his hammer broke, lightning crackled across his hands. His eyes glowed white. Blue sparks danced across his whole body.

The crowd gasped.

Thor didn't know how he did it. But it happened. He didn't need the hammer. The power was coming from somewhere inside him.

Just then, the Grandmaster, who did not like losing, pushed a button. Thor's sparky collar went ZAP. He fell over.

"And the CHAMPION wins!" yelled the Grandmaster.

Everyone cheered. The family did not cheer.

The Hulk had a room in the tower. It was a giant room, with a giant bed, and a giant hot tub, and lots of giant weapons on the walls. He sat in the hot tub, looking grumpy.

Thor was in there too, sitting on the giant bed, rubbing his neck.

Paisley walked in. Nobody had told her she could. She just did.

The Hulk looked at her. He blinked. His big eyebrows went up.

"Crown girl," he said slowly, in a deep, rumbly voice.

Paisley's whole face lit up. "You remember me!"

"Crown girl," said the Hulk again. "Breathe girl."

"That's me," said Paisley.

"Crown girl in Hulk's room," said the Hulk. "Why?"

"Because we're going home," said Paisley. "Back to Earth. And you should come too. We all miss you. Natasha misses you. And Tony. And Steve. And I miss you."

The Hulk looked away. He splashed the water.

"Hulk likes here," he said. "People cheer for Hulk. People like Hulk. On Earth, everyone like Banner. Nobody like Hulk."

Paisley climbed up and sat on the edge of the giant hot tub.

"That's not true," she said. "I like Hulk. I like Bruce. I like both. You don't have to pick. You can be friends with each other."

The Hulk thought about that.

"Breathe with me," said Paisley. "In, like a flower."

The Hulk looked at her for a long time. Then he breathed in. SNIIIIIFF.

"Out, like birthday candles."

WHOOOOOSH. The water in the hot tub went splash, all over the floor.

The Hulk got smaller. His green faded. And there, in the giant hot tub, much too big for him, sat Bruce Banner. In a pair of very, very big shorts.

He blinked. He looked around. He looked at Paisley. He looked at Thor.

"Where am I?" said Bruce. "What happened? Why is everything so big? Why am I in a bathtub?"

"You've been the Hulk," said Thor. "For two years."

Bruce went very pale. "Two YEARS?"

"It's okay," said Paisley, patting his wet hand. "You're not lost anymore. We found you."

That night, Lindsey drew a check mark next to FIND BRUCE on her map. Then she drew another one, just because.

Now they needed to escape.

"The Grandmaster won't let us leave," said Bruce. "He likes his champions. He likes his guests. He doesn't let anyone go."

"Then we'll just have to go without asking," said Loki, smiling his sneaky smile.

Lindsey looked at him. Under LOKI = TROUBLE, there were still the words ...but maybe not ALL trouble.

"Okay, Loki," she said. "What's your plan?"

Loki's plan had a lot of parts. They needed a ship. They needed a pilot. They needed to get past the guards. And they needed to get through the biggest portal in the sky.

The woman who had zapped Thor turned out to be the pilot. Her name was Valkyrie. A long time ago, she had been one of Asgard's greatest warriors. She rode a flying horse and protected the king. But a long time ago, Hela had hurt her friends, and Valkyrie had run away to Sakaar and tried to forget about Asgard.

"I'm not going back," said Valkyrie, when Thor asked. "Asgard can take care of itself."

Thor looked at her. "Hela is back. She'll hurt the people of Asgard. Just like she hurt your friends."

Valkyrie didn't say anything.

Tim sat down next to her. "My friend Steve told me something," he said. "A good captain doesn't give up. And a good captain doesn't leave anybody behind." He looked at her. "Not even the people who left first."

Valkyrie looked at him for a long time.

"Fine," she said finally. "I'll fly the ship."

Lainey's job was the guards. There were guards in every hallway. So Lainey did what she did best. She ran.

"HI!" she yelled at the first guard. "CATCH ME!"

The guard chased her. So did the next one. And the next one. Lainey ran up and down and around the Grandmaster's tower, collecting guards like a train collects train cars. By the time she was done, there were twenty guards chasing her in one big line, all huffing and puffing.

On the way, she ran past a big jail full of the other fighters from the arena. Big ones and small ones and weird ones. One of them was made of rocks. He was huge and blue-gray and rocky, but he had a very soft, gentle voice.

"Hello," said the rock man, as Lainey zipped past. "I'm Korg. I'm planning a revolution. But I haven't printed enough pamphlets."

Lainey skidded to a stop. "A revolution? Like, to get out?"

"Exactly like that," said Korg. "Would you like to join?"

Lainey looked at the big lock on the jail door. She looked at the guards coming down the hallway. She pressed the big button next to the door.

CLANK. The jail door slid open.

"Revolution!" said Korg, very politely. "Off we go, everyone."

All the fighters poured out of the jail and ran right into the line of guards chasing Lainey. Lainey ran the other way, laughing.

Sydney and Bruce had their own job. The ship Loki picked was the Grandmaster's own spaceship, a big, shiny, fancy one. But it was locked. And the controls were all scrambled.

"I've never seen wiring like this," said Bruce, staring into a panel full of colorful wires. "It's all backwards."

Sydney looked over his shoulder. "It's not backwards. It's just in a different order. Like reading a book from the end." She pointed. "Look. Red goes to blue here. And blue goes to green over here. If you follow the colors, they make a circle."

Bruce stared at the wires. Then he stared at Sydney. "You're right. Oh my gosh. You're right."

Sydney pulled out a tiny screw. The tiniest, shiniest one. "Can I?"

"Please," said Bruce.

Twist, twist, twist. The ship's engines roared to life.

"I'm so glad you're here," said Bruce.

"Me too," said Sydney.

Then they all flew.

Valkyrie steered the ship. Bruce and Sydney kept the engines running. Thor and Loki stood at the windows. Tim and Paisley and Lainey held onto the seats. And Lindsey sat in the front, right next to Valkyrie, looking at the giant swirling portal in the sky.

The biggest portal on all of Sakaar. Spinning and swirling, red and orange and purple, like a huge angry storm.

"Nobody's ever flown through that thing on purpose," said Valkyrie. "It's like flying into a blender."

"Every storm has a pattern," said Lindsey. She watched the swirls. She watched them spin. Around and around. "Look. See where the purple goes? It always comes back to the same place. Like water going down a drain. If we follow the purple, we'll stay in the calm part."

Valkyrie looked at her. "You are a very strange person."

"Thank you," said Lindsey.

They flew into the portal. It was loud and bumpy and the ship shook like a leaf. Lindsey said "Left!" and Valkyrie went left. "Up!" and Valkyrie went up. "Follow the purple!" They followed the purple.

And then—WHOOOSH—they popped out the other side.

Right above Asgard.

Asgard was in trouble.

Hela had taken over the golden palace. She had brought her giant wolf, Fenris, who was as big as a house. She had brought an army of skeleton warriors that she had woken up from under the palace. And she was chasing the people of Asgard out of the city.

But one person was protecting them.

Heimdall.

He had led all the people of Asgard into the mountains and hidden them in caves. Now he was leading them across the rainbow bridge, trying to get them to safety. Hela's army was right behind them.

"There!" said Lindsey, pointing at the bridge. "They're all on the bridge!"

Valkyrie brought the big ship down right at the end of the rainbow bridge. The doors opened.

"Everybody on!" yelled Tim, standing at the door. "Come on! This way! Hold hands! Nobody gets left behind!"

The people of Asgard poured onto the ship. Moms carrying babies. Old men with canes. Kids holding stuffed animals. Lindsey stood at the door and helped them find seats. Sydney showed them where the bathrooms were. Paisley handed out juice boxes she had saved from Sakaar.

And Lainey ran back and forth across the bridge, helping the slowest people go faster.

Then Fenris the giant wolf came running down the bridge.

He was enormous. His fur was gray and green. His teeth were huge. He was growling.

The Hulk jumped out of the ship. He was green again, but this time Bruce had chosen to let him out. He roared at Fenris.

"HULK SMASH WOLF!"

He grabbed Fenris by the tail and swung him around in a circle and threw him off the edge of the bridge. Fenris went flying out into the sea of stars.

Then Hulk turned around, looked at Paisley, and gave her a big green thumbs up.

Paisley gave him two thumbs up back.

But Hela was too strong. She stood at the other end of the bridge, with her black antler crown and her army of skeletons. And Thor was fighting her alone.

He had no hammer. He had a sword. She knocked it out of his hand.

He fell down on the bridge.

He got back up.

She knocked him down again.

He got back up again.

On the ship, Paisley grabbed Lindsey's arm. "He needs his hammer."

"His hammer is broken, sweetheart," said Lindsey.

"No," said Paisley. "No, he doesn't need it. Remember? He doesn't NEED it." She pulled away and ran down the ramp, onto the rainbow bridge.

"PAISLEY!" yelled Tim and Lindsey at the same time.

Paisley ran to the edge of the bridge, where Thor was lying, trying to get up.

"Thor!" she said. "Thor! Remember what you told us? At the campout?"

Thor looked up at her, tired and hurt.

"Worthy means being good on the inside," said Paisley. "It's not the hammer. It was never the hammer. Odin said you're stronger than you know. And in the arena, you made lightning with no hammer at all. I saw you!"

Thor stared at her.

"You're not the god of hammers," said Paisley. "You're the god of THUNDER."

Something in Thor's face changed.

He stood up. He closed his eyes. He breathed in.

And the sky exploded with lightning.

CRAAAAAACK!

Lightning came down from every direction at once. It wrapped around Thor. His eyes glowed white. Blue sparks covered his whole body. He rose up off the bridge into the air, crackling with power.

Hela's skeleton army stopped and stared.

Thor came down like a meteor. BOOOOOM! The lightning blasted every single skeleton warrior off the bridge. They went flying out into the stars like a bunch of dry leaves.

Paisley's crown fell off from the wind. Tim ran out and scooped her up and carried her back to the ship, both of them laughing and crying.

But Hela was still standing.

"You can't beat her," said Loki, running up beside Thor. "Not even like this. She gets her power from Asgard itself. As long as Asgard is here, she can't be beaten."

Thor looked at Loki. Then he looked up at the golden city. The towers. The palace. The waterfalls. His home.

"Then Asgard can't be here," said Thor quietly.

"What?" said Loki.

"There is an old story," said Thor. "Ragnarok. The end of Asgard. A fire giant named Surtur will come and burn it all down." He looked at his brother. "Loki. Go to the vault. Wake up Surtur."

Loki stared at him. Then he nodded and ran.

"Thor," said Lindsey, at the door of the ship. "What are you doing?"

Thor looked at all the people of Asgard sitting on the ship. The moms. The old men. The kids with stuffed animals.

"Asgard is not a place," he said. "It never was. Asgard is its people. And they are right here."

A few minutes later, the ground under the palace began to shake. A giant shape rose up out of the vault. He was taller than any tower, made of fire and black rock, with a huge flaming sword and a crown of horns.

Surtur.

He swung his flaming sword down at the golden city. Hela screamed and fought him. But Surtur was too big. The fire swept over the palace, over the towers, over Hela. And Surtur swept Hela away into his fire world, far, far away, where she would be stuck forever and could never hurt anyone ever again.

The golden city cracked. It glowed red. And then, slowly, beautifully, terribly, it broke apart into a thousand glowing pieces, floating out into the sea of stars.

Everyone on the ship watched out the windows. Nobody said anything.

The people of Asgard cried. Thor stood at the window with his hand on the glass. Valkyrie stood beside him.

Paisley climbed up on a seat next to Thor. She took his hand. She didn't say anything. She just held it.

Lindsey took out her map. The very first map of Asgard ever made by a person from Earth. She looked at the drawings. The towers. The rainbow bridge. The waterfalls.

"I'll keep this safe," she said softly. "Forever. So nobody forgets what it looked like."

Thor turned around and looked at her. His eyes were wet. "Thank you, Lady Lindsey," he said. "That means more to me than I can say."

Loki came onto the ship just as the doors closed. He was out of breath. His coat was singed. He was holding his arms tight around his middle, like he was hiding something under his coat.

Lindsey looked at him. Her eyes narrowed.

"Loki," she said. "What's under your coat?"

Loki froze. "Nothing."

"Loki."

Loki sighed a long, long sigh. He opened his coat. Hidden inside, glowing bright blue, was the Tesseract. The blue cube. The Space Stone.

"You took it from the vault," said Lindsey.

"I couldn't leave it there!" said Loki. "If Surtur burned it, who knows what would happen. And if it fell into space, someone would find it someday. Someone bad. I was keeping it safe!" He paused. "Mostly."

Thor stared at his brother.

Then he laughed. A tired, happy, surprised laugh. "You stole something to keep it safe. That is the most Loki thing I have ever heard."

"I'll hold onto it," said Loki. "I promise. I'll keep it safe."

Lindsey looked at him for a long moment. Then she turned her map over. She found 1. Space Stone (blue). She crossed out "Thor took it to Asgard" and wrote:

Loki has it! Keeping it safe. (He promised.)

On the front side of the map, she added one more line under LOKI = TROUBLE:

...still watching.

Loki read it over her shoulder. "That's fair," he said.

Before they left the ruins of Asgard behind, Paisley went to her backpack. She opened it very carefully. And she pulled out something heavy, wrapped in her sweater.

She unwrapped it on Thor's lap.

It was the pieces of Mjolnir. All of them. Big chunks and little chunks of gray metal. Every single piece that had fallen on the grass on the cliff in Norway.

Thor stared at them. "You... kept them?"

"You don't throw away a friend's broken things," said Paisley. "Maybe somebody can fix her someday."

Thor picked up the biggest piece. He held it in his hands. He didn't say anything for a long time.

Then he picked Paisley up and hugged her so tightly that her crown popped off and bounced across the floor, and Korg caught it.

"Here you go," said Korg gently, handing it back. "Lovely crown."

"Where will we go now?" asked an old Asgardian man, sitting on the ship. "We have no home."

Thor looked at Tim and Lindsey.

"Earth," said Lindsey, before Thor could even ask. "Norway. Where Odin went to sleep. Where the cliffs look out on the sea. It's beautiful there. And it already knows your story."

Thor smiled. "New Asgard," he said.

When the big ship finally landed on the green cliffs of Norway, the people of Asgard walked out onto the grass and breathed the cold sea air. Kids ran around in the fields. Korg started building a little house out of rocks. Valkyrie stood on the cliff with her arms crossed, looking at the water, and almost smiled.

Lindsey reached into her bag and pulled out a little cloth pouch. She handed it to Thor.

"What is this?" asked Thor.

"Seeds," said Lindsey. "From the golden tomatoes. The ones you planted in my garden. They're probably the last Asgard plants anywhere." She smiled. "So now New Asgard can grow some too."

Thor opened the pouch. He looked at the little golden seeds. And he started to laugh. And then he started to cry. And then he did both at the same time.

"Lady Lindsey," he said. "You are the best finder in all the nine realms."

The family went home that night through one of Stephen's glowing portals. The house was quiet. The garden was dark. The golden tomato plant stood tall in the moonlight, next to the heart-shaped flower from Wakanda, glowing softly purple in the dirt.

Paisley fell asleep the second her head hit the pillow.

But far, far away, out in the deepest dark of space, a giant ship was waiting.

It was huge. Bigger than the Helicarrier. Bigger than Ronan's ship. Bigger than anything. And inside it, sitting on a big stone chair, was someone very large, with purple skin and a golden glove on his hand.

The glove had six empty holes in it.

"The Space Stone has left Asgard," said a whispery voice beside him.

The large purple someone smiled a slow smile.

"Then it's time," said Thanos.

## Chapter 15: Thanos Arrives

It was harvest day.

The golden tomato plant was taller than the house now. Its branches reached all the way up to the gutters. And it was covered, absolutely covered, in golden tomatoes. Big ones, round and shiny, glowing in the morning sun like a tree full of little lamps.

"Everybody get a basket," said Lindsey. "We're picking all of them today."

The girls ran outside with baskets. Lainey climbed the ladder. Sydney held the ladder. Paisley carried the full baskets to the porch, one by one, very carefully, so none would bruise. Tim stood at the bottom of the plant with the biggest basket of all, catching tomatoes as Lainey tossed them down.

"Ready, Dad?" said Lainey.

"Ready," said Tim.

She tossed one. He caught it. She tossed another. He caught it.

"This is going great," said Tim. "Not one tomato on my head."

That's when the sky went BOOM.

Not thunder. Something else. A crack of color, like a tiny piece of rainbow, flashed in the sky right above the house. And something big and heavy came falling out of it.

It fell straight down.

It landed right in the middle of the golden tomato plant.

CRASH! SPLAT! SPLAT! SPLAT!

Tomatoes went everywhere. One landed on Tim's head. SPLAT.

Tim stood very still. Golden tomato juice dripped down his face.

"Not again," said Tim.

Out of the squashed leaves of the tomato plant crawled a man. He had messy gray hair and glasses, and he was wearing a very torn shirt. He was dirty. He was out of breath. And he looked terrified.

"BRUCE!" yelled everyone.

Bruce Banner looked up at them. His eyes were wild.

"Thanos," he gasped. "Thanos is coming."

They brought Bruce inside and gave him a glass of water and a towel. His hands were shaking so badly he spilled the water. Paisley sat next to him and held the glass for him.

"Breathe," she said. "In like a flower. Out like birthday candles."

Bruce breathed. In. Out. In. Out. Slowly, his hands stopped shaking.

"Okay," he said. "Okay. Here's what happened."

The family sat around him on the couch and on the floor and listened.

"We were all in New Asgard," said Bruce. "In Norway. Everybody was building houses. Korg was making a garden out of rocks. Thor was teaching the kids how to make lightning. It was nice. It was really, really nice."

He swallowed.

"Then a giant ship came. Bigger than anything. And out of it came Thanos."

"What does he look like?" asked Sydney quietly.

"Big," said Bruce. "Bigger than the Hulk. Purple. With a big chin. He wears gold armor. And on his left hand, he wears a giant golden glove. And in the glove..." Bruce held up one finger. "There was already a purple stone."

Lindsey went pale. She turned her map over. 3. Power Stone (purple). Locked in the Nova Corps vault on Xandar.

"He got the Power Stone," she whispered. "He went to Xandar first."

"Is Xandar okay?" asked Paisley. "Are the Nova Corps okay?"

"I don't know," said Bruce. "But Thanos said something about it. He said nobody on Xandar could stop him, so he just walked into their vault and took it. Like he was picking up his mail."

"What did he want in New Asgard?" asked Tim.

"The blue stone," said Bruce. "The Tesseract. Loki had it."

Everyone went very quiet.

"Loki tried to trick him," said Bruce. "He made a fake Loki out of magic and a fake Tesseract. It was really good. It almost worked. But Thanos is really smart. He saw through it." Bruce looked at his hands. "So Loki gave it to him. To keep everybody else safe. Valkyrie and Heimdall got all the people into the caves under the cliffs. Nobody got hurt. But Thanos took the Space Stone."

Lindsey wrote on her map, under the Power Stone. THANOS HAS IT. And under the Space Stone. THANOS HAS IT, TOO.

Then she drew a big circle around both of them.

"Where's Thor?" asked Lainey. "Did Thor fight him?"

"Thor fought him," said Bruce. "Really hard. And I turned into the Hulk and fought him, too." He looked at the floor. "And he beat us. Both of us. He beat the Hulk like it was nothing."

Paisley held his hand tighter.

"Then Thanos used the Space Stone to open a hole in the sky," said Bruce. "And he threw Thor through it. Way out into space."

"NO!" said Lainey.

"I don't know where Thor went," said Bruce. "But Heimdall still had a tiny bit of rainbow power left. Just a little. Enough for one person. So he grabbed me and sent me here. To warn everybody." He looked around at them. "He said, 'Go to the family. They'll know what to do.'"

Everyone looked at Lindsey.

Lindsey looked at her map for a long, long time. She looked at the list.

1. Space Stone (blue). THANOS HAS IT.
2. Reality Stone (red). The Collector has it. In a place called Knowhere.
3. Power Stone (purple). THANOS HAS IT.
4. Mind Stone (yellow). In Vision's forehead.
5. Time Stone (green). Doctor Strange keeps it.

"He has two," said Lindsey quietly. "He needs six. Two of the stones he still needs are here. On Earth. One is in New York. One is with Vision."

"So that's where he's coming next," said Sydney.

"He won't come himself," said Bruce. "He has helpers. He calls them his children. They're really scary. They'll come first."

Tim stood up. He wiped the last of the tomato off his face.

"Then we'd better go to New York," he said. "Now."

Doctor Strange's house, the Sanctum, was a tall, old building on a quiet corner in New York City. It had a big round window on the top floor with a funny symbol in it, like the sun. Strange opened the front door before they even knocked.

"I was expecting you," he said. His red cape fluttered over his shoulder. Then it saw Paisley and zoomed off his back to hug her. "And so was my cape, apparently."

Wong was there too, holding a big old book. "Hello, Lainey," he said seriously.

"Hi, Wong," said Lainey. "Want to hear a joke?"

"Absolutely not," said Wong. But the corner of his mouth twitched.

Strange took them up a big curving staircase to a room full of books and strange objects. Then he turned around.

"Bruce," he said. "What happened?"

Bruce told him everything again.

Strange listened. Then he called someone with his magic. A glowing orange circle opened in the air. On the other side was a big garden in the middle of a park. Tony Stark was walking there with a woman with red hair, holding her hand.

"Tony," said Strange. "We need you. Now."

Tony turned around, startled. "Who are you? Why are you in my park? Is that a sorcerer?" Then he saw the family. And Bruce. "Bruce?"

"Hey, Tony," said Bruce.

Tony stepped through the glowing circle without another word. He hugged Bruce so hard Bruce's glasses fell off.

"Two years," said Tony. "Two years, and not one phone call."

"I was kind of busy," said Bruce. "On another planet. In a bathtub."

"I'm going to need you to explain that," said Tony. "Later. Right now, I need to know what's happening."

So Bruce told the story a third time. And as he told it, Tony's face got more and more serious.

When Bruce was done, Tony looked at Strange. Specifically, he looked at the gold necklace shaped like an eye around Strange's neck.

"That's an Infinity Stone," said Tony. "Isn't it?"

"It is," said Strange.

"Then let's get rid of it," said Tony. "If Thanos needs all six, and we destroy one, he can't win."

"No," said Strange. "We can't. I swore to protect this stone. With my life."

Everyone went quiet.

Then Paisley stood up from the couch. The cape was still wrapped around her shoulders like a blanket.

"No," she said.

Strange looked at her. "No?"

"Not with your life," said Paisley. "That's a bad promise. You protect it with your friends. Like Steve says. Nobody gets left behind. Not even you."

Strange opened his mouth to say something smart. Then he closed it. He looked at her for a long moment.

"That's..." he started. "That's actually a much better promise."

"I know," said Paisley.

Wong nodded slowly. "The child is right."

Strange touched the gold eye around his neck. "Okay," he said quietly. "With my friends, then."

Just then, everyone's hair stood up.

The windows rattled. A deep humming sound filled the air, so low you could feel it in your teeth. Books slid off the shelves.

"What is that?" said Tim.

They ran to the big round window and looked out.

A ship was coming down over the city. It was huge and round, like a giant metal donut, spinning slowly as it lowered between the buildings. Dust and wind blew everywhere. People on the street below screamed and ran.

"That," said Bruce in a small voice, "is one of Thanos's ships."

They ran down to the street.

The wind was so strong that Paisley had to hold her crown with both hands. Papers flew through the air. Cars honked. People ran in every direction.

Two figures were walking down the middle of the street toward them.

One was huge. As big as the Hulk. Dark gray and rocky, with a giant hammer instead of one of his hands. He growled when he walked.

The other one was thin. Very thin, and very pale, almost white, like a ghost. He didn't have a nose, just two little holes. And he didn't walk. He floated, a few inches above the ground, with his hands folded in front of him like a fancy butler.

"Hear me," said the pale one, in a soft, whispery, snooty voice. "I am Ebony Maw. And this is my brother. We are the children of Thanos. We have come for the green stone."

"Well, you can't have it," said Tony. His suit came flying out of nowhere and wrapped around him, CLICK CLICK CLICK, like a second skin made of shiny red and gold metal. This was his newest suit. It didn't have a single crack or bolt. It was made of tiny little pieces that moved like water.

"Whoa," whispered Sydney. "It's made of tiny robots. Millions of tiny robots."

"Nanotech," said Tony. "I'll show you later."

Strange stepped up beside Tony. Orange magic sparked around his hands. Wong stepped up beside him, with a magic shield on each arm.

Bruce stepped up too. He closed his eyes. He clenched his fists.

"Come on," he whispered. "Come on, big guy. Come on out. We need you."

Nothing happened.

"Come on!" said Bruce louder. "HULK!"

Nothing. Bruce's face went red, then a little green, then red again. He grabbed his head. "He won't come out. He won't come!"

"Why not?" asked Lainey.

Bruce shook his head. "I don't know! He's hiding! He won't come!"

The big rocky monster with the hammer hand charged at them.

Tony blasted him. Wong shielded everyone. Strange opened a glowing portal right in front of the monster, and the monster ran straight through it and came out the other side in a snowy field very, very far away.

"Where'd he go?" asked Lainey.

"Antarctica," said Strange. "He'll be cold for a while."

Tim grabbed the girls and pulled them behind a parked car. Lindsey grabbed Bruce and pulled him behind it too. Bruce was sitting on the curb, holding his head in his hands.

"I can't do it," he said. "He's scared. The Hulk is scared. Thanos beat him so badly, he doesn't want to come out anymore."

Paisley knelt down in front of Bruce.

"That's okay," she said.

Bruce looked at her.

"It's okay to be scared," said Paisley. "Even big guys get scared. Even the Hulk. Especially the Hulk. Because he's never lost before." She put her hand on his. "He'll come out when he's ready. Like Bucky. Like you did. We'll wait for him."

Bruce looked at her for a long time. "You think so?"

"I know so," said Paisley. "Big feelings are easier with a friend. And he's got a lot of friends."

Bruce took a deep breath. In, like a flower. Out, like birthday candles.

Inside him, very quietly, something rumbled. Not a roar. Just a little grumble. Like a big green giant turning over in bed and saying, not yet.

Bruce smiled a little. "Okay. Okay. I'll wait for him."
