Then Steve grabbed Paisley's other hand. Then Thor grabbed Steve's. Then Bruce grabbed Thor's.

Then everyone.

Natasha. Clint. Sam. Bucky. Rhodey. Pepper. Peter. Strange. Wong. T'Challa. Shuri. Okoye. M'Baku. Quill. Gamora. Nebula. Drax. Mantis. Rocket. Groot. Scott. Hope. Vision. Valkyrie. Korg. Loki. Carol landed and grabbed hands too. Nick Fury. Maria Hill.

And it kept going. Wakandan warriors. Asgardians. Sorcerers. Nova Corps pilots. Ravagers. Hundreds of people. Thousands. A giant chain of hands, all across the battlefield, in a huge spiral around the flagpole, everybody holding on to somebody.

The light from the stones poured out of Tony's glove. It went into Sydney. Then into Lindsey. Then into Tim. Then into the girls. Then into Steve. Then into Thor. Then into everyone. Down the whole long chain. Spreading out. Getting smaller. Getting lighter.

It didn't feel like lightning anymore.

It felt like the biggest, warmest hug in the whole universe.

Tony stood up. He wasn't shaking anymore. He looked down the chain. At Sydney, holding his hand. At the family. At everyone.

Then he looked at Thanos.

Thanos was still trapped in Wanda's red magic. He looked at the chain of hands. He looked at all of them, holding onto each other. He looked like he didn't understand what he was seeing.

"You could have had everything," said Thanos. "All six stones. All the power in the universe. And you gave it away. To all of them."

"Yeah," said Tony. "That's the whole point."

He looked at Sydney. She looked up at him.

"Ready?" said Tony.

"Ready," said Sydney.

Tony raised the glowing glove. He looked down the long chain of people, all the way to the very end.

"And we," said Tony, "are the Avengers."

He snapped his fingers.

SNAP.

A wave of light rolled out from the glove. It was every color at once. Blue and red and purple and green and yellow and orange, all twisted together like the rainbow bridge. It washed over the whole field. It washed over everyone in the chain. It didn't hurt. It felt warm. It felt like sunshine.

And then it reached Thanos's army.

The outriders stopped. The monsters stopped. The flying ships stopped.

A soft golden wind picked them up. All of them. Gently. Like dandelion fluff. It lifted them up off the field, up into the sky. Higher and higher. Thousands of them. Millions.

And Thanos too.

He rose up off the ground. Wanda's red magic let him go. He floated up into the sky with his army, slowly, the golden wind wrapping around him.

On his way up, he looked down. He found Gamora in the chain, holding Quill's hand on one side and Nebula's hand on the other.

Gamora looked back up at him. She didn't look away. She didn't look scared.

Thanos looked at her for a long, long moment.

Then he closed his eyes.

The golden wind swept him up, higher and higher, with all his army. They got smaller. And smaller. Until they were just a tiny glittering speck in the blue sky. And then the speck flew up and away, past the clouds, past the moon, past all the stars, out to the farthest, emptiest corner of space. A place so far away that there was nothing there at all. No planets. No ships. No stones.

A place where they could never, ever come back from.

And then they were gone.

The sky was blue. The field was quiet. The sun was shining.

Nobody let go of anybody's hand.

They just stood there, in the giant chain, breathing.

Then Tony fell down.

The glove on his hand was smoking. Black and burned. The six stones had popped out of it and fallen into the grass, where they lay in a little circle, still glowing softly. Tony's arm was red and tired, like after holding something way, way too heavy for way too long. The light in the middle of his chest, the little round glowing light that had always been there, flickered.

Dim. Bright. Dim.

"TONY!" yelled Pepper.

Everyone let go of each other's hands and ran.

Pepper got there first. She knelt down next to him. She took off his helmet. His eyes were half closed. He was breathing very slowly.

Peter got there next. "Mr. Stark? Mr. Stark, are you okay? Mr. Stark?"

Then Steve. Then Bruce. Then Thor. Then everyone, crowding around.

The light in Tony's chest flickered again. Dimmer.

Paisley pushed her way through the crowd. Nobody stopped her. She got down on her knees in the grass next to Tony's head.

She put her small hand on the flickering light in his chest.

"Tony," she said. "It's Paisley. You did it. You did so good. But you're really, really tired. And your heart is going too fast. So breathe with me. Okay? In, like smelling a flower."

Tony's eyes fluttered. He looked at her.

He breathed in. Very slowly.

"Out, like birthday candles," said Paisley.

He breathed out.

"In," said Paisley.

He breathed in.

"Out."

He breathed out.

Everybody around them breathed too. Pepper. Peter. Steve. Bruce. Thor. Natasha. The whole family. Hundreds of heroes, standing in a field, breathing in and out together. Slowly. Like one giant person.

In. Out. In. Out.

The light in Tony's chest stopped flickering. It glowed. Steady. Soft. Blue.

Shuri knelt down on Tony's other side. She pressed a kimoyo bead against his tired, red arm. It glowed green. "This will help," she said. "It will take a while. But it will help."

Bruce, big and green and gentle, put two fingers on Tony's neck. He listened. He nodded.

"He's okay," said Bruce. "He's just tired. He's really, really tired. He needs to sleep. Probably for a long time." He smiled. "But he's okay."

Tony opened his eyes a little more. He looked up at all the faces around him. Pepper. Peter. Paisley.

"Hey," he whispered. "Did we win?"

"We won," said Paisley.

Tony smiled. A tired, happy, crooked smile.

"Good," he whispered. "Wake me up for spaghetti."

And he closed his eyes and fell fast asleep, right there in the grass, with Pepper holding one hand and Paisley holding the other, breathing slow and steady and peaceful.

Pepper started to cry. Happy crying. Peter started to cry too. So did Steve. So did Thor, who was always the first one to cry and never cared who saw.

Paisley didn't cry. She just sat there, holding Tony's hand, watching the blue light glow steady in his chest.

"Nobody got left behind," she whispered.

Tim knelt down behind her and wrapped his arms around her. Lindsey knelt down next to him. Lainey and Sydney squeezed in too.

"Nobody got left behind," said Tim.

Across the field, people were finding each other. Hugging. Laughing. Crying. Quill picked Gamora up and spun her around. Nebula stood next to them, awkward, until Gamora pulled her into the hug too. Thor hugged Loki, who pretended to hate it and very clearly didn't. Steve and Bucky sat down in the grass side by side, too tired to stand up. Wanda held Vision's hand and put her ear against his chest, listening to his silver heart go thump, thump, thump.

Natasha found Clint. Clint found Natasha. They didn't say anything. They didn't need to.

Lindsey stood up. She pulled out her big map, the one she had been carrying since the very first day at Stark Tower. It was wrinkled and folded and soft from being opened so many times. It had a little burn mark on one corner from Asgard. It had a smudge of spaghetti sauce from a Sunday dinner.

She turned it over to the list of stones. She looked at all six. All the circles. All the crossed-out words. All the hearts she had drawn around the edge.

Then she turned it over to the front.

LOKI = TROUBLE. (Crossed out.) LOKI = FRIEND.
FIND BUCKY. (Check.)
FIND BRUCE. (Check. Check.)
THANOS = WANTS ALL SIX STONES. ...but he doesn't have ANY.
WE WILL FIND EVERYONE.

She clicked her pen.

Under the very last line, she wrote:

WE DID.

Then she put the map away, and she went and sat down in the grass with her family. All five of them. Tim's arm around Lindsey. Paisley in Lindsey's lap. Lainey and Sydney squished in on either side.

In the middle of the field, the six Infinity Stones lay in a little circle in the grass. Glowing softly. Blue. Red. Purple. Green. Yellow. Orange.

Like a tiny rainbow. Resting.

"What do we do with them now?" asked Sydney.

"Tomorrow," said Lindsey. "We'll figure that out tomorrow."

"Today," said Tim, "we just sit here."

So they did.

They sat in the grass, in the sunshine, with all their friends around them, and they didn't do anything at all for a long, long time.

And it was perfect.

## Chapter 22: Goodbyes and New Beginnings

Tony slept for three whole days.

He slept in a big soft bed at his house by the lake, with Pepper sitting next to him, and the windows open so he could hear the water. The light in his chest glowed steady and blue the whole time. Every morning, Shuri came through one of Strange's glowing orange circles to check on him. Every morning, she said the same thing.

"He's okay. He's healing. He's just very, very tired."

The family visited every day. The girls brought him drawings. Sydney drew him a robot with a heart on its chest and a little crown on its head. Lainey drew him running across a battlefield, with a speech bubble that said I KEPT UP!!! Paisley drew everybody holding hands in a long, long line that went all the way around the edge of the paper and met itself at the other side.

Pepper taped all the drawings to the wall next to his bed.

On the morning of the fourth day, Tony opened his eyes.

He looked at the ceiling. He looked at the window. He looked at Pepper, asleep in the chair beside him. He looked at the wall full of drawings.

Then he looked at Paisley, who was sitting at the end of his bed, holding a cup of orange juice, waiting.

"Hey," said Tony, in a scratchy voice.

"Hi," said Paisley. "You slept for three days."

"Huh," said Tony. "New record." He sat up slowly. He looked at his arm. It wasn't red anymore. It was just a little stiff. "Did I miss anything?"

"Spaghetti," said Paisley. "But we saved you some."

Tony laughed. Then he winced, because laughing made his tummy sore. Then he laughed again anyway.

"You know what I really want?" he said.

"What?"

"Shawarma," said Tony.

Paisley grinned. "Daddy said you'd say that."

Pepper woke up. She saw Tony sitting up. She burst into tears and threw her arms around him. Tony hugged her back. Then he looked over her shoulder at Paisley.

"Thanks, kid," he said quietly. "For the breathing. I heard you. The whole time."

"Big feelings are easier with a friend," said Paisley.

"Big everything is easier with a friend," said Tony.

That afternoon, everyone gathered on the big wooden porch of Tony's lake house. It was a beautiful day. The lake was blue and sparkly. Ducks were swimming. A soft breeze was blowing.

And sitting on a little table in the middle of the porch was the vibranium box.

Inside it, sitting on purple cloth, were the six Infinity Stones. Blue. Red. Purple. Green. Yellow. Orange.

Everybody looked at them.

"So," said Steve. "What do we do with them?"

"Destroy them," said Rhodey.

"We can't," said Strange. "Not really. They're older than the universe. Wanda tried to break one. It didn't work forever. And even if it did, breaking them might break other things too."

"Then lock them up," said Sam. "All in one place. The strongest vault in the world."

"That's what we did before," said Gamora quietly. "Remember? The Nova Corps had the strongest vault in the galaxy. My father just walked in and took it."

"And if they're all in one place," said Lindsey, "then someday, somebody else will try to take them all at once."

Everybody looked at her.

"So we split them up," said Lindsey. "Far apart. Each one in a different safe place. With a different friend watching over it. And nobody ever brings them together again. Ever."

Everybody thought about it.

"That's a good plan," said Steve slowly.

"That's a great plan," said Tony.

"Who gets which one?" asked Lainey.

They took turns.

"The Time Stone goes back with me," said Strange, picking up the green stone very carefully. "To the Sanctum. To the Eye of Agamotto. Where it belongs." He looked at Paisley. "And I'll protect it with my friends. Not with my life."

"Good," said Paisley.

"The Mind Stone goes to Wakanda," said T'Challa. "Into a vault so deep under the mountain that only Shuri knows the way down."

"And I'll forget the way," said Shuri. "On purpose. So nobody can make me tell."

"The Power Stone goes back to Xandar," said Quill, picking up the purple stone with a pair of special gloves Rocket had made. "The Nova Corps say they're building a new vault. A better one. With a lock that only opens if a bunch of people hold hands at the same time."

Sydney gasped. "That was my idea!"

"They heard about it," said Quill. "Everybody's heard about it. You're famous on Xandar, kid."

Sydney turned bright pink.

"The Reality Stone," said Gamora, "comes with us. On the Milano. We'll keep it moving. Always flying. Never in one place for long. A stone that never stops moving is very hard to find."

"The Space Stone," said Carol, picking up the blue one, "comes with me. I'm going a long way away. Farther than anyone has ever been. I'll find somewhere quiet, on the other side of the galaxy. And I'll hide it so well that even I won't remember where."

That left one. The orange one. The Soul Stone.

Everybody looked at Tim. Then at Natasha and Clint.

"It goes back to Vormir," said Natasha. "To the top of the mountain. Between the pillars."

"With no Stonekeeper?" asked Bruce.

"It doesn't need a Stonekeeper anymore," said Natasha. "The only way to get it is to understand what love really is. And anybody who understands that..." She smiled a little. "...would never use it to hurt anyone."

"I'll take it back," said Tim. "With Nat and Clint. We'll go together. And we'll all come home together. Again."

"Nobody gets left behind," said Clint.

Lindsey was holding her big map. She unfolded it. She turned it over to the back.

There was the list. All six stones. All the crossed-out words. All the circles and arrows and hearts. Everywhere each stone had ever been. Everything they had ever learned about them.

She looked at it for a long, long time.

"Thor," she said. "Can I borrow some lightning?"

Thor looked at her, surprised. "Of course. But why?"

Lindsey's map folded out into lots of panels, like a big brochure. Over the years she had filled almost every one. The notes on the front. LOKI = FRIEND. FIND BUCKY. WE WILL FIND EVERYONE. WE DID. The very first map of Asgard ever made by someone from Earth. The hidden kingdom of Wakanda, with the waterfall drawn extra big. Those panels she kept.

But the list of stones was on one panel all by itself. Very carefully, she folded it back and forth along its crease, until it came away in her hand. Then she held it out.

"Nobody should have a map of where all six stones are," said Lindsey. "Not even me. Especially after today."

Thor understood. He held out one finger. A tiny spark of lightning jumped from it, no bigger than a firefly. It touched the corner of the paper.

The list caught fire. A small, gentle flame. It burned slowly, curling up, turning black, then gray, then into little floating bits of ash that drifted away over the lake on the breeze.

Lindsey watched it go.

"Mom," said Sydney. "Aren't you sad? You worked so hard on that."

Lindsey thought about it.

"No," she said. "It did its job. We found everything we needed to find."

Then she folded up the rest of the map and put it in her pocket. Right where it always went.

The goodbyes took a whole week.

The Guardians left first. They were going back out into space, to keep the Reality Stone moving and help people on faraway planets.

Rocket shook Sydney's hand very seriously. "If you ever want a job," he said, "fixing spaceships, call me. You're the best assistant I ever had."

"You're the best raccoon I ever met," said Sydney.

"I'm NOT a—" Rocket stopped. He sighed. "Fine. For you, I'll be a raccoon."

Groot, tall and teenaged, bent all the way down to Paisley. "I am Groot," he said softly.

"I love you too," said Paisley, and hugged his trunk.

Gamora hugged Lindsey for a long time. Nebula stood next to them, awkward. Then she walked up to Tim, and without saying anything, she hugged him. Very quickly. Very stiffly. Like she had practiced.

"Thank you for carrying me," said Nebula.

"Anytime," said Tim.

"I know," said Nebula. "That's why I'm saying thank you."

Then Thor came out of the Milano with a bag over his shoulder.

"I am going with them," said Thor. "For a while. To see the stars. To help people. To figure out who I am, now that I'm not a prince of a golden city anymore." He looked out over the lake. "Valkyrie will be the king of New Asgard. She will be a very good one. Much better than me."

"You'd be a good king," said Paisley.

"Perhaps someday," said Thor. "But not yet."

Then he reached into his bag. And he pulled out Mjolnir. The hammer with the shiny gold lines where it had been put back together.

He knelt down in front of Paisley.

"Princess Paisley," he said. "You saved her. Every single piece. So I would like her to stay with you."

Paisley stared at the hammer. "But I can't lift her," she said. "Nobody can lift her. Only you. And Vision. And Steve."

Thor smiled.

"Then she will wait in your garden," he said. "Next to the golden tomatoes. She will keep watch over your family. And when anyone is ready..." He winked. "She will know."

That night, Thor flew to the family's backyard. He set Mjolnir down in the grass, right next to the golden tomato plant, which had grown back almost all the way since Bruce crashed into it. Then he stood up and looked at the hammer for a long time.

"Goodbye, old friend," he said softly.

Then he hugged everyone. One at a time. Very carefully, so nobody fell into any pancake batter.

And he flew up into the stars with the Guardians.

Carol left the next morning, a golden streak going up into the sky, carrying the Space Stone to the other side of the galaxy. Goose rode on her shoulder. Before she went, she gave Paisley a little photo of Goose, with his tentacles out, eating a meatball.

"So you won't forget him," she said.

"I could never forget him," said Paisley.

Tim, Natasha, and Clint went to Vormir one more time. This time there was no storm. The sky was clear and purple, and the two moons were shining. They walked up the mountain together, holding hands on the slippery parts. At the very top, between the two stone pillars, Tim set the orange stone down gently in the snow.

It glowed for a moment. Warm and orange, like a little sunrise.

Then it sank into the snow, slowly, until it was gone.

"Goodbye," said Tim.

Natasha looked up at the bright star shining over the mountain.

"Let's go home," she said.

And they did.

Then it was Steve's turn.

He came to the family's house on a Saturday morning. He was wearing a nice suit. Gray, with a blue tie. His hair was combed very neatly. He was holding a big old-fashioned record player in his arms, and a stack of old records in a paper bag.

"Would you come with me?" he asked. "All of you?"

"Where?" asked Lainey.

Steve smiled. It was a nervous smile. The girls had only seen him nervous once before.

"I owe somebody a dance," he said.

They drove to the big peaceful house with white walls and a garden full of roses. They walked down the hallway, past the nurses in soft shoes. Steve knocked on the door.

"Come in," said a voice.

Peggy was sitting by the window, just like before. Her white hair was soft and curled. She was wearing a pretty blue dress. And when she saw Steve standing in the doorway in his suit, holding a record player, her whole face lit up like a sunrise.

"Steve," she said. "What on earth?"

"I'm late," said Steve. "I know I'm very, very late. But I made you a promise."

Peggy put her hand over her heart.

Steve set the record player down on the little table by the window. He took a record out of the paper bag. It was old. Very old. He put it on the record player, and he lowered the needle very carefully.

Scratch, scratch. Then music. Soft, old, swinging music, with trumpets and a slow, happy beat. The kind of music Steve and Peggy had heard a very long time ago, when they were both young.

Steve walked over to the bed. He held out his hand.

"Peggy," he said. "Would you like to dance?"

Peggy looked at his hand. Her eyes filled up with tears.

"I've been waiting seventy years for you to ask me that," she said.

She took his hand.

Steve helped her stand. Very gently. Very slowly. He held her hand in one of his. He put his other hand on her back. She put her hand on his shoulder.

And they danced.

Not fast. Not fancy. Peggy couldn't move very fast anymore. They just swayed. Back and forth. Slowly. Around and around the little room, in a small, gentle circle, in front of the sunny window. Peggy rested her head on Steve's shoulder. Steve closed his eyes.

The family stood in the doorway and watched.

Lindsey held Tim's hand so tight. Tears were running down both their faces. Lainey stood perfectly still, for once in her whole life. Sydney didn't draw. She just watched. And Paisley held onto Lindsey's dress and smiled the biggest smile she had ever smiled.

The song ended.

Steve and Peggy stopped dancing. But they didn't let go.

"Well," said Peggy softly. "Was it worth the wait?"

"Every single day of it," said Steve.

Peggy laughed. Then she looked over Steve's shoulder at the family in the doorway.

"And you're the ones who taught him how to find his way back," she said. "Aren't you?"

"He mostly did it himself," said Tim.

"No," said Steve, still holding Peggy. "I didn't. They did."

Peggy smiled at them. Then she looked at Paisley.

"Your turn, darling," she said.

"My turn?" said Paisley.

"Steve," said Peggy. "Play the next song. And dance with that little girl in the crown. She's earned it."

So Steve put on the next record. A fast one this time. A real swing song, with drums and horns. And he danced with Paisley. He spun her around. He swung her out and swung her back. Then he danced with Lainey, and Sydney, and Lindsey, while Tim held Peggy's hand and helped her clap along. Then Tim danced with Lindsey, and Peggy laughed until she cried.

It was the best dance any of them had ever been to.

On the way out, Steve stopped in the hallway. He reached into his jacket pocket. He pulled out something small and brown and soft, with one floppy ear.

Biscuit.

He knelt down in front of Paisley. He held the little stuffed puppy out to her.

"You let me borrow him," said Steve. "So I wouldn't be lonely. Until I found Bucky. And until I got my dance." He smiled. "I found Bucky. I got my dance. And I'm not lonely anymore. I haven't been lonely in a really long time. Because of you."

Paisley took Biscuit. She held him against her cheek. He smelled a little like Steve's jacket. And a little like spaghetti.

"Thank you for taking care of him," said Paisley.

"Thank you for taking care of me," said Steve.

The next morning, Steve asked the family to meet him at the lake by Tony's house.

Sam was there. And Bucky. They were sitting on a wooden bench by the water. Steve was standing next to them. He was holding his shield.

The round one. Red and white and blue, with the silver star in the middle.

"Sam," said Steve. "I want you to have this."

Sam stared at the shield. He didn't take it. "Steve. Come on. That's yours."

"It was mine," said Steve. "For a long time. But I'm tired, Sam. Not tired like sleepy. Tired like it's time." He looked at the lake. "Peggy said I could visit every afternoon. And there's spaghetti every Sunday. And I've got a lot of years to catch up on." He smiled. "I think I'd like to just be Steve for a while."

He held out the shield again.

Sam looked at Bucky. Bucky nodded.

Sam looked at the family. Tim nodded. Lindsey nodded. All three girls nodded so hard that Paisley's crown almost fell off.

Sam slowly reached out. He took the shield. He slid it onto his arm.

It fit perfectly.

"How does it feel?" asked Steve.

Sam looked down at it. "Like it's somebody else's," he said quietly.

"It's not," said Steve. "Not anymore."

Sam looked up at him. Then he smiled. "Thanks, Cap."

"You're the Cap now," said Steve.

Lainey ran up to Sam and looked at the shield very closely. Then she looked up at him.

"Sam," she said. "Can I race it?"

Sam laughed. "Yeah, Little Rocket," he said. "Let's see what you've got."

He threw the shield. It flew across the lawn, spinning, curving, bouncing off a tree, BONK, then off a rock, CLANG, then curving back toward Sam.

Lainey raced it. She zigged when it zigged. She zagged when it zagged. And at the very end, as the shield came curving back, Lainey and the shield reached Sam's hand at exactly the same time.

She touched his hand. The shield touched his hand.

A tie.

Everybody cheered so loud the ducks flew off the lake.

Lainey stood there, panting, grinning, with her hand on top of Sam's and the shield.

"Not almost," she said. "Not almost anymore."

On Sunday, everyone came for spaghetti.

The house was too small. So they had it in the backyard. Tim and Steve and Bucky carried out every table they could find and put them end to end, in one long row that went all the way from the back porch, past the oak tree, past the golden tomato plant, past Mjolnir sitting quietly in the grass, all the way to the back fence.

Lindsey made the biggest pot of spaghetti in history. Tim made a thousand meatballs. Aunt May brought a walnut date loaf. Natasha brought a pie that she said she baked herself. Nobody believed her. Then Clint whispered that she really did bake it, and everybody believed her, and it was delicious.

Everybody came.

Steve and Bucky and Sam, with the shield leaning against his chair. Natasha and Clint and Laura and their three kids, including the baby, who chewed on Clint's bow. Rhodey. Bruce, big and green and wearing his glasses, sitting on two chairs at once. Wanda and Vision, holding hands under the table. Vision tried the spaghetti and said it was much better than pickles. Pietro, who finished his whole plate in about two seconds.

Doctor Strange and Wong. The cape sat in its own chair and stole meatballs. Lainey told Wong a joke, and Wong laughed so hard he snorted spaghetti out of his nose, and then everyone laughed even harder.

T'Challa and Shuri and Okoye and Nakia came through a glowing orange circle, with M'Baku, who shouted "HOO! HOO!" when he saw Tim, and Tim jumped and knocked over a pitcher of lemonade, and M'Baku laughed so hard he had to sit down.

Peter Parker and Aunt May and Ned. Scott and Hope and Cassie. Cassie and Paisley sat next to each other with the ant farm between them, feeding Sparkle tiny crumbs of garlic bread.

Loki came. He stood at the edge of the yard for a long time, like he wasn't sure he was invited. Then Paisley ran over and took his hand and pulled him to the table and sat him down right between her and Lindsey.

Lindsey passed him the bread.

"Thank you," said Loki. And he said it like he meant it.

Nick Fury and Maria Hill came. Fury didn't smile. But when Paisley gave him a sparkly napkin folded into the shape of a pirate hat, the corner of his mouth twitched.

And at the very end of the table, with Pepper beside him, sat Tony. He was still a little tired. His arm was still a little stiff. But he was there. And sitting on the table in front of him, next to his spaghetti, was a big paper bag.

"What's that?" asked Lainey.

Tony opened it. Inside was shawarma. Lots and lots of shawarma.

"From the place," said Tony. "The one from the Battle of New York. They fixed it up. It's really nice now. The owner says hi."

Everybody laughed.

Then Tim stood up at the head of the table. He tapped his glass with his fork. Ting, ting, ting.

Everybody got quiet.

Tim looked down the long, long table. At all the faces. At all the friends. At all the people who had been strangers once, and weren't anymore. At his wife. At his three daughters.

He put his hand over the red-and-gold star pin on his shirt, right over his heart.

"A long time ago," said Tim, "my friend Steve taught me what makes a good captain. He said two things. A good captain never gives up. And a good captain never leaves anybody behind."

He looked at Steve. Steve smiled.

"I've been trying really hard to be a good captain," said Tim. "For my family. But I figured something out." He looked around the table again. "All of you are my family. Every single one of you."

He lifted his glass.

"To nobody getting left behind," said Tim.

Everybody lifted their glasses. Even Loki. Even Nebula, who had come back for one night just for this. Even Groot, who wasn't there but had sent a little twig in a flower pot, which Paisley lifted up for him.

"TO NOBODY GETTING LEFT BEHIND!"

They ate. They talked. They laughed until their tummies hurt.

Then, when dinner was almost over, Thor's voice boomed out of Tim's star pin. He was calling from somewhere out in space, with the Guardians.

"FRIENDS!" said Thor. "I am told there is spaghetti!"

"There's spaghetti," said Lindsey, laughing.

"Then I would like to say something," said Thor. Everyone leaned in to listen.

Thor cleared his throat.

"May I please have another?"

The whole table cheered.

That night, after everyone had gone home, the family set up the big green tent under the oak tree. Just like the very first campout. Tim made a little fire. Lindsey brought out the sleeping bags. The girls brought out almost everything they owned.

Paisley brought her crown, her three stuffed puppies, and Biscuit, who was back where he belonged. Lainey brought her silver horseshoe from Sleipnir. Sydney brought her notebook. Her old one was full. This was a brand-new one. On the cover, in big letters, it said: ROBOTS WITH KINDNESS. VOLUME TWO.

They roasted marshmallows. Paisley liked hers golden brown. Lainey liked hers on fire. Sydney liked hers exactly halfway in between, and she wrote down how many seconds it took.

Then Lindsey pointed up at the sky.

"See that bright one?" she said. "The North Star. If you're ever lost at night—"

"—you can find it," said all three girls together. "And it will help you find your way home."

Lindsey laughed. "You remember."

"We'll always remember," said Sydney.

They lay on their backs on the sleeping bags and looked up at the stars. Somewhere out there, Thor was flying with the Guardians. Carol was flying with Goose. The Milano was zooming through space with a red stone that never stopped moving. And on a cold, quiet mountain far away, an orange stone was resting in the snow.

"Mommy?" whispered Paisley.

"Yes, sweetheart?"

"Do you think we'll see more amazing things?"

Lindsey looked up at the stars. She thought about Stark Tower. About the Hulk in the little lab. About Thor in the tomato crater, and Steve in Times Square, and the rainbow bridge that sang. About spaceships and space raccoons and a giant tree who said only three words. About a hidden kingdom and a magic doctor and a kid in a red hoodie with sparkles on it. About holding hands with a hundred thousand people in a field.

"I think," said Lindsey, "we're going to see amazing things for the rest of our lives."

Paisley smiled and snuggled into her sleeping bag.

Then she sat back up.

"Wait," she said. "I want to try something."

She climbed out of the tent. She walked across the dark grass, past the little fire, past the golden tomato plant, to where Mjolnir sat quietly in the moonlight. Its gold lines shone like little rivers.

Tim and Lindsey followed her. So did Lainey and Sydney.

Paisley wrapped both of her small hands around Mjolnir's handle. She closed her eyes. She didn't pull hard. She didn't wish hard. She just held it, the way you hold a friend's hand.

"Hi, Mjolnir," she whispered. "Thanks for watching over us."

And the hammer moved.

Just a tiny bit. Just a teeny, tiny little wiggle.

Paisley's eyes flew open. She let go. She stared at the hammer. She stared at her hands. She stared at her family.

"Did you see that?" she whispered. "Did everybody see that?"

Sydney's mouth was hanging open. She opened her new notebook to the very first page. Her hand was shaking. She wrote one word at the top, in big letters.

WORTHY.

And under it, very carefully:

PAISLEY. (It wiggled!!!)

Tim scooped Paisley up into his arms. Lindsey wrapped her arms around both of them. Lainey and Sydney squeezed in too, all five of them in one big hug, laughing in the moonlight, next to a magic hammer and a tree full of golden tomatoes.

"Okay," said Tim finally. "Everybody back in the tent. It's late."

They walked back across the grass together, holding hands. All five of them. Nobody in front. Nobody behind.

And far above them, in the dark, a rumble of thunder rolled softly across the sky.

Rrrrrrrrumble.

Not a scary one.

A happy one. Like a big, faraway friend, laughing.

THE END
